`BossSpawnRedirectPatch` handles this on the same `SpawnBoss` and `SpawnEnemy` postfixes the portal redirect uses. A Boss-flag spawn that arrives while the portal redirect is not armed is a miniboss; on a level that gets the redirect (`ShouldArm`) it is moved when `BossSpawnRedirect.IsInsideBossBox` holds: within `MinDistanceFromSpawner` (35) in XZ of the portal and between 2 units below (`BoxFloorBelowSpawner`) and 10 units above (`BoxRoofAboveSpawner`) the portal's height. The portal is found with `FindObjectOfType<InteractableBossSpawner>` at spawn time, because a miniboss can spawn before the portal is ever used. The destination is chosen exactly as for a portal boss (ring, kill-floor margin, `FloorPlacement`), and a miniboss outside the band is left where the game put it, so unrelated spawns are untouched. The log line is `BossSpawnRedirect: miniboss <type> moved from (...) to (...)`. Portal bosses and Boss Curse copies keep their armed path unchanged.

Verified in the running game on a Windows build under Proton: with the player standing in the box, the pig above was moved to (175.7, 15.1, 84.9), 59 units from the portal. With the player placed next to that spot the pig reached them and took damage from the player's weapon (health bar 699/780).

Reproducing it takes the timeline: the first miniboss spawns about 3 minutes into the run. `run.py perf timescale --value 6` shortens the wait, `invulnerable` and a teleport that repeats every few seconds keep the player alive and in place (teleport to a floor point, since a drop from y 40 deals fall damage that `invulnerable` does not stop), and a chest or level-up window freezes the run until `dismiss-offers` closes it.

### Boss event log lines

`BossEventPatch` (`boss_event_patch.cs`) subscribes once at startup to the game's static boss events and writes one line per occurrence, formatted by `BossEventLog` (`boss_event_log.cs`), so `log_wait` can match them. The lines are logging only; they run on vanilla maps and mod-loaded levels alike.

| Line | Event (owner) | Observed |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `BossEvents: A_BossSpawned` | `InteractableBossSpawner.A_BossSpawned` | Once per spawner activation, not once per boss: a Boss Curse summon of two bosses logged one line |
| `BossEvents: A_StageBossDied` | `EnemyManager.A_StageBossDied` | Once when the fight ends, also for a curse summon |
| `BossEvents: A_BossDefeated <bool>` | `InteractableBossSpawner.A_BossDefeated` | Once at the kill; `False` on the default relaunch runs, `True` on a `--tier 1` run where waves continue (meaning inferred from those runs); a custom level is now capped to tier 0 (see "Custom tier cap"), so `True` no longer appears there |
| `BossEvents: A_NumBossesDefeated <n>` | `InteractableBossSpawner.A_NumBossesDefeated` | Cumulative counter; 1 after one boss, 3 after a following curse summon of two. Did not fire on the tier 1 run |
| `BossEvents: A_BossDied` | `GraveyardBossRoom.A_BossDied` | Graveyard boss room only; never seen on the Warehouse |

Wait for the kill with `log_wait` on `BossEvents: A_StageBossDied`; match `A_NumBossesDefeated` only on runs that raise it.

Live findings behind the shape:

- Anchoring every copy to one authored `bossSpawnPosition` stacked curse copies (the game's own `BaseInteractable.GetOffset` stagger between the summons measured only 2.7/2.9 units in a live run), and bosses released at the half-pipe floor under the box climbed its walls toward the portal — hence the keep-out from the spawner rather than an authored point.
- A spacing floor of 8 let copies land 14.7 units apart and they still fought on the same spot, so `MinSpacingBetweenCopies` is 20 — but first-fit then landed the next pair 21.2 apart, just over the floor and still reading as stacked, because both copies iterated one shared-seed candidate list and the first survivor past the keep-out always wins. Hence the per-copy seed and maximin `PickSpot`: with 48 candidates the farthest-from-taken pick routinely lands copies dozens of units apart instead of hugging the floor.
- Dropping each boss straight down under its own spawn fails on this level: `LiveEditPatch.GroundCollider()` is the generated tile mesh, which knows nothing about the bundle-placed warehouse mesh (the redirect logged `no ground under the boss` for both spawns), and gravity alone left the bosses in the pipe at the box foot. Hence the all-collider raycast around a ring instead of a drop at the spawn point.

## Custom tier cap

A custom level always runs at the game's Tier 1, `mapTierIndex` 0. The harness `tier` argument and the level file's `tier` are 0-based, so `tier: 0` is the HUD's "Tier 1" and `tier: 1` is "Tier 2". The two tiers behave differently after the stage boss: at `mapTierIndex` 0 `A_BossDefeated` is `False`, no `InteractablePortal` (the `PortalBlue` objects) ever becomes active, the objective list ends at "Survive..." and waves continue; at `mapTierIndex` 1 `A_BossDefeated` is `True` and one `PortalBlue` activates at once and loads a vanilla-generated Tier 2 stage, such as "Deep Forest". No custom level is built for that stage, so a custom level running at tier 1 or above dropped the player through the level when they took the portal.

`CustomTierCap` (`custom_tier_cap.cs`) clamps the tier to 0, and `LevelLoaderPatch.PrepareRun` applies it when it builds the `RunConfig`. Every way a tier reaches a load goes through `PrepareRun`: the level file's `tier`, the harness `load` override, `new-level` and the editor's level settings. A capped load logs `LevelLoader: tier <n> capped to 0` after the usual `LevelLoader: starting ...` line, which still shows the requested tier. The file schema is unchanged, so an existing file with a higher `tier` loads and runs as Tier 1. Vanilla runs never reach `PrepareRun` and keep their own tiers and portals.

To see the Tier 1 behavior, `relaunch` a level, send `editor off` and make the player `invulnerable`, then interact with the boss spawner and `kill_enemies bosses`. A run left in the editor, or with the player not invulnerable, dies before anything can be observed. `interact` only matches active interactables, so it cannot force an inactive portal.

## Kill-floor heartbeat

The kill floor (see "Kill floor" in [runtime-map-structure.md](runtime-map-structure.md)) logs a line for every actor it kills. The heartbeat adds a second line every 5 seconds that says what the watcher can see, which is what to read when something that should have died did not:

```text
KillFloor: watch enemies=26 stageBosses=1 lowestEnemyY=-69.03 lowestBossY=-66.47 bosses=['Enemy1(Clone)' id=18 y=-66.2 lowest=-66.4 inEnemies=True]
```

`enemies` is the size of `EnemyManager.enemies` and `stageBosses` the size of the manager's stage boss list. `lowestEnemyY` and `lowestBossY` are the lowest point any enemy or boss reached since the previous heartbeat, and each boss entry gives its id, pivot `y`, current `lowest` point, and whether it is also in `enemies`. A boss is only identified by id, because it has the same `Enemy1(Clone)` name as every other enemy.

The lowest figure is the collider bottom where one was read and the pivot otherwise. The watcher reads an actor's collider only while its pivot is within `KillFloor.ColliderProbeMargin` (25 units) of the kill floor, because Unity builds bounds lazily and asking a freshly spawned actor for them rebuilds them; the heartbeat's running minimum therefore reports pivots for actors standing well above the floor. The kill decision itself always gets the collider when the actor is in that band, so nothing falls through unseen: by the time an actor reaches the floor its pivot is below it and is always probed. A heartbeat line is also where a mod-side hitch shows up first — `perf report`'s `maxMs` for the `KillFloor` tick is the figure to compare when one appears.

It is off by default, because it adds a line every 5 seconds. Turn it on with `run.py heartbeat on` and off with `run.py heartbeat off`; both log `KillFloor: heartbeat on` or `off` and apply at once, including to a level that is already running. To start with it on, set `MEGABONK_KILL_FLOOR_HEARTBEAT` to `1` or `true` in the environment of the game process, for example in the `.env` that the taskfile reads or in front of the launch command. The variable is read once when the mod loads, and a later command overrides it. Verified in the game that the variable reaches the process under CrossOver.

The kill line itself always carries the actor's id, world position and `lowest` point, with `(still present, attempt N)` appended when an actor stays in the game after a kill.

## Clean screenshots

`hud` with `enabled:false` (`run.py hud off`, MCP `hud(on=false)`) sets `UiManager.Instance.hudGroup.alpha` to 0, which hides the stock HUD (stage clock, XP and HP bars, minimap, quest box, weapon icons, the map title card) and the coordinate label parented under the minimap. This does not reset the independent coordinate preference: `hud on` restores the readout if coordinates were enabled. The HUD command also fades the `CanvasGroup` of each active `HealthUI` with `followPlayer` set, the health and shield bar drawn under the character, which sits outside the HUD group; the game's own `Update` does not fight the alpha. `enabled:true` puts back the alpha that was there when the HUD was first hidden, so a HUD that was already faded stays faded and a second `hud off` does not overwrite the saved value. For a photo-mode frame, use `freeze`, `camera` to the pose, `hud off`, `screenshot`, then restore the HUD, clear the camera and resume.

World-space elements are not part of the HUD group and stay in the frame: enemy name labels, price tags, damage numbers and elite crown icons. The level-up, pause and death screens are separate UI and are not hidden either. An editor on/off round trip leaves the HUD hidden. A level load or new run builds a new HUD, so the HUD is visible again after a `load`; send `hud off` again after each.

## Position readout

Coordinates are an independent, explicit toggle, **off by default**. Press **F7**
in gameplay or the editor, use `run.py coordinates on|off`, or call MCP
`coordinates(on=true|false)`. Toggling coordinates does not enter the editor,
pause/unpause the run, move the camera or change the stock HUD. F7 is ignored
when the game's debug console, the mod's tilde console or an editor text field is focused. The preference
survives editor entry/exit and level loads within the process; restarting the
game resets it to off. `coordinates` returns the requested `enabled` state and
the current `visible` state; rendering may follow on the next frame.

When enabled on a harness-armed custom level with a living player, the original
yellow monospace (`RobotoMono`) readout draws under the minimap. Its original
minimap anchoring, transparent background, font size, dynamic column widths and
text formatting are unchanged. Overlap with the minimap or editor panels is
intentional; this is not a new bottom-right panel. The player's own line is
labeled `me:`:

```text
me:        pos(120, 2, 3) floor -72
enemy: 4   pos(125, 2, -10)
```

Every line shares the same columns (label, id, then `pos(x, y, z)`), but the column widths are computed each refresh from what is actually on screen that refresh (`PositionReadout.Render`), not a fixed guess: the label column is as wide as the longest label shown (at least `me:`), the id column at least 3 digits (wider only if an id needs more, so ids don't jitter the column within a normal run), and each of x/y/z is as wide as that coordinate's widest current value. A value wider than its column still prints in full, only pushing what comes after it on that line; lines share these widths so the `pos` column lines up between them. `pos` itself is the world position rounded to whole units. `floor` on the player's line is the kill-floor height, and `BELOW FLOOR` is appended when an actor's position is below it (the kill floor uses the lower of the position and the collider bottom, so an actor can be killed a moment before its line is flagged). Boss-tier lines also carry the enemy's type name, directly after the position or the `BELOW FLOOR` flag with a single space — it is not column-aligned across lines, since reserving width for it on every line left large dead space when only one boss-tier actor was shown. The player's line is refreshed ten times a second, the actor lines every 0.75 seconds: that sweep walks every live enemy, so at a few hundred of them a 4 Hz pass was a measurable share of a frame for text nobody reads that fast.

Vanilla levels, a disabled harness, loading screens and dead players draw no
readout even if the preference is on. The label is a copy of a native minimap
direction letter, parented to the minimap root and resized to the text's
preferred width. A missing minimap delays rendering until it exists. `state`
exposes `coordinatesEnabled` and `coordinatesVisible` separately and continues
reporting player position/cell when coordinates are off. The `cells` log is also
independent: it tracks player and boss-tier cell changes without requiring the
readout. Conversion, visibility and formatting rules live in `position_readout.cs`.

### Enemy kinds

Actors are classified by the game's own `EEnemyFlag` bits (`Elite=1, Boss=2, StageBoss=4, Challenge=8, SummonerMiniboss=16, FinalBoss=32`), read through `Enemy`'s public `IsElite()`/`IsBoss()`/`IsStageBoss()`/`IsFinalBoss()` methods (the raw field is private) and reduced by `ActorKinds.Of` in `actor_kind.cs`, most severe first: a final boss is never also shown as a stage boss, a stage boss or miniboss never as a plain elite. A stone golem and the other miniboss types (`MinibossGolemSand`, `MinibossScorpion`, `MinibossPig`, …) are spawned through `EnemyManager.SpawnBoss` with the `Boss` bit, the same bit a one-off boss carries, so `IsBoss()` alone is enough to classify them as `miniboss`; `EEnemyFlag.SummonerMiniboss` has no public getter and was not observed set on a spawned miniboss.

| Kind | Label | Always listed | Shows the enemy type |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Final boss | `final boss:` | yes | yes |
| Stage boss | `boss:` | yes | yes |
| Miniboss | `miniboss:` | yes | yes |
| Elite | `elite:` | up to 3, nearest/below-floor first | no |
| Ordinary enemy | `enemy:` | up to 3, nearest/below-floor first | no |

Final bosses, stage bosses and minibosses are always listed (read from `EnemyManager.stageBosses` first, then any other boss-tier actor found walking `EnemyManager.enemies`, deduplicated by id); boss-tier lines append the enemy's `EnemyData.enemyName` (e.g. `MinibossGolem`) after the position. Elites and ordinary enemies each go through their own `NearbyEnemySelector` (`nearby_enemy_selector.cs`): below the kill floor first, then the rest ordered by horizontal distance (x and z) to the player, ties by lower id, capped at 3 each. With hundreds of enemies alive the readout stays bounded (boss tier plus at most 6 more lines).

The selectors reuse fixed buffers, so a refresh allocates only the few strings it shows. With about 400 enemies a refresh took 0.6 to 1.4 ms and allocated 3 to 5 KB; nothing runs on every frame, and the sweep's cost is now the 0.75 s cadence rather than 0.25 s — at 240 enemies the whole `PositionLabel` tick measured 0.022 ms/frame at the old cadence and 0.012 ms/frame at the new one, with the per-refresh figure unchanged.

Verified in the running game: the player's `pos` tracked the player through a level with tiles removed, and a miniboss spawned by a scratch probe calling the game's own `SummonerController.EventMiniboss` showed `miniboss … MinibossGolem` with `IsBoss()` true. A boss spawned by the boss portal, a final boss, and elites were not exercised.

### Cell-change log

The grid cell, height level and `outside` text are not drawn on screen; a harness-gated toggle logs them instead, mirroring the kill-floor heartbeat. Off by default, turned on with `run.py cells on` and off with `run.py cells off` (both log `PositionLabel: cell log on` or `off` and apply at once), or started on with `MEGABONK_CELL_LOG` set to `1` or `true` in the game process's environment (read once at mod load; a later command overrides it).

When on, it logs one line per actor whenever that actor's cell or height level changes (not every tick): the player under the name `me`, and every boss-tier actor under its label, id and type name:

```text
PositionLabel: me cell (10,7) h0 pos (120, 2, 3)
PositionLabel: miniboss 41 MinibossGolem cell (11,7) h1 pos (157, 16, 3)
```

`outside` follows the cell when the position is outside the tile grid, as before. The grid size and spacing are read from the live tile grid and `MapParameters` when the level is armed, so it is not tied to the 15x15 Forest map. Change tracking is pure logic in `cell_change_tracker.cs`.

Caveats:

- The kill floor kills the player within a quarter of a second of crossing the floor, so `BELOW FLOOR` is on screen only for those few frames and was not captured in the running game.
- Cell and height level come from an actor's transform, not from the tile under it, so on a slope or while falling they are an approximation.

## Frame-time profiling

The harness carries a frame profiler, for the question "what costs what when the screen is full of enemies". Everything in it is gated on the harness being enabled — every timer, every patch, every verb — so a normal session makes no timing calls at all. Measured results and the follow-ups they opened are in [performance.md](performance.md).

| Verb | What it does | Result |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `start` | Clears the frame buffer and every tick and probe total, so the next report describes only what follows | `window` id, `cleared` |
| `report` | Summarizes what has been sampled since `start`, leaving it running | `frames`, `pausedFrames`, `meanMs`, `p50Ms`, `p90Ms`, `p99Ms`, `maxMs`, `minMs`, `modMsPerFrame`, `enemiesMin`/`enemiesMax`, `playerHp`, `timeScale`, `paused`, `vSyncCount`, `targetFrameRate`, `renderCull` (`{armed, renderersOff}`), `physicsFreeze` (`{armed, rigidbodiesKinematic, rigidbodiesFound, enemies, suppresses, fidelity}`), `animationCull` (`{armed, behavioursOff, componentsFound, byType, enemies, suppresses, fidelity}`), `enemyPositionSum` (the sum of live enemy positions — two samples say whether a culled swarm is still moving), and a `ticks` and `probes` array of `{name, ms, calls, maxMs, enabled}` |
| `status` | The same view without ending the window, logged every 2 seconds as `Perf: state:` | as `report` |
| `reset` | Releases a held time scale, re-enables every tick and probe, restores the render cull, the physics freeze and the animation cull, and revokes the player grant `health` applied | the released values, and one `*Restored` flag per control: true means it was armed and was restored, false means it had nothing to restore |
| `spawn --count N` | Places N enemies in rings around the player through the game's own spawn path, up to 300, stopping after 10 consecutive null spawns | `requested`, `spawned`, `enemies`, `poolMax` |
| `timescale [--value X]` | Holds `MyTime.timeScale` at 0.05–20 every frame, or releases it when no value is given | `timeScale`, `heldTimeScale` |
| `toggle --target NAME --enable\|--disable` | Switches one mod tick (`KillFloor`), one probe (`EnemyManager.Update`) or `all` | the target's new state |
| `vsync --disable` | Sets `QualitySettings.vSyncCount` to 0 and `targetFrameRate` to 0, which is what uncaps the frame rate enough to measure | `vSyncCount`, `targetFrameRate` |
| `render --enable\|--disable` | The render-only control: `--disable` sets `Renderer.enabled = false` on every renderer under every live enemy while AI, physics and animation keep running; `--enable` restores exactly what it hid (renderers the game disabled itself are left alone). It rescans the swarm when the population moves or every 0.5 s, and that rescan is the `RenderCull` entry in `ticks` | `enabled`, `armed`, `enemies`, `renderersOff` |
| `physics --enable\|--disable` | The solver control, same shape and rescan as `render`: `--disable` sets `Rigidbody.isKinematic = true` on every Rigidbody under every live enemy while AI, renderers and animation keep running, which takes the swarm out of Unity's solver; `--enable` writes back the value each body was found with, and bodies the game already had kinematic are never claimed. The rescan is the `PhysicsFreeze` entry in `ticks` | `enabled`, `armed`, `enemies`, `rigidbodiesKinematic`, `rigidbodiesFound` |
| `animation --enable\|--disable` | The animation control: `--disable` sets `Behaviour.enabled = false` on every `AnimatedMesh` and `Animator` under every live enemy with renderers, colliders and rigidbodies still running; `--enable` restores each component's own prior `enabled`. The rescan is the `AnimationCull` entry in `ticks` | `enabled`, `armed`, `enemies`, `behavioursOff`, `componentsFound`, `byType` |
| `health [--hp N]` | Keeps the player alive through a window: the invulnerability grant (20 Mirror stacks, cooldown zeroed, plus 2 Feathers) and `PlayerHealth.hp`/`maxHp` written to 1,000,000/100,000,000, or `--hp` | `playerHp`, `playerGrantRestored` |
| `census` | Counts loaded instances of `Enemy`, `EnemyHpBar`, `VisualizeTargeting` and `AnimatedMesh`: `scene` = on active game objects, `live` = also enabled and active in hierarchy, `any` = everything `Resources` sees, prefabs included. The way to tell a dead method from a ghost component | `census` array of `{name, scene, live, any}` |

The three live-enemy controls (`render`, `physics`, `animation`) are independent: each reports what its last sweep examined (`renderersOff`, `rigidbodiesKinematic`/`rigidbodiesFound`, `behavioursOff`/`componentsFound`) and `status` says which are `armed`, which is what a sweep reads to prove a window held exactly one of them. `enabled: false` arms a control and `enabled: true` restores it, in all three cases, and while armed the 0.5 s rescan keeps re-applying it — so the counters in a verb's own response are the state at that instant, not the state through the window: a body the game re-dynamics mid-window is frozen again within half a second, and arming at 240 enemies logged `froze 116 rigidbodies` on the first sweep and `restored 240` at the end. Measure one control per window: two armed at once is the A/B of neither. Each control's `suppresses` and `fidelity` nodes state in the harness's own words what else leaves the frame with it, because a control that suppresses more than the work it targets makes the figure a bound, not a share.

`run.py perf report` and the MCP `perf` tool return the JSON; `perf health` is the invulnerability setup of the "Player invulnerability" section plus a hit-point floor, applied in one command so it can be sent before a spawn.

**Frames.** One sample per frame from `Time.unscaledDeltaTime` in the mod's `OnUpdate`, while `MyTime.paused` is false. While it is true the frame is counted and not sampled (`Perf: pause: on/off` is logged on each transition). Paused frames are UI frames: Unity keeps rendering a frozen run at a rate that says nothing about gameplay, and those frames are long, so a window that contains any is a different measurement than the one being asked for. `pausedFrames` is how the caller finds out.

**Ticks and probes.** Each mod tick is wrapped in `PerfMonitor.Timed`, and eight Harmony prefix/postfix pairs instrument the game's own enemy path: `EnemyManager.Update`, `EnemyManager.FixedUpdate`, `Enemy.MyFixedUpdate`, `EnemyMovementRb.MyFixedUpdate`, `EnemyDissolve.Update`, `AnimatedMesh.Update`, `MinimapUi.Update` and `AlwaysManager.Update`. `Enemy.MyUpdate`, `EnemyHpBar.Update` and `VisualizeTargeting.Update` were probed and dropped: never called, or never instantiated ([performance.md](performance.md#frame-time-against-enemy-count)). Both use `Stopwatch.GetTimestamp()`, never `Time.*`, because a tick can run inside a frame whose delta is the thing being measured. `calls` matters as much as `ms`: 240 enemies produce 83.9 `Enemy.MyFixedUpdate` calls per frame, 0.35 of a call per enemy per frame, which is the 50 Hz fixed update against the ~143 fps a 7.02 ms mean implies. `maxMs` is the one to read when the complaint is a hitch rather than a low average — an average of 0.013 ms/frame can hide a 112 ms single call.

Every `report` and `status` also carries a `probeAttach` array of `{name, patched}` from Harmony's patch info, so a zero-call probe is classified in one step: `patched: false` is an attach failure, `patched: true` with zero calls means the method was not invoked, and `perf census` then separates "never invoked" from "never instantiated" by counting live instances of the components the probes attach to.

`toggle all off` is the control that separates the three kinds of cost: mod work (the `ticks` array), stock work the probes can see (the `probes` array), and everything else, which is engine work — rendering, the physics broadphase and solver, GC. Probes measure the game, they are not part of playing it, and a report should be read as a diagnostic build's numbers.

### Taking a measurement

The sequence that produces a number worth comparing, and the reason each step is there:

1. Relaunch rather than reload. A rebuilt DLL loads once, at startup, and a `load` into a running game restarts the run while leaving whatever the previous run left behind.
2. Load with a fixed `--seed` (and `--tier`/`--map` if they matter), then `spawns off` so the wave spawner cannot move the population during a window.
3. `invulnerable`, `editor off`, `resume`. The editor freezes the run, which is the wrong thing to measure.
4. `perf vsync off`. At a locked 60 fps every window reads 16.7 ms and none of the differences are real.
5. Per window: gate on the run being live (`state`'s `levelLoaded`, `killFloorArmed`, and not `editor` or `frozen`; `perf status`' `paused` false), teleport the player to a fixed point, `perf health`, spawn the top-up to the target count, `perf start`, wait, `perf report`. Spawn and teleport stay outside the window.
6. Keep the report only if `pausedFrames` is 0, `vSyncCount` is 0, the frame count is plausible for the window length, and `enemiesMin..enemiesMax` brackets the target. Otherwise take it again.

Dismiss reward windows during the window, at about 1 Hz: kill-floor deaths award XP, XP opens the level-up screen, and the level-up screen pauses the run. A 160-enemy window collected 784 paused frames this way before it was thrown out.

`scripts/perf_sweep.py` drives all of it — a fresh launch, the load with retries until `KillFloor: armed`, the count sequence, and the two controls at the top count (every tick and probe off, then back on) — writing one JSON per window with the report plus the window's target, attempt count, rewards skipped, and a `_suspect` note when the population thinned below 80% of target:

```sh
uv run scripts/perf_sweep.py --out /tmp/perf                      # the default 0, 40, 80, 120, 160, 200, 240
uv run scripts/perf_sweep.py --count 0 --count 240 --seconds 30 --seed 20261102
uv run scripts/perf_sweep.py --no-relaunch --out /tmp/perf_probe  # measure the run already loaded
uv run scripts/perf_sweep.py --count 0 --count 240 --density strung  # see density A/B section below
uv run scripts/perf_sweep.py --count 240 --captures 2 --capture-dir /tmp/RenderDoc  # RenderDoc F12 frames per window
uv run scripts/perf_sweep.py --count 0 --count 240 --render-arms 3 --seconds 20  # see render-only A/B section below
uv run scripts/perf_sweep.py --count 0 --count 240 --physics-arms 3 --seconds 20  # see engine-side A/B section below
uv run scripts/perf_sweep.py --count 0 --count 240 --anim-arms 3 --seconds 20     # the animation-stop A/B
```

On a direct-Proton Linux host (no `task game:run` available), pass `--launch-cmd` to relaunch instead:

```sh
uv run scripts/perf_sweep.py --count 0 --count 240 --launch-cmd "sh $HOME/.local/share/scripts/launch-megabonk.sh"
```

The command is split with `shlex` and run without a login shell, so a leading `~` is never expanded — give absolute paths. The launcher must return promptly: detach the game (`setsid ... &`) rather than exec it, and export `DISPLAY`/`XAUTHORITY` yourself — over SSH they are unset, and the Proton launcher inherits them; take `XAUTHORITY` from the running Xwayland's `-auth` argument.

**Density A/B** (`--density gathered|strung`). `gathered` (default) teleports the player to a fixed point so the swarm converges there. `strung` does the same spawn, then teleports the player `--strung-offset` world units along +X so the swarm is left behind — isolating contact-density cost at equal enemy counts. The A/B result and what it showed are in [performance.md](performance.md#attributing-the-per-enemy-cost).

**Render-only A/B** (`--render-arms N`). Replaces the count ladder with `rendon`/`rendoff` window pairs at each count, N repeats per count, arms strictly alternating and the leading arm flipped every repeat: cost walks upward through a session, so an arm that is always measured second would absorb that drift on its own. The `rendoff` arm sends `perf render --disable`, which hides the enemies' renderers and nothing else: their AI, Rigidbody and animation components keep running, and the window's `enemyPositionSum` delta is what proves it. Because the arms are compared inside one session, the session-to-session offset does not enter the delta. The window JSON records `_renderArm`, `_renderersOff`, `_cullMsPerFrame` (the harness's own cost, from the `RenderCull` tick), `_positionSumBefore`/`_positionSumAfter` and `_playerAtOrigin`/`_origin` (the player read after the teleport that stands the swarm, against the point it was meant to reach, which is what "the player teleported to the fixed point" is measured against); a window is flagged `_suspect`, and kept, when the cull disabled nothing at a population, disabled something at zero, was still hiding renderers in the `rendon` arm, or found the player more than 8 units off that point (a few units is the swarm shoving the player during the settle, which is not a failed teleport). `--launch-cmd` with `ENABLE_VULKAN_RENDERDOC_CAPTURE=1` in the environment turns the same ladder into a capture run for [headless replay](#renderdoc-on-a-direct-proton-linux-host).

**Engine-side A/B** (`--physics-arms N`, `--anim-arms N`). The same ladder pointed at the two engine controls instead of rendering: `physbase`/`physkin` arm `perf physics --disable` (the live enemies' rigidbodies kinematic, so the swarm leaves the solver with AI, renderers and animation running) and `animbase`/`animoff` arm `perf animation --disable` (their `AnimatedMesh`/`Animator` components stopped with renderers, colliders and bodies running). The three flags share one plan builder and are mutually exclusive: two armed controls in one window is the A/B of neither, so the sweep disarms the other two before it arms one and restores all three in its `finally`. Every window carries `_control`, `_controlArm`, `_controlState` (the harness's own counters at arm time), `_controlMsPerFrame` (that control's tick, which the armed arm pays and the split has to subtract) and `_probesMsPerFrame` for the four stock probes that say whether the arm touched work it was not meant to: `Enemy.MyFixedUpdate`, `EnemyMovementRb.MyFixedUpdate`, `AnimatedMesh.Update`, `EnemyManager.FixedUpdate`. A window is flagged `_suspect` when an armed control changed nothing at a population, changed something at zero, left a baseline arm holding state, or when `status` reports a second control armed as well. Measured results and what each arm's own fidelity says are in [performance.md](performance.md#engine-side-ab-the-solver-and-animation).

A `load` refused with "no MapData found" on a direct-Proton host is the first-run Photosensitivity Warning, not a bad level: the sweep drives `KEY_ENTER` through the menu service and retries.

**RenderDoc captures** (`--captures N`). Requires the game to have been launched with `ENABLE_VULKAN_RENDERDOC_CAPTURE=1` (the Vulkan implicit layer registered in `~/.local/share/vulkan/implicit_layer.d`). The sweep triggers F12 through `run.py menu drive` N times per window (evenly spaced); new `.rdc` files in `--capture-dir` (default `/tmp/RenderDoc`) are paired with the window JSON and moved under `<out>/captures/`. F12 freezes rendering for the capture frame, so **capture runs perturb frame times** and should be separate from the timing sweep. See "RenderDoc on a direct-Proton Linux host" below.

**GC sampler.** Every `perf report` includes `gcPauseMsPerFrame` and `gcCollections`: time the mod's .NET CLR spent suspended for GC during the window, divided by frames. This measures the mod's own managed GC (which allocates nothing in steady state and reads 0.000 everywhere in practice). The game's il2cpp Boehm GC has no managed events and is invisible here — it appears only as frame-time outliers in `p99Ms`/`maxMs`.

### RenderDoc on a direct-Proton Linux host

The RenderDoc Vulkan implicit layer captures frames from the DXVK layer underneath Proton, making GPU-level frame analysis available without a development build.

**Install (user-level, no root).** Download the official tarball from renderdoc.org, extract to `~/.local/share/renderdoc`, and symlink the binaries:

```sh
curl -sL -o /tmp/renderdoc.tar.gz https://renderdoc.org/stable/1.47/renderdoc_1.47.tar.gz
tar xzf /tmp/renderdoc.tar.gz -C ~/.local/share
mv ~/.local/share/renderdoc_1.47 ~/.local/share/renderdoc
ln -sf ~/.local/share/renderdoc/bin/renderdoccmd ~/.local/bin/renderdoccmd
```

**Register the layer.** Copy the layer JSON and fix its `library_path` to point at the extracted `.so` (the tarball ships `/io/dist/lib/…` which only resolves inside the build container):

```sh
mkdir -p ~/.local/share/vulkan/implicit_layer.d
cp ~/.local/share/renderdoc/etc/vulkan/implicit_layer.d/renderdoc_capture.json \
   ~/.local/share/vulkan/implicit_layer.d/
python3 -c "
import json, pathlib
p = pathlib.Path.home()/'.local/share/vulkan/implicit_layer.d/renderdoc_capture.json'
d = json.loads(p.read_text())
d['layer']['library_path'] = str(pathlib.Path.home()/'.local/share/renderdoc/lib/librenderdoc.so')
p.write_text(json.dumps(d, indent=2))
"
```

**Add the capture env var to the launch.** On a direct-Proton systemd-run launch, add `--setenv=ENABLE_VULKAN_RENDERDOC_CAPTURE=1`. The layer is confirmed loaded when `librenderdoc.so` appears in the game's `/proc/<pid>/maps`.

**Trigger a capture.** F12 is RenderDoc's default capture hotkey. Via `run.py menu drive` (KEY_F12 is in the uinput whitelist): `uv run scripts/run.py menu drive '[["key","KEY_F12"]]'`. Captures land in `/tmp/RenderDoc/` as `wine64-preloader_<date>_frame<N>.rdc`.

**Headless replay, no Qt host.** `scripts/renderdoc_replay.py` opens a capture and reads per-event GPU durations, draw-call counts and the per-enemy submission shapes straight from a plain interpreter — the Qt GUI is not needed, and `renderdoccmd convert -f capture.rdc -o frame.xml` is a dead end because that format carries no GPU timestamps. Three things about v1.47 cost time to find and are the reason an earlier round concluded this was impossible; two more say what the resulting numbers are worth:

- **The pymodule is ABI-pinned to the python it was built against.** Importing `renderdoc.so` under the wrong minor version dies in a way that looks exactly like the "needs the Qt host" failure. `ldd renderdoc.so` names the `libpython3.x` it wants; on the player laptop the module in `~/git/renderdoc-build/renderdoc/build3/lib` is built against `python3.14` while the system `python3` is 3.13, so the tool is run as `python3.14`. Point it at a different build with `MEGABONK_RENDERDOC_LIB`.
- **`InitialiseReplay`/`ShutdownReplay` bracket every call.** No API call is valid before the first or after the second, and they are what the Qt host normally calls for you — that, not a missing GUI, is why the module is documented as unsuitable for standalone use. Omitting `ShutdownReplay()` dumps core during interpreter teardown, which also reads as an import-time crash.
- **Only `renderdoc` exists standalone.** The `qrenderdoc` module (UI windows and docks) is not available outside the GUI, so anything the tool needs must come from the replay controller: `GetRootActions`, `EnumerateCounters`, `FetchCounters([GPUCounter.EventGPUDuration])`, `GetVulkanPipelineState`.

- **`EventGPUDuration` is a relative clock, not a frame clock.** Its sum for a frame the harness measured at 4.88 ms mean came back as 22.8 ms, and the worst captures reported 101 ms on frames carrying a third of the vertices of a 44 ms frame, so the milliseconds are replay artifacts (serialized submission, per-query flush, idle clocks) and only ratios within one session mean anything. The Intel ANV driver offers no whole-frame `GPUCounter.Duration` to cross-check against — `EnumerateCounters` starts at 1 — and this pymodule has no `REVENT`, so a begin/end range cannot be requested either. Use `shapeGpu` and `drawRecords` to ask what share of the frame's work a set of draws accounts for, and get milliseconds from the harness's own frame time.
- **Structured and replay modes agree on draw calls, not on instances.** Both count the same draws; the serialized log's `instanceCount` under-reads a long tail of draws whose instance count the replay controller's action model reports higher (1,635 against 3,209 instances on one capture). Quote draw calls and submitted vertices, which both modes agree on.

```sh
python3.14 scripts/renderdoc_replay.py --baseline "$G/count_0_0.rdc" --pipelines \
  --json /tmp/replay.json "$G"/count_0_0.rdc "$G"/count_240_0.rdc
```

`--baseline` turns the run into a per-enemy split (GPU µs on the replay clock, draw calls, vertices and instances per extra enemy) against an empty-level capture. Replay mode also lists the frame's largest color targets, so a duration can be read against the resolution it was measured at. `--structured` parses only the capture's serialized API log — draw calls, instances and vertices submitted per shape — with no replay device, so it costs no GPU time and is safe to run while something else owns the box. Replaying is a GPU workload: the tool refuses to open a replay device while `Megabonk.exe` is running, which would skew both the game and the capture, and takes `--force` to override.

Captures are written under `/tmp` and do not survive a reboot; regenerate them with the sweep command above before replaying.

### What ruined a measurement before it was understood

- **A paused window looks like a slow window.** `state`'s `frozen` is the harness freeze, not `MyTime.paused`, so a run held by the death screen reports `frozen: false`; `perf status`'s `paused` and `pausedFrames` are the only reliable gate. `dismiss-offers` cannot close `DeathScreen`.
- **`perf health` alone does not keep the run alive.** A player knocked off the map is killed by the kill floor through `PlayerHealth.KillPlayer`, which bypasses both Mirror and `PlayerHealth.hp`, and the run ends mid-window on the death screen. Teleporting the player back before each window is what keeps a sweep going.
- **Enemy count is not the variable, the swarm's shape is.** Teleporting the player to a fixed point gathers the whole population around them; leaving the player where the run drifted leaves the same 240 enemies strung out across the map. Those two states measured 9.63 ms and 7.02 ms, because physics contacts scale with local density. Fix the position and the counts become comparable — and note that convergence grows through a session, so later windows in a sweep read high.
- **Total frame time is noisy; per-tick totals are not.** Repeating the same 240-enemy window in one session gave means of 6.5, 7.0, 7.8 and 9.6 ms, so anything smaller than about 1.5 ms in a total is not evidence. `ticks` totals and `maxMs` are steady enough to compare builds.
- **Do not screenshot a measured run.** Each capture is a synchronous whole-backbuffer readback on the game's main thread (see "Custom-level startup cover"); a window that took one is not a frame-time measurement.
- **The harness is inside the numbers.** It polls twice a second, so a 20-second window contains about 40 `DevHarnessPoll` ticks — 0.027 ms/frame of diagnostic cost at 240 enemies, in the `ticks` array where it can be subtracted.

Verified on the Windows build under Proton on the gamescope host, over populations of 0 to 240 enemies with the kill floor armed and `perf vsync off`; the resulting tables are in [performance.md](performance.md).

## Console command language

The in-game console (the tilde drop-down) turns a typed line into a harness command. The command language itself — tokenizer, verb registry, Tab completion, history and scrollback — is Unity-free pure logic in `console_command_tokenizer.cs`, `console_command_registry.cs`, `console_history.cs` and `console_scrollback.cs`, linked into `tests/MegabonkLevels.Tests` and unit tested there exactly like the rest of the harness logic. It does not touch the UI; the overlay renders these results.

- **Verbs** are `snake_case` with dot notation for namespaces (`player.god`, `perf.spawn`). Matching is case-insensitive. The full verb table, aliases and gating stars are in the task description; each verb binds its arguments to the same JSON object `run.py` sends and then goes through `HarnessCommand.TryParse`, so argument validation and error wording match the harness rather than duplicating it.
- **Tokenizing**: `ConsoleCommandTokenizer.Tokenize` splits a line into a verb and its arguments. A `"quoted run"` holds spaces inside one argument (`level.load "Bramblecrown Hill"`); consecutive whitespace collapses to one separator; a control character or an odd number of quotes is rejected with the reply to print, and a blank line is a no-op. Quoting is standard shell behavior — an even number of quotes opens and closes a run, and the text is kept exactly as typed.
- **Gating**: every verb carries a cheat/debug flag. When `MEGABONK_DEV` is not true, a flagged verb replies `requires MEGABONK_DEV` and never reaches the dispatcher; unflagged verbs (`state`, `level.list`, `ui.hud`, the music verbs) always bind. Because gating can hide the right verb, an unknown-verb refusal still suggests the closest matches from the whole table (a gated `enemies.kill` is offered for `killal` even when the dev flag is off).
- **Feedback**: bad arity or bad values reply with the verb's own usage line; the unknown-verb message names the nearest verbs.
- **Completion**: `help [filter]` lists the verbs the caller may use, one aligned line each with aliases and a short description; gated verbs appear only with the dev flag. `ConsoleCommandRegistry.Complete(line, levels)` completes the last word of the line (quote-aware, so a word inside an unterminated quote still counts) and returns the matches as a cycle, so Tab steps through them and wraps. The first word completes against verb names and aliases (case-insensitive prefix, ordinal order). A later word completes against the candidates of the verb in the first word, supplied per verb with `ConsoleVerb.WithCandidates(argumentIndex, levels)`; a verb without a provider, an unknown verb, or no match leaves the line unchanged. `level.load` and its alias offer the level titles (file names without `.mblevel.json`) for its first argument only; a title with whitespace is inserted in double quotes, which `level.load` binds back to the same file. `ConsoleCompletion.Line` is the whole replacement line, so the overlay never re-quotes. `ConsoleCompleter` holds the Tab state: a Tab on the line the previous Tab wrote steps to the next match (`music.p` gives `music.pause`, then `music.play`, then `music.prev`, then wraps), and any edited line completes afresh.
- **History** (`ConsoleHistory`, 200 entries) holds the lines Up/Down recall, and `history` lists them numbered. `Add` records a line and skips a consecutive duplicate, and the last entry is re-read without consuming it so the first `Previous` yields the most recent command. Empty lines are never recorded. **Scrollback** (`ConsoleScrollback`) is a bounded ring of output lines with a fixed-height viewport; `PageUp`/`PageDown` scroll a viewport at a time and clamp at the ends, `ScrollToBottom` follows new output, and a scroll-up holds its lines as the buffer trims until the view is pushed past the top.
- **Persistence** (`ConsoleLogStore`) keeps both across game sessions in `<game>/UserData/console_log.jsonl`, one JSON object per line: `{"k":"in","v":"<typed line>"}` for history, `{"k":"out","v":"<printed line>"}` for scrollback and `{"k":"clear"}` for the `clear` verb, which drops the scrollback written before it but keeps history. `run.py clear-console-log` and the MCP `clear_console_log` tool delete the file (see [Clearing the console log](#clearing-the-console-log)). The overlay appends as lines are submitted and, before the first submission or panel build in a game session, compacts the file to the newest 200 history and 400 scrollback entries and restores them once. Blank, corrupt or unknown lines are skipped, and an unreadable or unwritable file is ignored rather than raised, so persistence can never break the console. Delete the file to reset both.
- **Console-only verbs** are `help` (alias `?`), `clear` (alias `cls`) and `history`; none reaches the harness, and none needs `MEGABONK_DEV`.
- **`level.load`** resolves a bare target as a title against the saved level list — case, spaces and punctuation are ignored — and reports every candidate when the title is ambiguous; a target that already equals a saved file name (or a path) is left as typed. Options are `map/stage/tier/seed/noseed`. While the editor is open, a bare level name with no options binds to the console-only `EditorLoad` command, which runs the editor Load button's logic (`EditorTileTools.LoadLevel`): authored-start targets and transitions into or out of from-scratch levels start a fresh run; otherwise edits load in place only when map, stage, tier and seed match, with incompatible generated baselines refused. Options, a path, or the editor being closed bind to the harness `load` command, which always starts a fresh run; `relaunch`, `run.py load` and the MCP `load` tool are unchanged. `EditorLoad` has no JSON form.

Bare titles such as `level.load thps` read the installed saved file in `<game>/UserData/Levels/`, not the repository's `levels/` directory. Rebuilding the plugin does not refresh that installed JSON. Before replacing an old installed level with the repository version, preserve a byte-for-byte backup outside `UserData/Levels/` so it remains recoverable without becoming another selectable level. A schema migration reads old content compatibly; it does not replace an old authored layout with the current shipped one.

The music verbs (`music.play/pause/next/prev/track/list/now`) bind to the `music` harness command; see [Music transport](#music-transport).

- **`debug.screenshot <path>`** (gated) saves the rendered frame as a PNG. A relative path resolves under `MEGABONK_EVIDENCE_DIR`, which the launcher (`task game:run`, `run.py relaunch`) sets to the repo's gitignored `.tools/evidence` as the game sees it, the same folder the MCP `screenshot` tool defaults to; missing folders are created and an existing file is refused, so the console prints an error instead of overwriting. An absolute path is used as given, which is how `run.py` and the MCP tool send theirs (`Z:\...` under Wine on macOS). A game started outside the launcher has no `MEGABONK_EVIDENCE_DIR`, and a relative path then resolves against the game's working directory, the install folder. Quote a path with spaces.

## Console overlay

`ConsoleOverlay` (`console_overlay.cs`) is the visible console: a Fallout-style drop-down that slides **up from the bottom** of the screen. It is driven from the mod's `OnUpdate` through `PerfMonitor.Timed(PerfTick.Console, …)`, following the same tick and perf-timer pattern as the other per-frame ticks; there is no injected MonoBehaviour. The Unity-free decisions behind it — whether a toggle may run, how the slide advances, and what pause and cursor state a close restores — live in `console_state.cs` (`ConsoleState`, `ConsoleSlide`, `ConsoleEntryState`, `ConsoleLinePlan`) and are unit tested in `tests/MegabonkLevels.Tests/console_state_tests.cs` like the command language. The overlay itself only reads input, builds UGUI, and applies those decisions.

**Look**: a single dark gray translucent image (alpha 0.72, not the shared opaque bordered fill) anchored to the bottom edge, roughly 40% of screen height, using the game's RobotoMono TMP font and the shared editor UI helpers. It is one column of text rows laid out like a terminal: the input line has no fill of its own and is the last row of the output, a `>` prompt before the text (aligned with the `> <line>` echo in the scrollback) and a blinking block caret (`TextFieldView` with `blockCaret`, a translucent cell-wide block over the character at the caret; the palette search and save-name fields keep the thin bar). Scrollback lines longer than the row are hard-wrapped at the monospace column count (`ConsoleScrollback.Wrap`, applied in `Append` with the width measured when the panel is built), so a long reply such as `state` continues on the next rows instead of running off the screen; the persisted log keeps each line whole. The prompt row follows the last printed row (`ConsoleLayout.PromptRow`), so after a clear the prompt is at the top and output grows downward until the panel is full, then scrolls with the prompt on the bottom row; while the view is scrolled back the prompt stays pinned to the bottom row and a light gray placeholder that disappears with the first typed character and returns the next time the console is opened (`TextFieldView`, the same single-line model as the palette search and save-name fields).

**Text rows must be taller than the font's line**: a TMP label whose rect is shorter than one line (RobotoMono at 16 pt is 21.1 px) and whose overflow mode is Ellipsis reports `isTextOverflowing: true`, drops every glyph and never generates a mesh, so `renderedWidth` and `renderedHeight` stay at the no-geometry sentinel (-4294967300) while the text property holds the string. The scrollback rows were 20 px tall and drew nothing for that reason; the input line and placeholder drew because they use Overflow or a taller rect. Rows are now 22 px and use Overflow mode. The font metrics exist only in the live game, so this is checked there: after `help`, `query-objects TextMeshProUGUI --fields renderedWidth` must show real widths for the `Line*` rows.

**Toggle**: the backquote/tilde key opens and closes it. The toggle is read in `OnUpdate` before every mod hotkey and the console's own tick runs after the editor's, so the panel keeps rendering while the editor holds the run frozen. The character the backquote produces is dropped from the frame's text so the toggling key never lands in the input line. The toggle does nothing while the game's own F10 `DebugConsole` is active or the game's pause menu is up — those own the keyboard — so it neither opens over them nor closes them. While the console has focus the mod's F6–F9 hotkeys and the editor's hotkeys do not fire (the frame returns before reaching them), and a `PauseUi.Toggle` prefix consumes the game menu toggle, so Esc closes the console without opening the pause menu.

**Keys while open** (all read through `EditorInput`, never `UnityEngine.Input`, so the harness `key`/`type` commands drive it): Enter runs the line and echoes `> <line>` plus the reply into scrollback; Up/Down walk history; Tab completes the verb or the `level.load` title at the caret, and a repeat Tab on the unedited line cycles the matches; PageUp/PageDown scroll the scrollback; Left/Right/Home/End/Delete edit the caret and Backspace deletes; Ctrl-R opens the history search; Ctrl-W deletes the whitespace-delimited word before the caret, Ctrl-U deletes everything before it, Ctrl-K deletes everything after it (`TextFieldModel.DeleteWordLeft`, `DeleteToStart` and `DeleteToEnd`), Ctrl-A and Ctrl-E move the caret to the start and end of the line, and Option (Alt) with Left or Right jumps a whitespace-delimited word (`CaretKeys.WordLeft` and `WordRight`, repeating while held; under Wine on macOS the prefix needs `LeftOptionIsAlt` and `RightOptionIsAlt` set to `y` under `HKCU\\Software\\Wine\\Mac Driver`, or Option reaches the game as a plain arrow and moves one character); Ctrl-L clears the scrollback, including the persisted scrollback in `console_log.jsonl`, exactly as typing `clear` does, but leaves the input line, caret and command history untouched and records nothing; the mouse wheel scrolls the scrollback (see "Console scrollbar"); Esc closes the console. BackQuote, PageUp, PageDown, Home and End were added to the harness key-name allow-list (`KeyNames`, mirrored in `megabonk_harness.mcp_server`) so `run.py key` and the MCP `key` tool can drive those keys.

**Console scrollbar**: a thin thumb on the right edge of the scrollback appears only while the mouse wheel scrolls it, and disappears 2 seconds after the last wheel event; each further event restarts the timer. One wheel notch up moves three lines toward older output, and wheel events count only while the pointer is over the panel (the bottom 40% of the screen) and the history search is closed. The bar stays hidden at rest, while PageUp/PageDown scroll, when the whole scrollback fits in the viewport, and while the history search is open; opening the console hides it. The thumb height is the visible fraction of the buffer (at least 16 px) and its position follows the scroll offset, with the bottom of the track meaning the newest lines. `ConsoleScrollbar` (`console_scrollbar.cs`) holds the Unity-free timer and thumb geometry and `ConsoleScrollback.ScrollBy` the wheel scroll, both unit tested in `console_scrollbar_tests.cs` and `console_scrollback_tests.cs`; the overlay only reads the wheel and places the thumb. Verified in the live game with `run.py mouse scroll 700 800 --delta 2` after opening the console and running `help` a few times: a screenshot half a second later shows the thumb and the scrolled lines, one 2.5 seconds later shows no thumb, and a PageUp shows neither.

**Console history search**: Ctrl-R (either Ctrl) opens an fzf-style finder over the remembered history (up to 200 entries, restored from `console_log.jsonl`). The finder saves the current line and caret and clears the input line, which then holds the query; the rows are replaced by the matching entries, newest nearest the prompt and older ones above, then an info line (`matched/total`), then the query on the prompt row at the bottom (fzf's default layout, `ConsoleLayout.FinderIndex`). Results keep chronological order rather than relevance order. The selected row has a `>` gutter marker and the matched characters are drawn in amber. Keys: Up/Ctrl-P move up the list toward older entries, Down/Ctrl-N toward newer, clamped at the ends with the list scrolling to keep the selection visible; typing, Backspace, Ctrl-W, Ctrl-U, Ctrl-K, Ctrl-A, Ctrl-E, Option-Left/Right and the caret keys edit the query; Enter drops the selected entry into the input line with the caret at its end and closes the finder, without running it (fzf's default), so a second Enter submits it through the normal path (echoed, bound, run, recorded in history) and the entry can be edited first; it does nothing when no entry matches; Esc closes the finder only, restoring the line and caret exactly as they were, running nothing and adding no history entry, while Esc outside the finder still closes the console. Letters typed while Ctrl is held are dropped so the chord never types `r`, `p` or `n`. From the harness, `run.py key R --mod LeftControl` opens it and `run.py type` sends the query; send Enter as a `\n` inside `type` (a literal newline, for example `type $'\n'` in bash), because the overlay reads Enter from the input string and `key Return` does not submit.

The query is fzf's extended-search syntax: space-separated terms are ANDed, and a lone `|` joins the terms around it into an OR group that binds tighter than the space (`^core go$ | rb$ | py$` means `^core` and (`go$` or `rb$` or `py$`)).

| Term | Matches |
| --- | --- |
| `sbtrkt` | fuzzy: the characters in order, anywhere |
| `'wild` | exact substring |
| `^music` | prefix |
| `.mp3$` | suffix |
| `^state$` | whole line |
| `!fire` | lines without the substring |
| `!^music`, `!.mp3$` | lines not starting or not ending with it |

Matching is smart-case: case-insensitive unless the query contains an uppercase letter. A fuzzy term highlights the tightest span ending at its leftmost match. The matcher (`console_fuzzy.cs`) and the selection and scroll state (`console_finder.cs`) are Unity-free and unit tested in `console_fuzzy_tests.cs` and `console_finder_tests.cs`; the overlay only reads input and draws rows. Tab multi-select, previews and relevance sorting are not implemented.

**Pause and cursor**: opening pauses the run through `MyTime.Pause()` and records the pause and cursor state at that moment in a `ConsoleEntryState`. Closing restores it: only a run the console itself paused is unpaused, so a console opened inside the already-frozen editor (or during the death screen) leaves it frozen on close, and opening on the main menu pauses nothing because there is no run. The cursor is set visible and unlocked while open and its previous state is restored on close.

**Command execution**: a typed line is bound by `ConsoleCommandRegistry` and, if it names a harness command, run in-process through `DevHarness.Run` (the same entry point the file queue uses), so a console verb and the same `run.py` command cannot drift. `ConsoleState.Plan` turns the bind plus the harness result into the lines to print, whether the scrollback clears, and whether the line enters history; a refused line echoes the line and the refusal, a console-only verb (`clear`, `help`, `history`) prints its own reply, and a bound line prints what the harness returned.

**Backslashes**: input, replies and history-search rows display backslashes literally; TMP control-sequence parsing is disabled so a Windows path ending in `\tony.png` keeps its separator and `t` instead of turning `\t` into a tab. Real newline characters still split replies into scrollback lines. Structured replies without a `message` (such as `level.save`) remain JSON, including JSON's doubled backslashes; the console does not unescape that JSON or change the persisted text.

**Visibility to tooling**: the console registers `ConsoleInput`, `ConsoleScrollback` and `ConsolePanel` as widgets, so the harness `ui-tree`/`click-widget` commands see them even outside the editor.

Verified in the live game by screenshot: scrollback and input text, the placeholder, the block caret, `?`, `history`, `clear` and persistence across a relaunch. Not yet verified: the slide animation while paused, cursor restore, and the exact DebugConsole and hotkey interaction.

## Not covered

The harness does not press menu buttons or open the level-up dialog. Only `state`, `ui-tree`, `click-widget`, `save-level`, `new-level`, `list-levels`, `palette`, `query-objects`, `teleport`, `item`, `dismiss-levelup`, `dismiss-offers`, `interact` and `perf` return data; the other commands report success or failure and nothing else, and `state` does not report health, inventory, enemies or the level-up dialog (the profiler's `perf status` reports live enemy counts and the player's hit points). Use `freeze` to stop an idle player dying during a screenshot, or `invulnerable` when enemies must keep moving.

## Character selection

A level can limit its characters with the `allowedCharacters` field (see [level-format.md](level-format.md)); Warehouse allows Calcium (`ECharacter.Calcium`, 1) and Tony McZoom (`ECharacter.TonyMcZoom`, 13). Both ids live in `character_override.cs`, which also holds the name parsing (`calcium`, `tony`, `tonymczoom`, case-insensitive) and the pure grid-order and dev-flag logic. `CharacterPolicy` (`character_policy.cs`) turns a level's names into allowed ids, decides whether a character is permitted and builds the refusal text. A level without the field has no restriction. All of it is unit tested.

With `MEGABONK_DEV` unset, `LevelLoaderPatch.LoadAndStart` (F6 and `load-level`) does not start a level that has `allowedCharacters`; a level without the field starts at once with the character already selected. It holds the level, restricts the game's own Character Selection screen and logs `LevelLoader: waiting for a character to be confirmed`. The player then goes through the stock screens (Play, map, character). When a character is confirmed, a first-priority prefix on `MapController.StartNewMap` replaces the stock `RunConfig` with the held level's (seed, edits, music, title and the custom-run marking are prepared at that moment) and the run starts as the confirmed character. The map and tier picked on the stock screens are discarded; the level file decides them. `run.py character-screen off` (MCP `character_screen(on=false)`) cancels a held load and restores the stock grid. `run.py character-screen on` only restricts the screen.

`CharacterScreenPatch` restricts the screen after `CharacterMenu.OnEnable`, and at once if the grid is already built. It moves Calcium and Tony McZoom to the first two sibling slots under `characterGridParent`, and for every other character clears `canUseCharacter`, tints the icon black and shows `unavailableOverlay`, which is how the game already shows a locked character. The `cantUseCharacterReason` string is set to the refusal text ("Pick Calcium or Tony McZoom for this level"), which the game shows as a popup when a grayed character is clicked. It then reorders the private `characterButtons` list to the new sibling order and calls `SetupButtonNavigation` again, because the game derives keyboard and controller navigation from that list, not from the transforms; without this the d-pad and arrow keys jump around the old layout. Controller focus is set explicitly to Calcium, because the game focuses its saved character before the reorder and the controller otherwise starts on a stale button. The stock order, flags, tint, overlay and sibling index of every button are snapshotted on first sight and restored when the restriction ends. The screen is built once per session, so nothing is rebuilt or created. A menu with fewer than two buttons is not built yet and is skipped.

The game saves the confirmed character as `Preferences.selectedCharacter` in `config.json` as soon as it is confirmed. `CharacterScreenPatch` records the saved value when the restriction starts and writes it back with `SaveManager.SaveConfig` when the restriction ends, so a custom run leaves the player's own choice unchanged. Backing out of either selection screen cancels a held load and restores the stock character grid and saved preference. Forward navigation from characters to maps preserves the hold: the native menu enables the map tab before disabling the character tab, and the character-disable hook checks that destination. A successful start consumes the hold before the map screen disables, so closing the menus cannot cancel the run's startup cover. Check the preference by confirming a character other than the saved one, quitting the game, and reading `Saves/LocalDir/config.json` (under the bottle's `AppData/LocalLow/Ved/Megabonk`); a mod-loaded run that exits abnormally before the run starts leaves the confirmed character saved.

With `MEGABONK_DEV=true` (environment or `.env`; `task` loads `.env`) the screen is skipped. A load starts at once with Tony McZoom, or with the character named by the `character` field of `load-level` (`run.py load --character calcium`, `run.py relaunch --character calcium`, MCP `load(character="calcium")`); `squeaker` is accepted too when the squeaker pack is installed (see [Characters](characters.md#squeaker-in-the-game)). An unknown name rejects the load and logs the allowed names. `relaunch` needs the flag, because without it the load waits for the screen and the kill floor never arms. The flag assigns the static `CharacterMenu.selectedCharacter` directly and never touches the saved preference. If the assignment throws, `CharacterOverride.TryApply` returns a warning that the loader logs, and the run starts with the character the menu last selected.

Calcium's skateboard comes with the character: a run started as Calcium shows the skateboarder without any mod code selecting a skin, hat or mesh, so it is part of the game's own spawn for `ECharacter.Calcium`. Which asset carries it (`CharacterData.prefab` or a skin) is not traced.

To confirm which character spawned, aim the camera at the player (`run.py camera`) and read a screenshot; the editor's own camera can override the aim right after a relaunch, so send it again.

## Map Selection entries

`MapSelectionPatch` (`map_selection_patch.cs`) lists every `.mblevel.json` in `UserData/Levels` that has a `title` on the game's Map Selection screen, after the native maps. `CustomMapEntries` (`custom_map_entries.cs`) picks and orders them by title; an untitled level is not listed. A postfix on `MapSelectionWindow.InitButtons` clones `mapEntryPrefab` once per level under `selectionGroup`, calls `MapEntry.Set` with the level's base `MapData`, then overwrites the name and description (`mapDescription`, or `description` when it is absent) and sets the level's sidecar image as the thumbnail, or a flat purple placeholder without one (see "Level thumbnail" in [level-format.md](level-format.md)). The game wires explicit up and down navigation once when it builds the list, so the patch calls `selectionGroup.FindButtons()` and relinks the navigation of every entry; without that a controller or keyboard cannot reach the appended entries.

A postfix on `MapSelectionUi.OnMapSelected` records whether the selected button is a custom entry. A prefix on `MapSelectionUi.StartMap` handles Confirm. Confirm on a native map drops any level held earlier. Confirm on a custom entry checks `CharacterMenu.selectedCharacter` against the level's `allowedCharacters`: a character outside the list cancels the start, logs the refusal and shows it through `MenuNotice` (`menu_notice.cs`), a short overlay near the top of the screen for refusals on the game's own menus. An allowed character holds the level through `LevelLoaderPatch.HoldForNextStart`, and the `MapController.StartNewMap` call that `StartMap` makes next is swapped for the level's `RunConfig`. Unlike the harness hold, this does not restrict the character screen, because the character is picked before the map. `MEGABONK_DEV` has no effect on this path.

A custom level fixes its own tier, so its entry offers only the first tier button, like Graveyard; the level file still determines the actual run tier. A prefix on `MapSelectionUi.OnMapSelected` records the custom/native selection before the first stats refresh. Each `RefreshTiers` repair explicitly enables and unlocks the first button, hides and locks the others, and redirects navigation away from hidden tiers. Tier 1 links directly to Challenges and back. Navigation values are captured separately before mutation, because generated non-blittable interop values can be reference-backed.

The outer map-selection postfix selects and focuses the first tier after the game has restored the base map's remembered tier. Do not shorten/reindex the selection group during `RefreshTiers`: the native handler still uses that remembered index afterward. Selecting the first tier emits another native stats refresh, so navigation repair must also run on refresh, not just once afterward. Before the next map selection, restore captured navigation and let the native refresh set tier visibility and unlocks. A postfix on `MapStatsInfoUi.SetConfig` blanks only the runs, highscore and speedrun values for custom entries, since the base map's records do not describe the custom level. The same postfix sets the map name to the level's `title` and, when the level has a `.biome` sidecar, swaps the right-hand biome icon (see "Level biome icon" in [level-format.md](level-format.md)); the specs, tier and stat labels remain unchanged; selecting a native entry lets `SetConfig` restore its records. The hover methods of the selection buttons share their native code with unrelated methods and cannot be patched, so nothing reacts to hover.

To check it, run `task thps` to install the level and pack, then run `relaunch(no_level=true)` and click through Play, the character, Map Selection and Confirm by hand. The log shows `MapSelection: listed 'Warehouse'`, `MapSelection: selected thps.mblevel.json` and `LevelLoader: starting map=Forest`. A relaunch starts the game unfocused, so click its window before using the menu.

## Player installer

`task package:linux` builds the player-facing Linux download — the mod, the vendored Windows MelonLoader and .NET 6 archives, the installer, and any levels or packs you name — into `.tools/player-package/` plus a distributable zip, and `task package:check` re-verifies a built package against its manifest. Levels and packs are never bundled unless you pass `LEVELS=` and `PACKS=`, so a dev regression map cannot reach a player by accident. The package format, the manifest contract, and the Proton-specific behavior are in [player-installer.md](player-installer.md).

## Python tests

The tooling scripts under `scripts/` are covered by pytest tests in `tests/` (the C# tests live in `tests/MegabonkLevels.Tests`). `task scripts:test` runs them, and `task test` runs them after the C# tests. `[tool.pytest.ini_options]` in `pyproject.toml` is the only pytest configuration: it sets `testpaths = tests`, puts `scripts/` on the import path with `pythonpath` so a test does `import game_paths` directly, and runs the suite in parallel across CPU cores through pytest-xdist (`addopts = -n auto`, ~24s serial becomes ~7s). Pass `-n 0` for a serial run, which keeps `--durations` and failures in file order; `-p no:xdist` does not work because `addopts` still passes `-n`. Async tests are marked `pytest.mark.anyio` and select the asyncio backend in `tests/conftest.py`.

`task check` ends with `task lint`, which runs `markdownlint -c .markdownlint.jsonc .` and needs a `markdownlint` binary on `PATH`; a machine without one runs every test and then fails the check with `executable file not found`, so read the output above the final error before assuming a test broke. Install `markdownlint-cli` (`brew install markdownlint-cli`), not `markdownlint-cli2`, which is a different command with no `-c` flag that ignores `.markdownlintignore`.

`task check` runs the build, then `task installer:test` (gofmt, `go vet` and `go test` over the Go installer, which is the only player installer), then the C# and Python tests, then lint. The Go step needs the Go toolchain that mise installs from `.tool-versions` and fails when `go` is not on `PATH` rather than skipping, so run `task check` through `mise exec --` on a machine that has not activated mise. The Python installer and its parity tests were removed; `scripts/package_contract/` keeps only the manifest and pack checks the package builder shares with it.

`pyproject.toml` declares runtime dependencies under `[project.dependencies]`
and test and lint tooling under `[dependency-groups] dev`; `uv.lock` records
their resolved versions. uv installs the `dev` group by default, so a test run
needs no flags:

```sh
uv run pytest
uv run pytest tests/test_build_thps_level.py -q
```

`--with` is not needed: the `dev` group brings pytest and its plugins, and the
project dependencies bring runtime packages such as `python-decouple`, `mcp` and
`pillow`. Reserve `--with` for a genuinely ad-hoc dependency the project does not
declare. `uv run` automatically checks the lockfile and syncs the project
environment; add `--locked` when the command must fail instead of updating an
out-of-date lockfile. Name the group explicitly with `--group dev`, or leave it
out with `--no-dev`.

`tests/conftest.py` has an autouse fixture that removes every `MEGABONK_*` environment variable and points `game_paths.ENV_FILE` at a nonexistent file for each test, so a developer's shell or `.env` (for example `MEGABONK_WINE_PREFIX`) cannot change a result. A test that needs a setting sets it with `monkeypatch.setenv` or passes `env_file_values` to the function under test. Async tests use `@pytest.mark.anyio`; the conftest pins `anyio_backend` to `asyncio`, so no trio dependency is needed.

Tests that read build products the repo does not carry skip rather than fail. The precise form is a module-level `needs_extract` skipif over `build_thps_level`'s `NODES_PATH` and `OBJ_PATH` (over `check_thps_piece_inventory`'s `OBJ_PATH` in the inventory test, and over `pieces.json` and the piece prefabs in `tests/test_thps_pack_pieces.py`); `task thps` provisions the extraction under `.tools/thps1/warehouse`. `tests/conftest.py` backs this with a net so a file that forgets its guard still skips: `GENERATED_ARTIFACTS` names each product as a `module.ATTRIBUTE` reference or a repo-relative path, and a `pytest_runtest_makereport` wrapper turns a `FileNotFoundError` whose `filename` resolves to an absent entry into a skip. Add a new product to that tuple rather than writing another guard. Skips with those reasons are therefore expected on a fresh checkout, and failures are not.

An autouse conftest fixture also replaces `os.kill` with a stub that raises, so a test that reaches a real `os.kill` fails with an instruction to `monkeypatch.setattr(os, "kill", ...)` instead of signaling a live process. Tests that assert on the kills they perform install their own recorder, which replaces the stub. `wait_for_wineserver_to_exit` treats `EPERM` as a server it cannot kill rather than a crash: it counts killed and refused pids separately, prints both, and reports failure.

`task scripts:test` is just `uv run pytest`. Resolution policy lives in
`[tool.uv]`: `exclude-newer = "2026-10-31T00:00:00Z"` caps candidate packages and
`prerelease = "allow"` matches what `uv.lock` records under `[options]`. Both
belong in `pyproject.toml` rather than a Taskfile flag or a shell export:
`uv.lock` records the same values but is a generated file, so a lock regenerated
without them silently drops them and resolves differently. The interpreter comes from
`requires-python`, which already pins `>=3.13,<3.14`, so no `--python` flag is
needed either. Project dependency updates belong in `pyproject.toml` and
`uv.lock`. PEP 723 scripts run with `uv run --script` use their own inline
dependencies rather than the `dev` group; keep their headers/pins current
separately.

## Reading the game's code

Megabonk is an Il2Cpp build, so there is no managed IL to decompile anywhere in the install. Both sets of assemblies that look decompilable are stubs:

- `MelonLoader/Il2CppAssemblies/*.dll` are Il2CppInterop proxies. Every field is a `NativeFieldInfoPtr_*` and every method a `NativeMethodInfoPtr_*` thunk that calls into native code.
- Il2CppDumper's `.tools/il2cpp_dump/DummyDll/*.dll` are metadata shells: real signatures and `[Address(RVA = ...)]` attributes, but every method body is empty.

Running a decompiler on either returns that scaffolding and never game logic. The check takes seconds, and is worth doing before planning any work around "reading the implementation":

```sh
ilspycmd -t MyTime "<game dir>/MelonLoader/Il2CppAssemblies/Assembly-CSharp.dll" | head -20
```

A wall of `NativeFieldInfoPtr_`/`NativeMethodInfoPtr_` declarations, or a body of `{ }`, means there is nothing further to extract from that file.

What to read instead:

| Question | Where to look |
| --- | --- |
| A type's fields, method signatures, enum values, attributes | `.tools/il2cpp_dump/dump.cs`, regenerate with `task re:dump` |
| A string constant | `.tools/il2cpp_dump/stringliteral.json` |
| What a method actually does | Native code in `GameAssembly`, in Ghidra (see "Ghidra" below) or Binary Ninja, at the RVA listed above the method in `dump.cs` (also on `DummyDll`'s `[Address]` attribute) |
| A value set in the Unity editor rather than in code | Nowhere in the dump — confirm it in the running game |

`dump.cs` is the complete managed surface and answers most questions on its own, which is why it is worth grepping first; dropping to Binary Ninja is for the cases where the shape of a type is not enough and the actual logic matters.

That last table row covers a whole class of behavior that is not in code at all. Serialized ScriptableObjects, scene and prefab data are not emitted by the dumper, so the game's Rewired key bindings live in an input-manager asset: `MyInputManager`'s action names (`Jump`, `Interact`, `MapOverlay`, and the rest) are plainly visible in `dump.cs`, but which physical key each action binds is not recorded anywhere the dump can see. Questions of that shape get settled by trying it in the game, not by reading harder.

### Ghidra

Ghidra is available as an alternative to Binary Ninja for reading `GameAssembly`. `task ghidra:install` downloads the pinned release into `$HOME/.local/opt/ghidra`, points `$HOME/.local/opt/ghidra/current` at it, and on macOS builds the native components (decompiler, sleigh) with the bundled Gradle wrapper, because the macOS release ships without them. Every step checks its result first, so re-running is a no-op once everything is in place. `task ghidra:run` installs if needed and launches the GUI.

Ghidra 12.1 needs Java 21 or newer. The tasks use the `java` pinned in `.tool-versions` through mise, and `JAVA_HOME` is set per command rather than globally. The version is pinned in `taskfiles/ghidra.yml` (`GHIDRA_VERSION`, `GHIDRA_DATE`); a new version installs beside the old one and `current` moves to it. The first GUI launch may quarantine the locally built natives under Gatekeeper; clear it with `xattr -dr com.apple.quarantine "$HOME/.local/opt/ghidra/current/"`.

#### Using Ghidra through REA

The REA MCP server (`rea` tools: `open_binary`, `analyze_function`, `procedure_pseudo_code`, `xrefs`, and the rest) has a Ghidra provider that runs headless Ghidra against a target. It stays `not_configured` until `GHIDRA_INSTALL_DIR` and `JAVA_HOME` are set, which Claude Code passes on to the REA process. Set machine-specific variables in the `env` block of the gitignored `.claude/settings.local.json`; do not commit absolute home paths:

| Variable | Value |
| --- | --- |
| `GHIDRA_INSTALL_DIR` | `$HOME/.local/opt/ghidra/current` as an absolute path, so a version bump in `taskfiles/ghidra.yml` needs no change here |
| `JAVA_HOME` | the output of `mise where java`, a JDK 21 or newer (REA rejects a JRE or a 32-bit JDK) |
| `REA_GHIDRA_STARTUP_TIMEOUT_MS` | `3600000`. REA's default deadline for import plus auto-analysis is 330000 ms (5.5 minutes), and `GameAssembly.dll` did not finish within it |
| `GHIDRA_HEADLESS_MAXMEM` | `6G`. REA passes this as the headless JVM's `-Xmx`, falling back to `GHIDRA_MAXMEM` and then `2G`; an Il2Cpp binary has a very large function count, and the machine has 16 GB |

Use absolute paths for each machine (`$HOME` and command substitution are not expanded in `settings.json`). Local settings override the committed shared timeouts without exposing machine-specific paths. `JAVA_HOME` also has to be updated when the `java` pin in `.tool-versions` changes. Restart Claude Code after changing either value. Check the setup, outside Claude Code, with `GHIDRA_INSTALL_DIR=...
