`GameAssembly.dll` is a Windows x86_64 il2Cpp assembly (54.8 MB). The dump `.tools/il2cpp_dump/dump.cs` lists 114,472 methods across 73 images. Image 3, `Assembly-CSharp.dll`, is the game code: 1,221 types and 8,697 methods, of which 841 are the shared empty stub at RVA `0x3321E0`/`0x3321F0`, leaving about 7,850 real bodies. The other 72 images are Unity modules and third-party packages (Rewired, `com.rlabrecque.steamworks.net`, Discord, Newtonsoft, ProBuilder, TextMeshPro, Localization, Splines, Postprocessing, MK.Toon, Coffee.UIParticle, …). The ~18,200 types in the dump are the game's, not the mod's; the mod source (`src/MegabonkLevels.Mod/`, net6.0, ~14k lines) binds a small subset. libil2cpp is statically linked into `GameAssembly.dll` (it exports 240 `il2cpp_*` functions; the Linux `GameAssembly.so` exports 480), so there is no separate runtime to embed. REA + Ghidra analysis of the binary takes ~12 minutes per session. REA (`/morluto/rea`) is an analysis CLI/MCP (decompile, xrefs, def-use tracing); it does not emit compilable source — reconstruction is hand-authored. The Mac runs the Windows build under CrossOver, so a recomp buys macOS-native execution and drops Steam and the loader from the dev loop rather than cross-platform portability (the game already ships Linux and Windows builds).

### Entry point

The mod is a BepInEx IL2CPP plugin: `megabonk_levels_mod.cs` puts `[BepInPlugin("megabonklevels.runtime", "Megabonk Level Editor", ...)]` on a `BepInEx.Unity.IL2CPP.BasePlugin` subclass, and `Load()` is the hook. Doorstop is the injector: on Windows a `winhttp.dll` proxy plus `doorstop_config.ini` in the game directory, which pulls in `BepInEx/core` and the managed host under the bundled `dotnet/` (the DLL override and the one-proxy-at-a-time rule are under "BepInEx" above). Il2CppInterop then generates the `BepInEx/interop` stub assemblies from `global-metadata.dat` on first run, and the mod compiles against those. A recomp's first call back into the game must be a Unity/Il2Cpp entry (`Il2CppSystem.Reflection.Assembly.LoadFrom` or an equivalent native hook) to resolve types — there is no "just call the game's main" without the runtime. Candidate entry points are doorstop's own injection path (`libdoorstop.dylib` on macOS) or the native executable's own import table.

### Interop surface

The mod already mirrors the game's `EMap` flags enum by value (`level_file.cs`) and binds `Il2Cpp`, `Il2CppAssets.Scripts.*` (Managers, Actors/Player, Steam, MapGeneration/ProceduralTiles, Game/Other), `Il2CppTMPro`, `Il2Cppcom.rlabrecque.steamworks.net`, `Il2CppSystem.Action`, `Il2CppSystem.Collections.Generic.List<GameObject>`, `Il2CppReferenceArray<T>`, and `Il2CppConsistentRandom`. The dump confirms methods carry `// RVA: 0x... Offset: 0x... VA: 0x18...` (VA = image base + RVA). The interop surface spans every Unity module plus Rewired, Discord, Newtonsoft, and ProBuilder. A recomp must reproduce the Il2Cpp runtime's managed/native bridge (BepInEx/Il2CppInterop-style type resolution, delegate marshalling, Il2CppString) or the mod code cannot bind — this is the single largest chunk.

### Steamworks and the offline gate

The game initializes Steam itself before mod code runs (`steam-ready: done` after retries). The mod's only Steam coupling is the guarded read in `game_build_id_patch.cs` — `Steamworks.SteamApps.GetAppBuildId()`, plus `Steamworks.SteamAPI.IsSteamRunning()` to say *why* it is unavailable — reached from the harness `SteamReady` case and from level load and save.

Static analysis of the `SteamManager` methods (`SteamManager.Load` `0x18052AC00`, `IsInitialized` `0x18052ABB0`, `Update` `0x18052B310`, `.cctor` `0x18052B3B0`, with callees resolved through `dump.cs`, RVA = VA − 0x180000000) shows the game tolerates a failed `SteamAPI.Init()`: a false result logs `Debug.LogError` and returns, with no `Application.Quit`, no throw and no retry, so `initialized` stays false and `InitComponents` never runs. The main menu, level load and gameplay are not gated on `initialized`. What degrades without Steam: language falls back to a non-Steam default; achievements, stats and leaderboards never init; Discord rich presence and server-real-time skip their Steam parts; `Chatbox` passes a `steamId` of 0; and `SaveManager.Load(bool)` forwards that 0 into `Load(ulong, …)`. `RestartAppIfNecessary` is called once from `Load` with `AppId_t.Invalid` (0) rather than the real `APP_ID` (3405340, set in the `.cctor`); a true result logs and returns with no quit. `SteamAPI.IsSteamRunning()` and `SteamApps.GetAppBuildId()` have no callers in the game's own assemblies, only data refs — the mod is what calls them, so how they behave without a client is a mod concern, measured below.

The saves themselves live under `Application.persistentDataPath` in a layout that is only partly steamId-keyed: `Saves/LocalDir/config.json` holds settings (and is not keyed), while `Saves/CloudDir/<steamId>/` holds `progression.json`, `stats.json` and the Rewired `controller_config.json` — Rewired logs the directory it picked as `Rewired save path set to: …\Saves\CloudDir\<id>\controller_config.json`, which makes the chosen id observable in the log without any instrumentation. `SaveManager` carries `steamIdSave`, `cloudDirectory`/`localDirectory`/`defaultSavesPath`, and `lastSteamIdKey = "saves_last_steamid"`, with `GetCloudFolder`/`GetLocalFolder`/`GetProgressionPath` building the paths and `Load(ulong steamId, bool loadBackup, out string failReason, out string failPath)` reporting both why and where a load failed.

Both of the paths that static analysis could not settle are now settled by a run against the Windows build under Wine, with the *original* shim (not the stub) and Steam unable to start. Three results:

- **The game's own save survives a failed `SteamAPI.Init()`.** The run logged `[Steamworks.NET] SteamAPI_Init() failed.`, the game still reached the menu, and Rewired's path line went to `CloudDir\69\` first and then to `CloudDir\<the real 64-bit id>\` — no `CloudDir\0` was ever created. The id therefore does not come from Steam: `steamclient64.dll` is loaded and unloaded during the failed init, so nothing Steam-backed can answer afterwards, and the value is the player's own account id, which cannot be baked into the game. The one local store holding exactly that value is the PlayerPrefs key `saves_last_steamid` (on Windows, the `Software\<CompanyName>\<ProductName>` registry key of the profile), read by `SaveManager` as its offline fallback.
- **The no-key branch was then run directly, and it does not fall back the way the log line suggested.** With `wineserver` stopped, the `Software\<Company>\<Product>` hive deleted from the bottle's `user.reg` and the whole `Saves/` tree moved aside, the launch resolved `CloudDir\69\` and then `CloudDir\0\`. A control launch with the hive left in place, everything else identical, resolved `CloudDir\69\` and then `CloudDir\<the real 64-bit id>\`. So `69` is not the no-key fallback — it is the first of the two Rewired paths in either configuration — and the account id in the second path comes from that registry key: the same run against the stub, whose `SteamAPI_ISteamUser_GetSteamID` returns 0, still resolved the real id whenever the key was present. A profile with no key therefore never resolves an account folder, creates no `CloudDir/<id>` directory, and does not write a fresh `saves_last_steamid`. Neither run wrote save files at all (boot, one level load, one kill), so whether a *completed* run starts a fresh progression under a new folder is still unobserved; what is observed is that the id is unavailable without the key. Registry hives and the `Saves/` tree were restored from hash-verified copies afterwards.
- **The mod, not the game, was what failed without a working shim.** With the same setup, a harness level load was rejected: `DevHarness: 'LoadLevel' failed: System.InvalidOperationException: Steamworks is not initialized.` thrown by `Steamworks.InteropHelp.TestIfAvailableClient()` from `SteamApps.GetAppBuildId()`. So the stub was load-bearing for level loading rather than incidental: it answers `CreateInterface`, which is exactly what kept that call from throwing. The mod now reads the build id through one guarded accessor (`game_build_id_patch.cs` over the pure `build_id_source.cs`): an uninitialized client reads as `null`, is logged once per run rather than once per level load, and is retried rather than cached, because availability changes while the game starts. A null build id means *unknown* everywhere it lands — `GameBuildIdCheck.MismatchWarning` stays silent when either side is empty, the same rule `GamePackVersionCheck` uses, and a level saved offline records an empty `gameBuildId` so it cannot warn falsely later either. Measured on the same Wine install, all three configurations load the level and arm the kill floor:

| Shim | mod's build-id read | harness `steam-ready` |
| --- | --- | --- |
| stub | `21750826` | `ready (build 21750826)` |
| stub, `MEGABONK_STEAM_FAIL_INIT=1` | `0`, read as no build id | `not ready (steam-not-running)` |
| original, `steam.exe` disabled | throws | `not ready (steam-not-running)` |

`steam-ready` answers `{"ready":false,"reason":…}`, with `steam-not-running` only when the client can positively say Steam is down and `client-not-initialized` otherwise, including when `SteamAPI.IsSteamRunning()` itself cannot be consulted; the same text is in the log line and in what `run.py steam-ready` prints. Alongside it the probe reports what the verdict was read against — `steamRunning`, `steamSession` (whether `GetHSteamPipe`/`GetHSteamUser` are non-zero), `steamLibrary` (`stub`, `original`, `missing` or `unknown`, from the bytes of the loaded `Megabonk_Data/Plugins/x86_64/steam_api64.dll`) and `failInitEnv` — because the same words mean different things depending on which library is in place. One trap when reproducing this: `[Steamworks.NET] SteamAPI_Init() failed.` is *not* evidence that the original shim is active — the line appears with the stub installed too — so confirm the configuration with `task steamstub:status` or the probe's own `steamLibrary` field.

Two mechanics worth keeping: launching the Windows build with the original shim while Steam is stopped lets `steam_api64.dll` *start Steam for you*, which logs the account in and can sign other machines out, so rename the bottle's `steam.exe` aside for the duration and restore it afterwards; and the swap itself is `task steamstub:restore` / `task steamstub:install` around the shim at `Megabonk_Data/Plugins/x86_64/steam_api64.dll`, with the untouched original sitting beside it as `steam_api64.dll.steamstub.bak` and `task steamstub:status` naming whichever of the two is active even in a worktree that never built the stub.

Still static-analysis-only: what `RestartAppIfNecessary` returns for the invalid id it is called with, and the language-fallback and achievement-skipped degradations, none of which has been watched in a run — though `otherSettings.lastSteamLanguage` in `config.json` shows the language path has run at least once with Steam present.

So the verdict for the parts above is *degraded*, and the offline gate is narrower than it looked: launching and playing without Steam is observed, and so is the game's own save lookup, which resolves its `CloudDir/<steamId>` folder from `saves_last_steamid` even with `SteamAPI.Init()` failed. What remains unverified is DRM-wrapper behavior, not whether the game runs at all. Not covered by this analysis is any DRM or Steam wrapper outside `GameAssembly.dll` (the `UnityPlayer.dll` or the exe); a snapshot of those binaries would be needed.

The harness `steam-ready` probe reads Steamworks directly (`SteamApps.GetAppBuildId()`, guarded, with `SteamAPI.IsSteamRunning()` for the reason), not the game's own `SteamManager.IsInitialized()` (`0x18052ABB0`) — which is worth knowing as a second signal: it reports the game's own Init result and cannot throw (class-init check plus a static read), so a future probe could compare the two. `SteamAPI.IsSteamRunning()` only says a Steam client process exists, not that Init succeeded; the game never calls it and the mod calls it only to label a not-ready verdict. (`SteamClient.IsValid()` is the Facepunch Steamworks API and is not in this game, which uses `com.rlabrecque.steamworks.net`.)

A UnityPy read of `Megabonk_Data/globalgamemanagers` shows `PlayerSettings.forceSingleInstance` False and `runInBackground` True, so Unity itself allows a second copy on one machine; only Steam's single-session-per-account rule blocks it. That single-session conflict is the real gate for running several copies at once:

- A native build launched through Steam still conflicts — one session per account across all devices.
- Several copies at work therefore need either (1) the game running without a Steam session, which the offline dev copy below already does for launch and play, or (2) separate accounts or licenses.
- With the shim stub installed, several copies already work today on CrossOver, Linux and the Windows VM: the game launches and plays with Steam stopped and no login, with no recomp (see the offline dev copy below).

So the offline dev copy (drop Steam from the session) and the macOS-native build (drop CrossOver) are two separate goals: the first unblocks multi-machine development now and needs no recomp; the second is a larger, independent lift.

### Build / relink

The mod builds to `MegabonkLevels.Mod.dll` + `.pdb`, deployed to `$(ModInstanceFolder)` (`BepInEx/plugins/MegabonkLevels`) by the csproj `CopyOutputToPluginsFolder` target; it references `BepInEx.Core.dll`, `BepInEx.Unity.IL2CPP.dll`, `0Harmony.dll`, `Il2CppInterop.Runtime.dll`, and the interop stub assemblies under `$(GameFolder)/BepInEx/interop` as `<Reference>` (not copied, `<Private>False`). A recomp's output is a native DLL (or patched executable), not a managed assembly, and the linking decision gates everything: if it links the game's own native runtime, it must be loaded by the patched exe; if it bundles a runtime, it is effectively a redistributable.

### Routes

Three ways to a macOS-native, Steam-free build, largest first:

- **A — Full static recomp of the Linux build.** XenonRecomp-style recompile of the Linux `UnityPlayer.so` and `GameAssembly.so` (SysV ABI, Vulkan), plus a Linux-to-macOS shim for libc, windowing and Vulkan-through-MoltenVK. Measured: 87,741,504 B and 32,545,400 B of x86-64 to lift, and no maintained lifter produces rebuildable code at this scale — McSema is archived read-only by its owner and needs an IDA Pro license to recover control-flow graphs, and remill (still active) lifts instructions, which its own docs say is not enough without McSema. Largest. The reading about McSema and remill turned out not to matter: the liftability question was sampled on this binary with Ghidra's pcode as the front end and a purpose-written LLVM IR emitter, and the shipped `GameAssembly.so` code lifts and compiles for arm64 (see "Lifting the shipped code to arm64" below). What stays open for A is the OS shim and the Unity player, not the code lift.
- **B — Re-host `GameAssembly` only.** Translate the Linux `GameAssembly.so` into a macOS x86-64 dylib (running under Rosetta) paired with Unity's official 2023.2.22f1 macOS player, reusing `global-metadata.dat` and resolving icalls by name. The shader container was the blocker and is now measured rather than assumed: 99.33% of the shipped GLSL programs reach a compiled Metal artifact through a scripted pass, with a 49-program tail (see "Shader translation pass" below). The OS layer measured smaller than expected — 303 unique undefined libc-family symbols and nothing imported from the platform layer (scored in "Loading and shimming the Linux ELF runtime" below).
- **B′ — re-host `GameAssembly` with an arm64 payload.** Same shape as B — keep the shipped code, pair it with Unity's official player, reuse `global-metadata.dat`, resolve icalls by name — but the payload is a *recompiled* arm64 `GameAssembly` rather than a translated x86-64 one, so it pairs with Unity's official arm64 macOS player and the whole process is native with no expiry. Unity ships arm64 macOS players, and this repo's own probe player is a universal build whose arm64 slice already binds and invokes managed code natively, so the platform half is proven. What is unproven is the payload: it needs shipped x86-64 il2cpp code lifted to something a compiler can rebuild for arm64, which is the open question a bounded liftability spike has to answer on a measured sample rather than settle by citation. If lifting fails, B′ collapses into B and B's Rosetta expiry is the endpoint. If it works, B′ is the only artifact that is untethered, native and non-expiring without re-authoring 7,856 managed bodies, which makes it the highest-value unmeasured item in the whole route analysis. It is no longer only a hypothesis: a 3,000-body sample of the shipped code lifts to LLVM IR that the verifier accepts and `llc` compiles for arm64, and a differential test on synthetic C matches native arm64, but nothing real has been run (see "Lifting the shipped code to arm64" below).

- **C — pt-pc-style reimplementation.** AssetRipper export, reimplement the ~7,850 `Assembly-CSharp` bodies in C# from `dump.cs` and Ghidra pseudocode, add the packages (TMP, ProBuilder, Localization, Addressables, Splines, PostProcessing, AI Navigation, Steamworks.NET and Coffee.UIParticle are free or OSS; Rewired and MK.Toon are paid), and build for macOS. The mod becomes source in the project with no loader. Risk is behavior drifting from the shipped game. The pt-pc project (github.com/LoreanXavier/pt-pc) is a reimplementation on its own engine reading the original assets, not a static recomp — the model for this route. AssetRipper is not a shader answer: its shader decompilation was removed from master, first the legacy path and then the remainder, with what stayed documented as "provided as-is".

### Recommendation

Keep BepInEx for the dev loop. It is proven and works today, and a recomp is a large, hand-authored lift whose make-or-break is the Il2Cpp runtime bridge, not per-function reconstruction. The two goals split cleanly:

- **Offline copy (drop Steam)** — unblocks multi-machine development now and needs no recomp, and no longer needs the stub either. The game's own save is safe without Steam: `SaveManager` falls back to the `saves_last_steamid` PlayerPrefs value and finds the real `CloudDir/<steamId>` folder even when `SteamAPI.Init()` fails. The mod's one Steamworks read, the build id, tolerates an uninitialized client (see "Steamworks and the offline gate"), so level load and save no longer depend on the stub answering `CreateInterface` — with the original shim in place, `task steamstub:restore`, and Steam unable to start, a harness level load still arms the kill floor. What remains is the degraded Steam-only behavior (achievements, leaderboards, cloud sync) and the fresh-profile case: with no `saves_last_steamid` key the game resolves `CloudDir\0\` and creates no account folder, so a machine that has never had Steam running there does not pick up an existing `CloudDir/<steamId>/` progression until that key is present. This is the direct path to "several Macs at once".
- **macOS-native build (drop CrossOver)** — decided, no longer a cost-benefit question. Running the port under Wine or CrossOver is not an acceptable end state, and the game is unmaintained (see "Cost per game update"), so this is an unofficial port the project owns outright. What remains to choose is *how* — routes B and C, A last — not *whether*.

### Route decision: B over C

Between B and C, **B**: keep the shipped x86-64 code and re-host it, rather than hand-porting the managed layer onto an upstream il2cpp runtime. The deciding factor is not effort but failure mode. B fails loudly — an untranslatable program, a shim symbol that does not exist, an icall that never registers, all countable before anything launches. C fails quietly: a playable port whose behavior drifted, which is the one outcome that corrupts this repo's whole verification loop, because every harness result becomes a statement about the port instead of about the game. The clause that used to close this paragraph — C as the fallback if B's ELF-load-and-shim step failed — is spent: that step is scored `feasible` (see "Loading and shimming the Linux ELF runtime"), so C is no longer a hedge against an unknown, only a choice about the endpoint.

**B is x86-64 only, and that is a whole-process consequence.** Mach-O gives a process exactly one architecture: `dyld` refuses a slice it cannot run, so a translated x86_64 `GameAssembly.dylib` forces the player executable, `UnityPlayer`, and every other load-time dependency onto their x86_64 slices too. There is no configuration in which Route B pairs translated game code with a native arm64 player — the pairing is per-process, not per-library. Since Intel Macs are out of scope for this port, B's only audience is Apple silicon, which means B delivers the current CrossOver experience with the Wine layer deleted and Rosetta kept, and it inherits the date Apple attached to that: macOS 27 as the last release running Intel-only apps. C, built arm64 by Unity, is the only route that is simultaneously untethered, native, and non-expiring — and the only one, because with the game unmaintained there is no upstream to hand over an arm64 build.

So the two routes answer different questions and the honest framing is not "B, with C as a hedge". B is the fastest thing that boots a loader-free native macOS game process and it is worth building for that, but on this port's own stated target it is an artifact with an expiry date. C's remaining cost is one-time and larger — roughly 7,856 managed bodies, plus the two paid packages, plus a shader answer of its own, since AssetRipper exports baked shaders it cannot decompile. That last item is why the shader pass is not B-only work: the shipped data carries the GLSL **source** for all 7,305 programs, so the same scripted GLSL-to-Metal pass is infrastructure for either route, and the 49-program tail is on the trunk whichever endpoint is chosen.

**B's top blocker is the shader container, not libil2cpp's OS layer.** The Linux build's `Megabonk_Data` holds 141 `Shader` objects, each baked (`m_ShaderIsBaked=True`) with an empty `m_Script`, so there is no shader source in any shipped data. Each of the 141 carries two blobs: one GLSL **source** chunk (7,305 programs, each with its own `#version` and `void main`, 34,969,460 B decompressed) and one per-program container (13,336 directory entries, 54,764,176 B, per-program headers plus `VGlobals<digits>` and uniform-name strings, no SPIR-V magic anywhere). The Windows data is the same shape with DXBC instead of GLSL (16,276 entries, 23,314,604 B, 7,184 `DXBC` magics). macOS needs Metal, and Unity removed macOS OpenGL in 2023.1, so a matching-2023.2 macOS player would be Metal-only: there is no vendor blob to copy and nothing to hand to the driver but a translation. That is the work — and it is a scripted pass over 7,305 programs, not a port. The pass has now been run end to end; see "Shader translation pass" below for the measured answer, which confirms this paragraph rather than overturning it.

The OS layer, which looked like the blocker from reading the code, measured small. `GameAssembly.so` needs only `libdl`, `libpthread`, `libm`, `libgcc_s` and `libc`, imports **0** symbols mentioning Unity (icalls are resolved by name at runtime, so there is no link-time dependency on `UnityPlayer.so`), and has 303 unique undefined symbols — mostly `pthread_*`, signal, `sem_*`, socket, `dl*` and `_Unwind_*` — with no `futex`, `clone`, `epoll`, `inotify`, `timerfd`, `eventfd` or `getauxval`, which is the good news, since those are the calls that have no macOS equivalent. What does need shimming is glibc-specific ABI: the `__xstat64`/`__fxstat64`/`__lxstat64` aliases, `__tls_get_addr`, `_ITM_*`, the locale `_l` family, and a baked `fd_set` layout. Every one of those counts is now measured rather than read off the binary, with per-symbol verdicts, under "Loading and shimming the Linux ELF runtime" below; two of the assumptions this paragraph was written from did not survive it.

**The loader is no longer a MelonLoader-shaped unknown.** The plugin is a BepInEx IL2CPP `BasePlugin`, and BepInEx publishes an official `Unity.IL2CPP-macos-x64` build whose injector is doorstop's `libdoorstop.dylib`. It has been run against a matching-2023.2.22f1 macOS player on Apple silicon under Rosetta and reaches `Chainloader initialized` with `BepInEx/interop` generated — 80 assemblies, 16 MB, `Assembly-CSharp.dll` among them — so B's loader half is a maintained artifact rather than something to write. Its macOS gaps are now enumerated by depth rather than guessed: the app-bundle path probes (worked around with symlinks), dyld not seeing `GameAssembly.dylib` or `libdobby.dylib` under the names it spells (worked around with a private `DYLD_LIBRARY_PATH` directory), and BepInEx's native detour segfaulting inside `DobbyPrepare`, which is the one that still stops a plugin from loading. What it takes to get that far, and the crash that stops it there, is under "Companion checks" below.

**Route C, scoped honestly.** `dump.cs` has 73 images; `Assembly-CSharp` alone is 1,215 types with 8,697 distinct RVAs, 841 of them the shared empty stub, so about 7,856 real bodies — roughly 2.4 MB by RVA gap, with 1,390 of them over 512 B. Across all images there are 2,522 distinct managed classes with shipped code (907 in `Assembly-CSharp`, the rest Rewired, mscorlib, UIElements, Unity.Mathematics, Steamworks.NET, TMP, Localization, Discord). Serialized data is separable and is not the problem: 52,545 objects in 76 types, dominated by 14,155 `MonoBehaviour`, 9,492 `GameObject`, 1,209 `Texture2D`, 763 `Mesh`, 716 `Sprite`, 612 `Material` and 311 `AudioClip`, all readable with UnityPy. Roughly 31 shaders are outside engine built-ins (14 `Custom/*`, Archanor's VFX and Retro Arsenal, BadDog's `BGForceField`, Kaima's depth force field, IL3DN water, a cubemap skybox, MK.Toon) and MK.Toon and Rewired are paid. The gap is the same shader gap as B, plus every method body, with no oracle to check against but playtesting.

**Cost per game update, which stopped being the discriminator.** This paragraph used to argue that Route C was untenable rather than merely large because every content patch would cost 40–240 hours of re-porting changed methods, on top of Route B's 0.5–1 dev-day per push for re-running scripted steps. That argument assumed a moving game, and the game does not move: the Steam news API for appid 3405340 returns no developer announcement after **v1.0.64, published 2026-01-25**, which is 294 days of silence as of 2026-11-16, and the maintainer reports no work on it since then. So both routes are now one-time costs, and the honest comparison is one-time effort plus failure mode — B's 66 compat symbols, the 49-program shader tail and a `.so`-to-dylib translation, against C's roughly 7,856 hand-authored managed bodies; B fails loudly and C fails quietly. B remains the choice on that basis, but no longer because C's upkeep was prohibitive.

Three consequences worth spelling out. The `15 depot pushes a month` figure this section previously carried was measured while the game was still being patched and is stale; announcements are a proxy for depot pushes rather than proof of them, and the repository has no depot-history tooling, so the exact date of the last push is unconfirmed. The Unity-version-bump cliff is gone: nothing will bump the engine, so pairing with Unity's official 2023.2.22f1 macOS player is a permanently valid pairing rather than one that survives until the next patch. And `task re:shaders-metal`'s content-hash cache stops mattering for correctness, because the shipped shaders cannot change underneath it.

What did **not** change is the asymmetry that actually decided the route, and one that is now more visible rather than less. B fails loudly and C fails quietly, and C's silent behavioral drift would still turn every harness result into a statement about the port instead of about the game. Meanwhile B still carries a date the port does not: a translated dylib is an Intel-only app, and Apple names macOS 27 as the last release that runs Intel-only apps on Apple silicon, so B buys years rather than decades while C, built arm64, does not expire. With no upstream to fix that, an arm64-native artifact can only come from us — Route A needs a fork of the archived McSema, and Route C is the reimplementation — which makes "how long must this port live, on which machines" the open question rather than "how much upkeep will it need".

**Smallest first step** was the cheapest experiment that could still kill B: script all 7,305 GLSL programs through `glslangValidator` → SPIR-V → `spirv-cross --msl` → `xcrun -sdk macosx metal -c` and report the failure count with reasons. That has been run; the numbers are under "Shader translation pass" below. It ran alongside the two archive/loader checks under "Companion checks", and alongside one cheaper ask that still stands: upstream already ships Linux and Windows players, so a macOS player build from them deletes the shader blocker outright.

### Shader translation pass

`scripts/shader_metal_pass.py` (`task re:shaders-metal`) reads the native Linux build, pulls the GLSL chunk out of every `Shader` object, and drives each program through the four stages. Point it at a build directory; it needs `glslang` and `spirv-cross` (`brew install glslang spirv-cross`) and the Xcode command line tools, and no Unity install, license or game launch. It prints a JSON summary and writes `summary.json`, a per-program `cache.jsonl` (`--resume` reuses records whose source hash is unchanged) and the per-program `.vert/.spv/.metal/.air` artifacts under `.tools/shaders-metal/`. `task re:shaders-pull` refreshes the build from the player box (rsync source in the gitignored `.env` as `MEGABONK_LINUX_BUILD_RSYNC`). Measured with glslang 11:16.6.0, spirv-cross of 2026-09-16 and `metal 32023.883`.

Unit of work: the platform-15 chunk of each `Shader` object holds one `#ifdef <STAGE>`-guarded program per entry, so a program is one `(shader, stage, GLSL version, unit)` — 7,305 programs over 138 shaders (141 objects, 3 without a GLSL chunk), 32,527,272 B: `#version 150` ×4,302, `330` ×2,854, `420` ×146, `410` ×3, and exactly one geometry program. 3,652 vertex and 3,652 fragment programs. Only two preprocess edits are made, both mechanical: the `#version` is raised to `450 core` and the `#extension` lines are dropped (11,830 of them). `--no-normalize` scores the shipped source verbatim.

Result — the whole chain is scriptable. The first pass, with nothing but the shipped source and default tool flags, put **6,689 of 7,305 programs (91.57%)** through `metal -c`. Every failure but one fell into a single diagnostic and every category has a mechanical answer:

| Category | Count | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `'buffer' attribute parameter is out of bounds: must be between 0 and 30` | 583 | glslang's `--auto-map-bindings` spends one binding per array *element*, so indices reach 56 while the program uses at most 38 distinct ones. Renumber `[[buffer(N)]]`/`[[texture(N)]]`/`[[sampler(N)]]` densely (`compact_msl_bindings`) and 535 of the 583 compile. |
| `required extension not requested` (`gl_Layer`, `num_views` in the `Hidden/*` VR and multiview built-ins) | 29 | glslang names the candidate extensions in its own error; re-compile with those as `#extension … : require`. All 29 recover (`GL_ARB_shader_viewport_layer_array`/`GL_NV_viewport_array2` ×24, `GL_OVR_multiview{,2}` ×5). |
| `Multisampled array textures are supported from 2.1` | 3 | spirv-cross defaults to MSL 1.2; pass `--msl-version 20100`. |
| `unknown type name 'unknown'` in the one geometry program | 1 | spirv-cross's MSL backend emits `unknown` as the entry-point return type for `geom`; `--msl-capture-output` and `--stage geom` do not help. |

With those three mechanical fixes on (the defaults), the pass scores **7,305 glslang, 7,305 spirv-cross, 7,256 Metal — 99.33% end to end**, and 6,123 programs come back with bindings renumbered. Verdict from `summary.json`: `scriptable` (the thresholds are ≥0.95 scriptable, ≥0.60 scriptable-with-hand-edits, else hand-or-route-C).

**Go, on the shader question.** B's shader story is scripted translation, not translation by hand, so the outcome that would have flipped the decision to C did not happen: 49 programs — 0.67% — need authored work, in one category (uniforms that must move into blocks), against 4 shaders rather than 138. The route decision above stands.

**The hand-edit tail is 49 programs in 5 shaders**, and it is one kind of work:

- 48 programs exceed Metal's 31 distinct per-stage resource slots outright (max 38 distinct after renumbering): `IL3DN/WaterOptimized` (32), `TextMeshPro/Mobile/Distance Field` (8), `TextMeshPro/Distance Field` (4), `TextMeshPro/Distance Field Overlay` (4). These need their loose uniforms packed into named uniform blocks, which is authored work — a prototype of automatic block packing was rejected here because glslang refuses anonymous blocks and `--uniform-buffer` is not supported. spirv-cross's argument-buffer mode is *not* the way out: `--msl-argument-buffers` at MSL 2.0/3.0 and tier 0/1 emits code that does not compile at all (`member reference base type 'const constant float4 *const constant' is not a structure or union`) on this sample.
- 1 program, the only geometry shader (`Hidden/VR/BlitFromTex2DToTexArraySlice`), for the `unknown`-type reason above.

**glslang's target environment decides everything, and `opengl` is the right choice.** With `--glslang-target-env vulkan1.0` only 179 of 7,305 (2.4%) survive the front end, 7,124 of them on `'non-opaque uniforms outside a block' : not allowed when using GLSL for Vulkan` — Unity's baked GLSL declares its uniforms loosely, which is legal GL and illegal Vulkan. Nothing downstream is a risk on that path either: all 179 that pass glslang also pass spirv-cross and Metal. So the pass uses GL semantics and lets the host bind the resulting per-uniform slots.

Two caveats to keep the number honest. `metal -c` compiles only: it says nothing about blend state, depth conventions (`--fixup-clipspace`/`--flip-vert-y` are runtime decisions the host must make once), culling or the coordinate flips Unity applies, and a translated program still has to be *fed* — the host must bind each auto-mapped slot the renumbered indices imply. And a pass rate measured on one build re-measures per depot push; that is what `--resume` and `task re:shaders-metal` are for.

### Companion checks

- **Unity 2023.2.22f1 still offers macOS Build Support.** In the Unity archive (hash `6b19bf4f8115`), both `MacEditorTargetInstaller/UnitySetup-Mac-Mono-Support-for-Editor-2023.2.22f1.pkg` (554,499,901 B) and `UnitySetup-Mac-IL2CPP-Support-for-Editor-2023.2.22f1.pkg` (539,183,293 B) answer HTTP 200, alongside the matching `MacEditorInstaller` and `MacEditorInstallerArm64` editors (4,186,416,450 B and 4,181,442,624 B). So a Metal-only macOS player of the exact shipping Unity version is still obtainable; route B's "match the player to the build" requirement is satisfiable.
- **BepInEx's macOS IL2CPP half is published, complete and current.** `Unity.IL2CPP-macos-x64` ships on both channels: the last tagged pre-release `v6.0.0-pre.2` (2024-08-27, 32,523,182 B) and the bleeding-edge channel the repo already uses on Windows, up to `6.0.0-be.793` (2026-11-05, 32,735,133 B) at `builds.bepinex.dev/projects/bepinex_be`. The be.793 bundle carries `libdoorstop.dylib` (doorstop 4.6.0, and a universal `x86_64 arm64 arm64e` — so the injector itself does not need Rosetta), `BepInEx/core` with `BepInEx.Unity.IL2CPP.dll`, `Il2CppInterop.Generator.dll`, `Cpp2IL.Core.dll`, `LibCpp2IL.dll` and `0Harmony.dll` (the whole interop-generation chain, so `BepInEx/interop` is a shipped capability rather than something to write), `dotnet/` with `libcoreclr.dylib` and `libclrjit.dylib`, and `run_bepinex.sh`. Everything except doorstop is `x86_64` only, so the managed host still runs under Rosetta — which this Mac has and runs fine (`/usr/bin/arch -x86_64 /bin/echo`).
- **The loader runs a macOS player end to end under Rosetta and generates interop.** Against a minimal IL2CPP player built with the pinned editor 2023.2.22f1 (its `global-metadata.dat` is version 29, the shipped game's version), `BepInEx-Unity.IL2CPP-macos-x64-6.0.0-be.793` injects, writes `BepInEx/LogOutput.log`, logs `Running under Unity 2023.2.22f1`, downloads `https://unity.bepinex.dev/libraries/2023.2.22.zip`, runs Cpp2IL against the player's `GameAssembly.dylib` (`Using actual IL2CPP Metadata version 29.1`, `Using binary type Universal Mach-O File`, 15,955 method definitions) and generates **80 interop assemblies — 16 MB** — including `Assembly-CSharp.dll`, `UnityEngine.CoreModule.dll` (2.7 MB) and `Il2Cppmscorlib.dll` (4.3 MB) plus `assembly-hash.txt`, then logs `Chainloader initialized`. Cpp2IL's experimental Mach-O path is therefore usable, and `BepInEx/interop` is a produced artifact on this platform, not just a shipped capability. Launch the script itself translated — `arch -x86_64 sh ./run_bepinex.sh <App>.app` — because a universal binary follows its parent's slice and the older bundle's script ends in a bare `exec`, which ignores `ARCHPREFERENCE` entirely.
- **Everything that fails is the app-bundle layout, and the loader's own log says why: `System platform: Linux 64-bit`.** With the platform misdetected, `Paths.SetExecutablePath` never takes its macOS branch, so `GameRootPath` becomes the executable's own directory and the data probe looks for `<exe dir>/<Proc>_Data` then `<exe dir>/Data` — while a `.app` keeps its data at `Contents/Resources/Data`. `UnityPreloaderRunner` hardcodes `gameDataRelativeToManaged: false`, so no `DOORSTOP_*` variable can redirect it; `DOORSTOP_MANAGED_FOLDER_DIR` being set changes nothing. Two symlinks in `Contents/MacOS/` are the whole workaround, naming the two paths the loader probes for: `<Proc>_Data -> ../Resources/Data` and `GameAssembly.so -> ../Frameworks/GameAssembly.dylib` (the `.so` suffix follows the misdetected platform).
- **The two bundles fail at different depths from that one cause, so score a bundle by which exception it prints.** With no data path, be.793 fails early and honestly: `ArgumentNullException: Value cannot be null. (Parameter 'gameDataPath')` at `UnityInfo.Initialize`. The older `6.0.0-pre.2` (doorstop 4.3.0) keeps a wrong-but-non-null path, `UnityInfo.DetermineVersion` silently leaves the version at `0.0.0` — it reads the version string out of `<GameDataPath>/globalgamemanagers` at a fixed offset and defaults when the file is missing — and dies deeper in `Il2CppInterop`'s `UnityVersionHandler.GetHandler` with `System.ApplicationException: No handler`. That symptom is misleading: the pre.2 bundle does carry a `2023.2.0` version handler, so it is not missing support, it was never told the version.
- **The `Could not locate Il2Cpp game assembly` failure is dyld's search path, not a name list.** After interop generation and `Chainloader initialized`, be.793 logged `Could not locate Il2Cpp game assembly (GameAssembly.dll, UserAssembly.dll or libil2cpp.so)`, which reads like a missing candidate name. It is not: `IL2CPPChainloader.Initialize` calls `NativeLibrary.TryLoad("GameAssembly", ...)`, a bare-name `dlopen`, so on macOS only `GameAssembly.dylib` and `libGameAssembly.dylib` are ever spelled, and dyld looks in its search directories — never in the executable's own folder. `Contents/Frameworks/GameAssembly.dylib` is therefore invisible to it however many `.dll`/`.so` symlinks sit beside the executable. What works is a private directory holding both spellings as symlinks to the real dylib, put on `DYLD_LIBRARY_PATH` and `DYLD_FALLBACK_LIBRARY_PATH` — exported **from inside `run_bepinex.sh`**, because dyld strips `DYLD_*` when a SIP-protected binary is exec'd, and `arch` and `/bin/sh` are both SIP-protected, so a value set by the caller never reaches the player. With that the fatal disappears and the loader reaches `Chainloader initialized` with interop generated. Point the variable at the bundle's own `Frameworks` directory instead and Unity itself dies with `Failed to load il2cpp`, so keep the directory private to the two symlinks.
- **`libdobby.dylib` does *not* have that defect, and the control run proves it.** `INativeDetour`'s default provider is Dobby, P/Invoked under the bare name `dobby` from a library whose install name is `@rpath/libdobby.dylib`, which looks like the same problem one layer down. It isn't: deleting all four Dobby symlinks from the private directory (`libdobby.dylib`, `dobby.dylib`, `libdobby`, `dobby`) and relaunching still gets as far as `DobbyPrepare` and crashes there, so the library was already resolvable without them — BepInEx resolves its own `core` directory's native dependencies rather than leaving them to dyld. Only the `GameAssembly` load needs the private directory. The shipped `libdobby.dylib` is x86_64 and does export `DobbyHook`, `DobbyPrepare` and `DobbyCommit`.
- **A plugin still does not load, and the reason is the native detour, not the loader's path logic.** With all of the above in place the chainloader initializes, the player boots and runs managed code (`PROBE: managed Awake ran on X64` from a MonoBehaviour in the probe scene), and no `Loading <plugin>` line ever appears, because `IL2CPPChainloader` gates plugin execution on its `il2cpp_runtime_invoke` detour firing. The crash reports name the blocker: `EXC_BAD_ACCESS`/`SIGSEGV` at `0x0` inside `libdobby.dylib:DobbyPrepare`, called from BepInEx's `DobbyDetour` under `libdoorstop.dylib:il2cpp_doorstop_bootstrap`. A second, rarer signature in the same variant is `GameAssembly.dylib:Thread_get_CurrentThread` ← `SynchronizationContext_get_Current` ← `UnitySynchronizationContext_ExecuteTasks` ← `il2cpp::vm::Runtime::Invoke` ← `__NSFireTimer` — a null self-thread pointer, consistent with the injected CoreCLR and il2cpp each wanting the same thread-local slot. So the macOS loader's remaining gate is native hooking, the same family as the arm64 detour work upstream, not path resolution: `BepInEx/BepInEx#1407` ("Find the game data folder and GameAssembly inside a macOS app bundle", closed unmerged 2026-09-25) covers the layout half, `BepInEx/BepInEx#1096` is the open no-logs-no-config report for macOS Intel, and `BepInEx/BepInEx#1405` (merged 2026-11-05) is the arm64 detour fix, still waiting on a doorstop release exporting `doorstop_jit_memcpy` from `NeighTools/UnityDoorstop#114`. `Detours.DetourProviderType` in `BepInEx/config/BepInEx.cfg` selects the detour provider, and be.793 honors a pre-seeded config: setting it to `Funchook` produces a clean `System.DllNotFoundException: Unable to load shared library 'funchook'` in the preloader log instead of a crash, which means swapping providers is only a build problem, not a patch — the dlopen candidate list in that exception includes the working directory and the private `DYLD_LIBRARY_PATH` directory, so one `libfunchook.dylib` dropped beside the other two symlinks is the whole swap. The macOS bundle ships only Dobby, so libfunchook has to be built for macOS x86_64 first.
- **Three control runs separate what each fix contributes.** Run against the same self-built player, one variable at a time, scored by which fatal each prints. Without the private `DYLD_LIBRARY_PATH` directory the by-name fatal returns (`Could not locate Il2Cpp game assembly = 1`) and nothing crashes — that directory alone is what gets past the game-assembly load. Without the Dobby symlinks nothing changes (still `Chainloader initialized`, still the `DobbyPrepare` crash), which is what makes the bullet above what it is. With `DetourProviderType = Funchook` the crash is replaced by the `DllNotFoundException` above, so the detour is genuinely what stands between the loader and a loaded plugin. Together these say the macOS loader's path layer is finished and its hooking layer is the whole remaining problem.
- **Upstream has written the layout half.** `BepInEx/BepInEx#1407` adds both bundle-path fallbacks and a resolved-path fallback for exactly the game-assembly load above, and it was closed unmerged, which is why be.793 still fails on a stock bundle.
- **Probing this costs about a minute per run, and has to stay off the display.** `BepInEx/LogOutput.log` is the success signal; an unhandled preloader exception goes instead to `preloader_<timestamp>.log` **next to the executable inside `Contents/MacOS/`**, so a missing `LogOutput.log` is not by itself a failed injection. Redirect that file with `BEPINEX_PRELOADER_LOG=<absolute path>` to catch what `Entrypoint.Start` swallows. `ChainloaderLogHelper.RewritePreloaderLogs` deletes the preloader file mid-initialization while its handle stays open, so `Debug` lines logged after that point (`Runtime invoke pointer: 0x…`, `Runtime invoke patched`) are written to an unlinked inode and are simply lost — absence of those lines proves nothing. `LogOutput.log` is flushed per line, so trust it over `player.log`, which Unity buffers and can leave at 0 bytes. Launch with `-batchmode -nographics -logFile <path>` so no window is created, and stop with `SIGTERM` rather than `SIGKILL` — killing a windowed player leaves a WindowServer "not responding" sheet on the desktop. A *crashing* player is noisier still: each crash raises a "quit unexpectedly" dialog through `CoreServicesUIAgent`, which stacks, so clear them with `killall CoreServicesUIAgent` after a batch of runs rather than clicking them out. To keep a batch silent from the start, `defaults write com.apple.CrashReporter DialogType none` before it and `defaults delete com.apple.CrashReporter DialogType` afterwards — that domain holds unrelated keys, so delete the key rather than the domain, and read it back to confirm the walk-back. `lldb -p <pid>` cannot be used to inspect the player (attach fails with `lost connection` on the ad-hoc-signed player binary), and there is no `timeout` on macOS, so drive a bounded debugger run through a background job plus `pkill -x lldb`.
- **What a scored run looks like.** `probe_variant.sh <label> <bundle zip> [DATALINK|GALINK|GALINKDIR|DOBBY|PATCH_DATA|ENV=VAL …]` — scratch tooling beside the probe player source rather than a repo script, since it only makes sense next to a locally built player — copies the player and the loader bundle into a per-variant working tree, applies the requested layout fixes, launches translated and headless, waits for a decisive log line, stops with `SIGTERM`, and prints counts for injection, Unity version, metadata version, method count, interop assemblies, `Chainloader initialized`, the by-name fatal, plugin load and detour outcome. Score a variant by which fatal it prints, since each depth names a different defect: `Value cannot be null. (Parameter 'gameDataPath')` at `UnityInfo.Initialize` means the data symlink is missing, `Could not locate Il2Cpp game assembly` means dyld cannot see the dylib, and reaching `Chainloader initialized` with `PROBE: plugin Load ran` missing means the detour never fired.

### Binding the Il2Cpp runtime without a mod loader

`scripts/native_bind_probe/host.c`, driven by `scripts/native_bind_probe.py` (`task re:native-bind`), answers the question the loader work left open: can a plain native process bind the Il2Cpp runtime by exported symbol, with no doorstop, no loader and no detour library in the process? The host is a single C program with no dependency beyond `libdl`: it `dlopen`s a `GameAssembly` binary, resolves `il2cpp_*` by name, initializes a domain, and scores three legs. Each leg prints one `#JSON#` line of measured evidence, so a score is a log line rather than an inference, and a crash prints the same line with `crash_signal` filled in by a fault guard installed for `SIGBUS`/`SIGSEGV`/`SIGILL`/`SIGFPE`/`SIGTRAP`/`SIGABRT`.

**Score.** Legs 1–3 pass on macOS x86_64 under Rosetta against a locally built universal IL2CPP player; legs 1–2 pass on the shipped Linux game build, on the player box, over ssh.

| Leg | What it measures | macOS x86_64 (Rosetta) | Linux x86_64 (shipped build) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | `dlopen` plus exported-symbol binding, no loader present | pass — 343 loaded images, 0 naming a loader, 11 of the counted exports resolved, `il2cpp_init` then a non-null domain | pass — 29 images, 0 naming a loader, 11 exports, corlib `mscorlib.dll` |
| 2 | a managed static method reached by name, returned value printed | pass — 5 of the ladder | pass — 10 of the ladder |
| 3 | a self-authored trampoline on an exported function that still returns correct results | pass — `il2cpp_runtime_invoke` and `il2cpp_method_get_name` | not run (the control leg scores legs 1–2) |

The process is loader-free by measurement, not assumption: `loaded_images` counts every image dyld has mapped and `loader_images` counts those whose path matches any of `BepInEx`, `chainloader`, `MelonLoader`, `Dobby`, `CoreCLR`, `hostfxr`, `hostpolicy`, `mono`, `libil2cpp`, `dotnet`. Both runs report zero, and no window, Steam session or input device is involved, because the host *is* the process — no player is launched.

**Leg 1** is the shape of the whole binding path: `il2cpp_set_commandline_arguments_from_string`, `il2cpp_set_data_dir`, `il2cpp_init("<domain>")`, `il2cpp_thread_attach`, then `il2cpp_domain_get`, `il2cpp_image_get_name(il2cpp_get_corlib())` = `"mscorlib.dll"`, `il2cpp_class_from_name` for `System.Math` (class name reads back `Math`, and `il2cpp_class_get_namespace`/`class_get_name` agree), `il2cpp_object_new` + `il2cpp_object_get_size` = 16, and `il2cpp_method_get_name` = `"Abs"`. `proc_translated=1` on the Rosetta run records that the x86_64 slice really was translated.

**Leg 2** is a ladder rather than one call, because IL2CPP pruning is per-build and a method that exists in `dump.cs` need not survive into a given player. These are the shipped-Linux-build results, all invoked through the exported C API with no Unity player running:

| Method | Image | Returned |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `System.Math.Abs(-7)` | mscorlib.dll | `7` |
| `System.BitConverter.SingleToInt32Bits(1.0)` | mscorlib.dll | `1065353216` |
| `System.Int32.Parse("1234")` | mscorlib.dll | `1234` |
| `System.Convert.ToInt32(3.7)` | mscorlib.dll | `4` |
| `System.String.Concat("mega","bonk")` | mscorlib.dll | `megabonk` |
| `UnityEngine.Mathf.Abs(-5)` | UnityEngine.CoreModule.dll | `5` |
| `Rewired.Utils.MathTools.Abs(-42)` | Rewired_Core.dll | `42` |
| `InventoryUtility.GetWeaponMaxLevel()` | Assembly-CSharp.dll | `40` |
| `ExplorerUtility.IsLinux()` | Assembly-CSharp.dll | `true` |
| `MapController.GetStageIndex()` | Assets.Scripts.Managers, Assembly-CSharp.dll | `0` |

The last four are the ones that matter for a mod: they are game-authored code, in the global or game namespaces, invoked by name and returning real values. Candidates with no declared expectation pass when a value comes back at all, so game constants can be *read* by the probe without knowing them in advance. Overloading is decided by parameter and return types rather than by name and arity: `class_get_method_from_name` alone picks the wrong `Math.Abs` overload, so the host enumerates the class with `il2cpp_class_get_methods` and compares each candidate's `method_get_param`/`method_get_return_type` against the declared types.

**Leg 3** is the detour question, answered without a detour library. Every export in both binaries is a 16-byte-aligned stub — `55 48 89 E5 5D E9 <rel32>` on x86_64 — and the implementation lives far away in `__text`, reachable as `slot + 10 + rel32`. The host copies the 5-byte prologue into a page it allocates read-write and then makes read-execute, appends its own `movabs rax, <impl>; jmp rax`, and only then patches the export itself with a `ff 25` absolute jump to its hook — restoring the page to `r-x` before the first call through it. Baseline, patched and restored calls must agree (`7`, `7`, `7` for `il2cpp_runtime_invoke`; `Abs`, `Abs`, `Abs` for `il2cpp_method_get_name`) and the hook counter must read exactly 1. The arm64 host records `leg3 needs x86_64 absolute-jump patching` and does not count as a pass: patching is scored where Rosetta runs, which is where a macOS mod would run.

**So yes: a mod host can skip the chainloader's `il2cpp_runtime_invoke` trigger.** That trigger is a gate BepInEx imposes on itself — `IL2CPPChainloader` waits for its own `il2cpp_runtime_invoke` detour to fire before it loads plugins — and leg 3 shows a native host both reaching managed code through the exported symbol and diverting that same export with a jump it wrote itself. Nothing in the binding path needs Dobby, funchook or doorstop; the report's own conclusion line is `leg3=pass hook=il2cpp_runtime_invoke hook_calls=1` under `chainloader.by_symbol_invoke_available: true`.

What is *not* exported is worth stating plainly, because it changes how a native mod names its targets. Both `nm -D --defined-only` listings show exactly **240 `il2cpp_*` symbols and zero managed-body symbols**: there is no `il2cpp_Math_Abs_m886194904` to `dlsym`, in the probe player's dylib or in the shipped `GameAssembly.so`. A managed method is therefore reachable only through the C API (`class_from_name` → `class_get_methods` → `runtime_invoke`), and a function pointer to patch comes from the runtime, not from a symbol name.

Four findings cost real time and are the reason the host looks the way it does:

- **`il2cpp_runtime_invoke` returns a pointer into its own frame.** Snapshot the boxed value before anything else touches that stack. A boxed `Int32` holds the klass at +0, the monitor at +8 and the payload at +16, which is what `il2cpp_object_unbox` hands back; reading the box header as the value gives a plausible-looking pointer instead of `7`.
- **`il2cpp_string_chars` returns UTF-16 code units, not a `const char*`.** Read as a C string it yields exactly the first character, so `String.Concat("mega","bonk")` looks like it returned `"m"`. Decode against `il2cpp_string_length`.
- **A NULL namespace faults inside metadata lookup.** `il2cpp_class_from_name(image, NULL, "ExplorerUtility")` segfaults; global-namespace types store `""`, and passing the empty string finds them.
- **Text pages are not writable, and the order of restoration matters.** The sequence is `mprotect` read-write → write the jump → restore read-execute → *then* call. Calling through a page left read-write faults with `SIGBUS` on Apple platforms, which is easy to misread as a Rosetta problem.

The driver is stdlib-only Python and portable enough to run the same probe on the player box: it rsyncs `scripts/` to `~/native-bind-probe`, records the host's own `uname -sm` and `file` output for the binary under test, and re-invokes itself there with plain `python3` (`uv` is not on the non-interactive ssh `PATH`). Linux needs `-lm` at link time, `_GNU_SOURCE` for `sigaction`/`dladdr`, and can `mmap` a page read-write-execute directly where Apple needs the read-write → read-execute dance. `player_argv` and `stop_player` keep the headless contract for launching a probe player (`-batchmode -nographics -logFile`, `SIGTERM` only, never `SIGKILL`) for work that needs the player itself; the scored legs do not use them.

Two caveats keep the macOS number honest. Megabonk ships no macOS build, and none is coming — the game is unmaintained (see "Cost per game update"), so asking upstream for one is a closed question rather than a pending one, and this locally rebuilt probe player is a permanent fixture of the macOS legs rather than a stopgap. That means the macOS legs run against a locally built Unity 2023.2.22f1 IL2CPP **universal** (`x86_64 arm64`) player with its own 2.3 MB metadata, not against the shipped game; the shipped binary is exercised on Linux instead. And that probe player contains no Rewired, no game `Assembly-CSharp` types and no `UnityEngine.Mathf`, so on macOS only the mscorlib candidates of the ladder pass — the game-assembly rows above are Linux results. Refresh the bundle and re-run `task re:native-bind` after changing the probe project; the ladder is the fastest way to see what a given build actually kept.

**Where the probe player comes from.** It is an ordinary Unity player build, not a patched copy of Megabonk, and its project lives outside this repository: a minimal editor project (product name `ProbePlayer`, bundle id `com.probe.ProbePlayer`, company `probe`) built by the Unity Hub editor at `~/Applications/Unity/Hub/Editor/2023.2.22f1` — the same `2023.2.22f1 (6b19bf4f8115)` the game uses, which is what makes its `global-metadata.dat` a version this repo's Il2Cpp metadata reader accepts (`29`, against the shipped bundle's rejected `24.0.0`). Rebuilding it needs that editor with the macOS Build Support module, `Scripting Backend: IL2CPP` and `Architecture: Universal`, because the two things the legs depend on are IL2CPP's choice to emit `il2cpp_*` as Mach-O exports and a `Target Architectures` setting that produces both slices. It is not reproduced by a task: the editor is not installed by this repo's toolchain and a build needs a GUI session. The bundle the driver reports is the copy it rsynced back to `.tools/native-bind/ProbePlayer.app`, so `Contents/Resources/BuildInfo.txt` and `Contents/Resources/Data/ScriptingAssemblies.json` are the checkable record of what was actually probed. Both slices are exercised, because the development host is Apple silicon (`Darwin arm64`, Apple M4, `hw.optional.arm64=1`) and `.tools/native-bind/summary.json` records `host_id: darwin-arm64`. `--arch both` runs each leg twice and the two runs are distinguishable from inside the process: the x86_64 legs report `proc_translated: 1` — that slice runs under Rosetta — and the arm64 legs report `proc_translated: 0`. So the x86_64 column is the Rosetta case and the arm64 column is the native case, and a hooking failure can be attributed to one or the other rather than to macOS in general. `file` reporting both arches would not have been evidence of that; the per-leg `proc_translated` is.

The arm64 column reaches the same place as the x86_64 one and stops one step short. `leg1` passes, `leg2` passes all five mscorlib candidates and fails the same five whose assemblies a probe player lacks (`UnityEngine.CoreModule`, `Rewired_Core`, `Assembly-CSharp`), with `winners.arm64` the same `System.Math::Abs(int)`. `leg3` is the gap, and it is a gap in this repository's code rather than in the architecture: `scripts/native_bind_probe/host.c` guards the trampoline and absolute-jump patcher with `#if defined(__x86_64__)` and the `#else` branch emits `leg3 needs x86_64 absolute-jump patching`. Nothing measured so far says a native arm64 hook cannot work — it says the x86_64 patcher has not been given an `adrp`/`add`/`br` equivalent. Note also that the `dlsym` findings under "Binding the Il2Cpp runtime without a mod loader" were scored twice on this host, once per `--target-arch`, and `.tools/elf-shim-arm64/summary.json` and `.tools/elf-shim-x86_64/summary.json` agree exactly: 303 unique undefined names, 247 resolved by the loader as-is, `resolved_by_underscore_only: 0`. So the no-underscore and `iconv` results are native-arm64 facts, not something Rosetta made look easy.

### Loading and shimming the Linux ELF runtime

`task re:elf-shim` scores the step the whole B-over-C decision hung on. Route C is the fallback "if, and only if, B's ELF-load-and-shim step turns out not to work", and nothing had ever tried to make the shipped x86-64 ELF loadable on macOS. It now has a measured answer, and the kill switch did not fire: **elf-load-and-shim=feasible**, four legs passing against the shipped `GameAssembly.so` on both Apple targets. The driver is `scripts/elf_shim_probe.py` (stdlib only, no ELF library) with `scripts/native_bind_probe/symprobe.c` as the loader oracle; artifacts are `.tools/elf-shim-<arch>/summary.json`.

The design point that makes the numbers mean something is that **nothing is inferred from symbol names**. The driver reads the ELF itself and asks two oracles: the real macOS loader, via `dlsym(RTLD_DEFAULT, …)`, for what a host can actually resolve; and the relocation table, for which imports are *reachable at all*. An import that no relocation names is unreachable, so a shim for it would be theater — which is how the `_ITM_*` and ARM-EH `_ZGTT*` clusters resolve: all ten are weak, unreferenced, and satisfiable by a stub.

| Leg | Question | Measured |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Does every reachable import have a named answer? | 303 unique undefined, all reachable; 221 free, 8 alias, 17 locale-alias, 12 compat, 29 ABI-divergent, 10 weak-noop, 6 loader-owned, **0 unclassified** |
| 2 | Does glibc symbol versioning constrain the shim? | 12 distinct nodes (`GLIBC_2.2.5`→`2.17`, `GCC_3.0`→`4.2.0`); 291 live imports bind to one; none stranded |
| 3 | Can the relocations be translated? | 470,466 in 5 types — 466,320 `RELATIVE`, 2,015 `64`, 1,756 `JUMP_SLOT`, 374 `GLOB_DAT`, 1 `DTPMOD64`; **0 `COPY`, 0 `IRELATIVE`** |
| 4 | Which load strategy, and does it hold on both arches? | Self-relocate 4 `PT_LOAD` segments; 51,319,793 B of code; 16 B TLS; 21 init hooks; 240 `il2cpp_*` exports preserved |

Four findings changed what the notes above assert:

- **Version binding is a fact, not an obstacle — for a different reason than assumed.** The earlier framing was that the versioned nodes are recorded but "none of it binds to imported symbols". It binds heavily: 291 live imports name a node, 258 of them `GLIBC_2.2.5`. What makes it harmless is that version references are a contract with the *Linux* loader. A macOS build resolves imports by name in code we control, and Mach-O has no symbol versioning to satisfy, so the shim never has to fake a node. That is why leg 2 scores "no live versioned import lacks an answer" rather than pretending the bindings are absent.
- **The TLS problem people braced for is one relocation.** There are no `TLSDESC` relocations at all; `PT_TLS` is 16 bytes and there is exactly one `DTPMOD64`. So `__tls_get_addr` is live but trivial: one self-loaded module means one constant module token and a hand-written accessor over a 16-byte block.
- **The two classic `dlopen` blockers are simply not present.** No `COPY` relocations (which would need the link editor to allocate in the host executable) and no `IRELATIVE` (which would run resolvers requiring a working libc before the shim is ready). That absence, not the symbol list, is what makes a hand-written loader plausible.
- **`dlsym` accepts the unadorned ELF name, so the Mach-O underscore is a dyld courtesy.** All 247 resolvable imports resolved under their exact ELF spelling and none needed the underscored form — `dlsym(RTLD_DEFAULT, "malloc")` works and `"_malloc"` does not. A hand-written resolver walking `LC_SYMTAB`/`nlist` would not get that for free, so this is a reason to resolve through `dlsym` rather than a property of the translation.

Two more are worth their weight because they were only visible by measurement. `iconv_open` and `iconv_close` are absent from the default `dlsym` scope but present in `/usr/lib/libiconv.2.dylib`, so they need a `dlopen` and a forward — and the probe is built to tell that apart from a free symbol: **every `RTLD_DEFAULT` lookup runs before any `dlopen`**, because `dlopen` puts symbols in the global scope and would otherwise make a "just dlopen it" claim indistinguishable from a free one. The gettext family (`gettext`, `dgettext`, `bindtextdomain`, `bind_textdomain_codeset`) has no macOS counterpart at all; a passthrough is valid *only* while no `.mo` catalog is opened, and that has not been checked.

The arm64 half is scored by the same driver with `--target-arch arm64` on this Apple silicon host. All four `PT_LOAD` segments have `p_vaddr == p_offset` and 4 KB alignment, so they are congruent modulo both 4 KB and 16 KB pages and a self-loader can map them rather than copying. On x86_64 all four are directly page-aligned; on a 16 KB-page host only two are, and the other two need the standard map-down-aligned-then-advance step. That is an inconvenience, not a wall, and unlike everything else in this section it is a native arm64 measurement rather than an extrapolation from an x86_64 run.

What this does **not** prove, stated plainly: nothing was launched, so no claim is made that the runtime boots, that the 29 ABI-divergent shims behave correctly (their entries say what each one has to reconcile — `sigaction`'s `sa_restorer`, `struct termios`'s extra speeds, Linux `ioctl` request numbers), that the gettext passthrough is safe, or anything about performance. The 66 shim symbols are the work estimate, not a working shim, and the 466,320 `RELATIVE` fixups are an 11 MB relocation pass a loader has to apply correctly before `DT_INIT` and the 21 `DT_INIT_ARRAY` hooks run, with `PT_GNU_RELRO` then made read-only. A run with `--skip-dyld-probe` scores the ELF structure only and is labeled `not-scored` rather than `feasible`, because the import legs need the live loader.

### Lifting the shipped code to arm64

`task re:lift-probe` scores whether the shipped x86-64 il2cpp code can be lifted to something a compiler rebuilds for arm64, on a sample instead of a citation. The game code is the 46.8 MB `il2cpp` section of the Linux `GameAssembly.so`, which carries no symbols, so function bounds come from `.eh_frame`: 196,137 bodies, of which 75,845 are tiny (63 B or less), 100,919 small (up to 512 B) and 19,373 large. The driver draws the same number from each class with a fixed seed, dumps raw per-instruction pcode and the decompiler-recovered jump tables through headless Ghidra with auto-analysis off (`scripts/lift_probe/LiftDump.java`), translates each body to LLVM IR with `scripts/pcode_lift.py`, and scores four stages per body: fully decoded, lifted with no hole, accepted by the LLVM verifier, compiled to an arm64 object with `llc -O2`. A hole is a pcode op the emitter cannot translate; it is recorded, never skipped, so a clean body means every op lifted. Ghidra and the keg-only brew LLVM are found by path; the Ghidra project path must not contain a dot-prefixed directory, which is why the driver keeps it in a temporary directory.

Result on 3,000 bodies (1,000 per class): all 3,000 decode, all 3,000 pass the verifier, all 3,000 compile for arm64, and 2,978 (99.3%) lift with no hole (986 tiny, 992 small and 1,000 large). The 22 bodies with holes are 21 with a computed jump that has no recovered table (33 sites) and 1 with a jump-table case outside the body, so on that draw every hole was a control-flow gap rather than an instruction: no body carried an operation the emitter has no semantics for. The larger samples below found two such operations, both SSE. 193 bodies are indirect tail calls recognized by a straight-line approximation (a `BRANCHIND` whose target traces back through copies to a memory load), so a jump table the decompiler failed to recover could be classified as a tail call without any error. Every constant address resolved to a section symbol. The whole `il2cpp` section disassembles with no undecodable bytes: 11,773,906 instructions, 298 distinct mnemonics, no x87, no AVX, no `%fs:` thread-local access, 18,986 indirect jumps and 79,458 indirect calls. The instruction set is plain; the hard part is not decoding. The census is per section: the statically linked code in `.text` does carry x87, measured below.

**Reuse pcode when comparing optimization pipelines.** `--pcode-cache <path>` persists Ghidra's raw bodies and nonempty ELF section table in one versioned JSONL file. Reuse requires the input binary's SHA-256, exact ordered body addresses and sizes, dump-script fingerprint and Ghidra version to match. A partial, malformed or stale sample is dumped again in full, in one Ghidra invocation: analysing only missing bodies can change jump-table recovery. Missing bodies remain in the scoring totals. Keep each pipeline in a separate output directory and never overwrite published baselines.

**Optimization pipelines.** The default empty `--pipeline` keeps the original `opt -passes=verify` followed by `llc -O2`. `--pipeline 'default<O2>'` inserts the selected passes and a second explicit verifier before `llc`. The corresponding task variable is `LIFT_PIPELINE='default<O2>'`; quote shell redirection characters. Rejections retain stderr and body addresses and never fall back to compiling unoptimized IR.

| Pipeline | Verifier | arm64 object | Object bytes | Bytes per x86 byte | Scoring s | Whole-run s |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| empty (default, verify then `llc -O2`) | 3,000 | 3,000 | 14,014,640 | **9.12** | 45.1 | 52.6 |
| `default<O1>` | 3,000 | 3,000 | 9,495,144 | **6.18** | 61.5 | 69.1 |
| `default<O2>` | 3,000 | 3,000 | 9,351,080 | **6.08** | 61.0 | 68.6 |
| `default<O3>` | 3,000 | 3,000 | 9,351,112 | **6.08** | 60.1 | 67.7 |

The comparison uses seed 80, 1,000 bodies per class (3,000 bodies, 1,537,467 x86 bytes), binary SHA-256 `a86fd434…5ba1`, Ghidra 12.1.4, and one shared dump with 30 sections and 3,000 cache hits per run. `scoring_seconds` measures the parallel lift/verify/optimize/compile stage, not Ghidra or ELF inspection. New summaries also record `pcode_seconds` (dump or warm-cache lookup), `wall_seconds`, raw object bytes, full sampled x86 bytes and successful-object x86 bytes. The original cold cache-build run recorded 39.4 s for scoring but did not record Ghidra wall time; there is no measured cold-dump time to compare. The original census above remains verify-only plus `llc -O2`, not opt-O2.

These four sequential reruns use Homebrew LLVM 23.1.3 and the default eight scoring workers; whole-run times include CLI startup and ELF inspection, measured around each invocation. Evidence is under `.tools/lift-probe-opt-{none,o1,o2,o3}-verified/{summary.json,bodies.jsonl,execution.json}`. Every run requests and scores all 3,000 bodies; the table divides raw object-byte sums by the same full-sample denominator. O2 is the smallest by 32 bytes over O3, a negligible difference; both recover about a third of the verify-only size. Timings are single-run measurements, not evidence that O3 is consistently faster.

To reproduce O2 without replacing existing results:

```sh
task re:lift-probe LIFT_PER_CLASS=1000 LIFT_SEED=80 \
  LIFT_PIPELINE='default<O2>' LIFT_OUT=.tools/lift-probe-opt-o2-new \
  LIFT_EXTRA='--r2-per-class 0 --pcode-cache .tools/lift-probe-opt-cache.jsonl'
```

Rejected experiments are not full-sample size comparisons:

- `sroa,mem2reg,newgvn,instcombine,dce`: 2,995 objects, 9,550,712 bytes over 1,533,955 successful-body x86 bytes (6.23), scoring 76.2 s. Rejections: `0x35f49a0` (960 B), `0x38ceb00` (893 B), `0x477d970` (116 B), `0x47815d0` (84 B), `0x4987580` (1,459 B).
- `function(sroa,mem2reg,instcombine,dce)`: 2,995 objects, 9,683,472 bytes over 1,526,940 successful-body x86 bytes (6.34), scoring 203.5 s. Rejections: `0x35f49a0` (960 B), `0x38c0ac0` (590 B), `0x38ceb00` (893 B), `0x38d44d0` (520 B), `0x3c265d0` (7,564 B).

Re-executing all ten rejected bodies preserves the exact stderr: `Instruction Combining ... did not reach a fixpoint after 1 iterations`. These are optimization-stage rejections, not verifier or backend failures. The original modules verify, but rejected optimized bodies never reach `llc`. No verifier or fixpoint checks were disabled. This LLVM also rejects `default<Os>` as unsupported for all 3,000 requested bodies; the saved rows identify every address. Applying `default<O2>` to the differential oracle still exports every lifted function, produces a smaller object, and matches native arm64 (`bad=0`); verification explicitly runs before and after the production optimization helper.

That draw is one draw, and a construct that misses it is invisible in it. Two further samples take 5,000 bodies from each class instead of 1,000, 15,000 bodies per run, drawn with `GHIDRA_HEADLESS_MAXMEM=8G task re:lift-probe LIFT_PER_CLASS=5000 LIFT_SEED=<seed> LIFT_OUT=.tools/lift-probe-<seed> LIFT_EXTRA="--r2-per-class 0 --tool-timeout 600"` for seeds 81 and 82 against the same `GameAssembly.so` (`a86fd434…5ba1`): a dump that size needs more of the headless JVM's heap than the 6G default, and the largest bodies need more than the 120 s a single `opt` or `llc` run gets by default. Together with seed 80 the three runs cover 30,503 distinct bodies, a sixth of the section. The driver now pairs the sample it asked for with what Ghidra emitted and records any body the dump never reached against its address, so a run that dropped bodies would report them rather than score the survivors and call it the sample: both new runs asked for 15,000, scored 15,000, and report no failures and no unsampled rows. The seed-80 row predates that pairing and carries no failure record of its own; its 3,000 scored rows are the 3,000 it asked for, which is the same statement made by hand.

| Seed | Bodies | Decoded | No hole | Verifier | arm64 object | No hole, tiny | No hole, small | No hole, large |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 80 | 3,000 | 3,000 | 2,978 (99.27%) | 3,000 | 3,000 | 986/1,000 (98.60%) | 992/1,000 (99.20%) | 1,000/1,000 (100.00%) |
| 81 | 15,000 | 15,000 | 14,838 (98.92%) | 15,000 | 15,000 | 4,899/5,000 (97.98%) | 4,954/5,000 (99.08%) | 4,985/5,000 (99.70%) |
| 82 | 15,000 | 15,000 | 14,850 (99.00%) | 15,000 | 15,000 | 4,926/5,000 (98.52%) | 4,946/5,000 (98.92%) | 4,978/5,000 (99.56%) |

Decoding, the verifier and the arm64 compile are still unanimous, and the no-hole figure holds to about half a point, between 98.9% and 99.3%. What the bigger sample changes is the large class: seed 80 found no hole at all in its 1,000 large bodies, where 5,000 finds 15 and 22. Per class the hole rate is 1.4% to 2.0% of tiny, 0.8% to 1.1% of small and 0% to 0.44% of large, and four kinds account for every hole in all three runs: the two the first draw showed, and two it did not.

| Hole kind (bodies carrying it) | seed 80 | seed 81 baseline | seed 82 baseline | seed 81 with sign-mask lifting | seed 82 with sign-mask lifting | seed 81 with word-shuffle lifting | seed 82 with word-shuffle lifting |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `BRANCHIND` with no recovered table | 21 | 152 | 144 | 152 | 144 | 152 | 144 |
| `JUMPTABLE_TARGET`, a case outside the body | 1 | 2 | 3 | 2 | 3 | 2 | 3 |
| `CALLOTHER:movmskps` | 0 | 7 | 2 | **0** | **0** | **0** | **0** |
| `CALLOTHER:pshuflw` | 0 | 1 | 1 | 1 | 1 | **0** | **0** |

The last two are the kinds the seed-80 draw never showed. `movmskps`, which writes the four float sign bits into a general-purpose register, appears in 7 bodies in seed 81 and 2 in seed 82: `0x4906740` at 43 B is the smallest carrying it, and the largest, `0x48da5c0` at 45,834 B, was drawn by both seeds. `pshuflw`, which permutes the four low-order words and copies the source's upper 64 bits unchanged, appears once per seed, at `0x3ab7dc0` and `0x42f4cb0`, both 513 B. Both were holes in the baseline; the emitter now handles both operations. Everything else scales with the sample rather than moving — indirect tail calls in 6.4%, 6.5% and 6.9% of bodies, an indirect call in 30.1% to 30.6%, no unmapped constant reference in any run, and arm64 objects at 9.1 to 9.3 times the x86 bytes.

Full reruns with sign-mask lifting use the same 5,000 bodies per class, seeds, binary and options, writing to `.tools/lift-probe-movmskps-{81,82}` without replacing the baselines. Each requests and scores all 15,000 bodies, all decode, verify and compile for arm64, and neither reports a failure or missing body. Seed 81 is now 14,845/15,000 hole-free (98.97%; 4,900 tiny, 4,956 small, 4,989 large), and seed 82 is 14,852/15,000 (99.01%; 4,926 tiny, 4,946 small, 4,980 large). Comparing every sampled address, size and hole list against its baseline shows exactly the seven and two `movmskps` carriers lose that hole, with every other hole list unchanged.

Full reruns with word-shuffle lifting use the same settings in `.tools/lift-probe-pshuflw-{81,82}`. Each requests and scores all 15,000 bodies; all decode, verify and compile for arm64, with no failure or missing body. Seed 81 is 14,846/15,000 hole-free (98.97%; 4,900 tiny, 4,956 small, 4,990 large), and seed 82 is 14,853/15,000 (99.02%; 4,926 tiny, 4,946 small, 4,981 large). Comparing every address, size and hole list against the sign-mask reruns finds exactly one changed body per seed: `0x3ab7dc0` and `0x42f4cb0` lose `CALLOTHER:pshuflw`, with every other hole list unchanged. Only the two control-flow hole kinds remain in these samples.

**Lifting a `BRANCHIND` whose stack is proven balanced.** The remaining control-flow holes are almost all the indirect tail call, a function pointer read from a fixed slot and jumped to after the frame is torn down. A `jmp reg` with `rsp` at its entry value is `call reg; ret` only when the target is another function, and a frameless function with an unrecovered switch also has `rsp` at entry, so balance alone is not enough. The emitter lowers the jump as a call and a return in two cases: the straight-line rule it always had (the target traces back through copies to a `LOAD`), or `rsp` proven at its entry value on every path into the site *and* the target read from memory, a `LOAD` or a `COPY` from a fixed slot. A computed target (arithmetic on a loaded value) or one with no definition in the body stays a recorded hole whatever `rsp` does. The pass tracks the net `rsp` change per op, treats a `CALL` as net zero, and joins the lattice {balanced, off-by-a-constant, untrackable} across successors; an inconsistent path or an untrackable `rsp` (a write the pass cannot follow, or a branch whose target the dump never emitted) is never proven. The original rule is kept as a floor, so the proof only ever adds lifted sites. Bodies whose frame is restored through `leave` or `lea rsp` (a `COPY` of `rsp` from a register) stay untrackable holes: the pass follows arithmetic on `rsp`, not an opaque assignment.

`scripts/branchind_report.py` reads a run's `bodies.jsonl` and writes `branchind.json` beside it with the proven and unproven census and one reason per unproven site. "Unproven" covers every site the proof did not license, including sites the original load rule lifted without a proof, so it is larger than the hole count. Measured on the seed-81 and seed-82 draws (5,000 bodies per class, same binary, cache reused so only scoring reruns) in `.tools/lift-probe-tailcall4-{81,82}`:

| Seed | Sites proven | Unproven sites | Unproven sites in bodies that keep a hole | `BRANCHIND` hole bodies | Hole-free bodies |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 81 | 922 | 318 | 17 | 152 to 11 | 14,846 to 14,987 |
| 82 | 860 | 427 | 39 | 144 to 26 | 14,853 to 14,971 |

Comparing every address, size and hole list against the sign-mask-and-word-shuffle reruns (`.tools/lift-probe-pshuflw-{81,82}`) finds the only changed bodies are `BRANCHIND` carriers that lose the hole, 141 and 118 of them, with every other hole list and every address and size unchanged. All 15,000 bodies per seed still decode, verify and compile for arm64. Every newly lifted site reads its target from a fixed slot; none is switch-shaped. The memory-read requirement costs nothing on these draws: a run without it lifts the same bodies, so it is a guard on the rule, not a measured loss.
