A `leave`-style frame, a computed target or a jump whose body hangs on an undecoded branch remain holes, 11 and 26 bodies here.

`CALLOTHER` is the one opcode the emitter gives semantics to selectively, and only for a shape it can check. Ghidra folds the packed SSE arithmetic into a single `CALLOTHER` carrying the SSE mnemonic id as its first input (`97` and `96` for `divps` and `divpd`, `9d` and `9b` for `minps` and `maxps`, as the varnode prints them) and the mnemonic name in `user`, with the destination written twice, as `out` and again as `in[1]`, and the other operand as `in[2]`. The emitter lifts one only when `out`, `in[1]` and `in[2]` are all 16 bytes and `in[1]` is the same varnode as `out`; anything else, including an unmatched width or a destination that is not also the first source, is left as a hole rather than guessed at. Both operands are read as `i128`, split into four 32-bit lanes or two 64-bit lanes, each lane pair handled in turn, and the results stitched with shifts and `or`s into the single `store i128` that writes the destination. For `divps` and `divpd` each pair is bitcast to `float` or `double` and one `fdiv` per lane follows, which is what x86 does lane by lane, so no special case needs spelling out.

`minps` and `maxps` do not lower that way. Per lane x86 returns the first source only while it is strictly less than the second (strictly greater for `maxps`), so the second source is what comes back when either lane is NaN and when both lanes are zeros of either sign. `llvm.minnum` and `llvm.maxnum` return whichever operand is not NaN and leave the choice between two zeros unspecified, so they can disagree with x86 on a NaN payload and on the sign of a zero. The emitter compares each lane with `fcmp olt` (respectively `fcmp ogt`) and `select`s between the two lanes' bit patterns as integers, so the winner is copied unchanged. Selecting on the floats instead would be a candidate for the optimizer to rewrite into a floating-point max, which does not carry those rules. `minss`, `maxss`, `minpd` and `maxpd` have not appeared in a sampled body, so they carry no id here and stay holes with their name recorded, like any other userop. The rule those tests assert is not taken on faith either: the same cases are run against the instruction itself, compiled for x86-64 and executed under Rosetta, before the lifted side is held to it.

`movmskps` has its own shape guard rather than weakening the packed-arithmetic guard. Re-dumping the seven distinct carriers from seeds 81 and 82 finds eight sites, all with userop id `a1`, a 4-byte register destination repeated as `in[1]`, and one 16-byte XMM source as `in[2]`. Ghidra follows that `CALLOTHER` with a separate `INT_ZEXT` into the full 8-byte register. The emitter reads the source as `i128`, extracts bit 31 of each 32-bit lane with logical shifts, and ORs those bits into positions 0 through 3 of an `i32` result; the upper 28 bits are zero. It writes exactly four bytes, leaving the following pcode op to perform the architectural zero extension. NaN payloads and signed zeros require no floating-point operations: only their raw sign bits matter. Unit tests run every sign mask across zeros, quiet/signaling NaNs, finite values, infinities and subnormals against an explicit bit reference, and check neighboring register bytes and the source stay unchanged. The differential fixture uses `_mm_movemask_ps` on x86-64 and an integer-bit reference on arm64, with 2,000 inputs including all sign masks of zeros and both NaN kinds; the reference is also checked against the x86 instruction under Rosetta. Both plain and O2-optimized lifted arm64 match.

`pshuflw` adds a fourth input to the packed shape: a constant immediate. Both shipped carriers emit userop id `bb`, destination XMM0 repeated as the first source, XMM0 as the second source, and `const,e0,8` as the selector. The guard requires exactly four inputs, the existing packed-width/destination checks, and a constant selector in 0 through 255; other shapes remain named holes. The emitter reads only the second source, folds each two-bit selector field into a low-word lane index, and copies words 4 through 7 from that same source before one `store i128`. Tests execute all 256 selectors against an explicit word reference with both aliased and distinct source/destination registers, nonzero upper halves, and checks that neighboring bytes and the separate source are unchanged. The differential fixture uses `_mm_shufflelo_epi16` with `0xe0` (including a duplicated selection), asserts the x86 build emits `pshuflw`, and compares all 128 result bits on 2,000 inputs against the arm64 word reference, both before and after O2. A separate Rosetta run checks that reference against the x86 instruction.

`task re:lift-holes`, after a `task re:lift-probe` run, names what those computed jumps are. It reads the probe's hole rows, re-dumps their pcode through Ghidra, walks each `BRANCHIND` target back through the ops that produced it, and reads a category off the result, recording as evidence the x86 text from `llvm-objdump`, the traced pcode, the section the target was loaded from, any `R_X86_64_RELATIVE` on that slot, and the jump tables the decompiler recovered. It changes no lifting and does not touch `scripts/pcode_lift.py`; `jump_holes.jsonl` keeps one row per body with every site's evidence, so a verdict can be re-checked by hand. The categories are unrecovered switch, indirect tail call, indirect call target, and other.

On the same sample, 22 bodies and 34 indirect jump sites:

| Category | Bodies | Sites | Identifying pattern |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| indirect tail call | 21 | 33 | jump through a register loaded from a fixed `_bss` slot, with frame teardown immediately before it |
| unrecovered switch | 1 | 1 | compare against an immediate and an above/below jump, then a table load at a constant `_rodata` base plus a scaled index |
| indirect call target | 0 | 0 | jump through a slot carrying `R_X86_64_RELATIVE` into executable code; none in this sample |
| other | 0 | 0 | |

All 21 tail-call bodies are the same construction, the il2cpp lazily-resolved invoke thunk: the function pointer lives in a global, one shared resolver fills it on first use, and the body ends by jumping through it.

```text
mov  rax, qword ptr [rip + slot]   # slot in .bss; 0x53648e0-0x5369790 across these 21
test rax, rax
je   slow
     <restore registers>
     jmp rax                        # the site
slow:
     lea  rdi, [rip + metadata]
     call 0x2056380                 # the shared resolver, the same target in all 21
     mov  qword ptr [rip + slot], rax
     <restore registers>
     jmp rax                        # the site on the cold path
```

In pcode the shape is `COPY reg <- ram,slot,8` and then `BRANCHIND reg`: Ghidra writes a fixed-address read as a copy out of the `ram` space rather than as a `LOAD`, so a rule over raw pcode has to match both. The slot sits in a non-executable section and carries no relocation, and of the file's 470,439 relocation rows none lands in `.bss` at all, so the value is written at run time and nothing static can name the target. Lifting it as an indirect jump through memory is already correct, which is what a rule here would have to preserve: match a target loaded from a fixed non-executable unrelocated slot with the jump at the end of the body, emit the indirect jump, and stop recording the body as having a hole.

The one switch is a genuine position-independent switch: `cmp rcx, 0x8`, `ja <default>`, `lea rdx, [rip + ...] # 0xbfd278`, `movsxd rsi, dword ptr [rdx + 4*rcx]`, `add rsi, rdx`, `jmp rsi`, a table of self-relative 32-bit cases in `_rodata` indexed by a scaled value with the bounds check ahead of it. The decompiler recovered the table and all ten of its cases, and one case lands past the end of the `.eh_frame` record the body was cut from, so what reads as a hole there is a short body boundary rather than a switch that cannot be recovered.

No site took indirect call target, the category reserved for a jump through a statically relocated pointer, the shape of a GOT- or vtable-style tail call. Its being empty is the useful part: none of the 34 sites has a target the emitter could have resolved statically, so every one of them is a hole rather than a missed direct branch. The categories stay structural rather than proven, because the decompiler's own table recovery is the only oracle available; "unrecovered switch" means the shape says switch and the recovered table, where there is one, says the same.

What the tail-call rule gets right is a separate question from what the hole rule gets right, and `task re:lift-tail-calls` asks it of the 193 bodies the probe counted as indirect tail calls. It re-dumps those bodies through Ghidra (`scripts/lift_tail_calls.py`, writing `.tools/lift-probe/tail_calls.jsonl` and `tail_calls_summary.json`), asks the emitter's own rule at every `BRANCHIND` site — `BodyLifter.tables` and `BodyLifter.defined_by_load`, the code the lift actually uses rather than a copy of it — and sets it against what the same decompile pass recovered. `LiftDump.java` emits three more keys beside `jt` for this: `sw` for a `switch` in the decompiled C, `dc` for a decompile that completed, and `uw` for the addresses its `Could not recover jumptable at 0x…` warnings name. The verdict is per body: agrees, disagrees, mixed, no tail call, switch beyond body, or not decompiled. It records only; the rule in `scripts/pcode_lift.py` is untouched.

Result on the same 193 bodies and their 198 indirect jump sites: **186 agree, 0 disagree, 4 are mixed, 3 are switch beyond body, 0 have no tail call left and 0 failed to decompile.**

| Verdict | Bodies | What the decompiler said |
| --- | --- | --- |
| agrees | 186 | no table and no `switch` attributable to this body |
| mixed | 4 | a table recovered inside the body at one site, a load-defined tail jump at another |
| switch beyond body | 3 | a `switch` in the C, but no recovered table address or case falls inside the body |
| disagrees | 0 | a table at a site the emitter tail-jumps |

Mixed are the four bodies `0x32a1690`, `0x3a98000`, `0x4423a90` and `0x4037a70`; switch beyond body are `0x405b3f0`, `0x270bb60` and `0x3c7fda0`. Each mixed body carries both constructions at once, and the emitter routes them differently, correctly:

```text
switch (recovered by the decompiler)     lea rcx, [rip + table] ; movsxd rax, dword ptr [rcx + 4*rax] ; add rax, rcx ; jmp rax
  LOAD u4 <- const,1b1,4, u9400,8 | INT_SEXT r <- u4 | INT_ADD r <- r, r | BRANCHIND r
tail call (no table, and none expected)  mov rcx, qword ptr [rax + 0x168] ; … ; pop rbx ; pop r14 ; jmp rcx
  LOAD u8 <- const,1b1,4, <base> | COPY reg <- u8 | BRANCHIND reg
```

The two things the audit settled are both about what the decompiler is and is not qualified to say. Its table recovery is a real second opinion and it never contradicts the emitter: the emitter reads that same recovered-table list and prefers it, so a table at a tail-call site is impossible by construction, and across 198 sites the decompiler produced one inside the body only where the emitter also saw a switch. Its warning is not. `Could not recover jumptable at 0x… Treating indirect jump as call` fires at **190 of the 198 sites**, because that is what the decompiler prints for any indirect jump whose target set it cannot enumerate, which an unconstrained vtable load is. It distinguishes nothing, so it is recorded per body and never decides a verdict.

The other trap is scope. The function the decompiler answers is not the `.eh_frame` record the body was cut from: in the three switch-beyond-body cases the decompiled C holds a `switch` while every recovered table sits outside the bounds — 3 tables and 44 cases for `0x405b3f0`, 45 and 180 for `0x270bb60`, 2 and 34 for `0x3c7fda0`, in every case with no address and no case inside — and reading the C by hand confirms the keyword belongs to that wider scope, with case targets below the body start and the body's own two `jmp`s appearing as calls through `[obj + 0x168]`. So tables and cases are counted twice, in the dump and inside the body, and a bare `switch` keyword with nothing inside the body to anchor it is its own verdict rather than a contradiction.

Nothing in the sample is a misclassification, and the population says why the rule survives contact with the binary: every one of the 198 sites traces `BRANCHIND <- COPY <- LOAD` and takes its target from a register-relative fixed offset (198 of 198), with 161 preceded by frame teardown and only 5 carrying a bounds check. A compiler jump table reads through a scaled index off a constant section base; where the shape census does report a scaled index (61 sites) it is inside the traced chain of an interface dispatch such as `mov rcx, [rax] ; mov rdx, [rcx + 0x168] ; jmp rdx`, with the base a data-segment offset like `0x138` or `0x168`, not a table. The rule's failure mode is real but unexercised: it is the difference between `[base + index*8]` and `[obj + offset]`, and this code puts every tail call on the second side of it. Note also that this population is disjoint from the hole census above: those 22 bodies jump through a slot Ghidra writes as `COPY reg <- ram,slot,8`, which the emitter refuses to trace rather than calling a tail call.

How much of the section actually uses exception handling is `task re:eh-probe`, which reads the same `.eh_frame` bounds as the lift probe with a stdlib parser (`scripts/eh_probe.py`, writing `.tools/eh-probe/summary.json`) and follows each FDE's LSDA pointer into `.gcc_except_table`. It reads bytes only and loads nothing, which is why the personality routine is reported as the slot its encoded value names rather than as a function. Of the 196,137 bodies, **8,373 (4.3%) carry an LSDA**, and all 8,373 point inside `.gcc_except_table` (1,110,448 B at `0xfba378`); each body has its own table, so distinct tables and bodies are the same count. The presence is concentrated by size: tiny **0 of 75,845**, small **2,642 of 100,919 (2.6%)**, large **5,731 of 19,373 (29.6%)**. Among the bodies that have one, no body has a single pad: **2,795 have two to four** distinct landing pads and **5,578 have five or more**. Across all of them, 144,668 call-site entries, 103,628 of which name a landing pad, resolving to 84,257 distinct pads; the largest body has 931. So a 3,000-body stratified sample should contain roughly 322 LSDA-bearing bodies (none tiny, about 26 small, about 296 large) — the lift probe reports no exception numbers at all, so "all 3,000 compile" says nothing about them either way.

Two things make those numbers worth acting on rather than filing. Exceptions are not a stall for the 95.7% of bodies that have no table, but for the 4.3% that do the emitter lifts none of the table, so such a body compiles and cannot unwind: a throw inside it finds no landing pad and the process dies instead of catching. And because 67% of the affected bodies need five or more landing pads, a partial implementation that handles one pad per body covers none of them. Whether a body whose table has a nonzero TType encoding also calls a shared runtime helper is unmeasured: resolving those pointers needs the image loaded, and this probe deliberately does not load it.

The format has four traps that cost real time, all now pinned by `tests/test_eh_probe.py`. An FDE's `pc_begin` and range are both the code pointer's own fixed-width form named by the CIE's `R` augmentation — **not** the ULEB128 range `.debug_frame` uses, and reading the range as ULEB desynchronizes every record after it. An FDE's CIE pointer is *backward*: the CIE sits at `entry + 4 - field`. The CIE's return-address register is one byte. And the LSDA header is LPStart encoding (plus an encoded value when it is not omitted), TType encoding plus a **ULEB offset** rather than an encoded pointer, then the call-site encoding and a ULEB **byte length** that bounds the table; every entry carries a fourth, ULEB action field. The length is authoritative and there is no terminator, so an all-zero entry is legal data. Note also that the file has two `.eh_frame`-named sections: the 10,950,964 B one holding the records, and `.eh_frame_hdr`, which is the binary-search table and is not the frame data.

Three CIEs exist. `0x0` is `"zR"` with 249,677 FDEs and no LSDA encoding, so its bodies cannot have tables. Two are `"zPLR"` with personality encoding `0x9b` and LSDA encoding `0x1b`, at `0x9b9dd8` (9,424 FDEs) and `0xa71910` (0 FDEs, so it is unreferenced: dead code, or its FDEs live outside the file). Exactly 8,373 of the 9,424 describe a body in the census section.

The census agrees with `llvm-dwarfdump --eh-frame` on every number they share: the 196,137 bodies and their 75,845 / 100,919 / 19,373 size split, all 9,424 LSDA addresses, and the 8,373 in-section FDEs belonging to an LSDA-bearing CIE. On its own terms the table data is consistent too: all 103,628 landing-pad entries land strictly inside their own body, no call-site table has an out-of-order start address, and all 9,424 tables end exactly at their declared length.

What those 466,320 base-relative fixups point at is `task re:reloc-probe` (`scripts/reloc_probe.py`, writing `.tools/reloc-probe/summary.json`). Every `R_X86_64_RELATIVE` record sits in `.rela.dyn` — 466,320 of the file's 470,466 relocations, the other 4,146 being `64`, `JUMP_SLOT`, `GLOB_DAT` and one `DTPMOD64` — and a shared object links at base zero, so a record's addend is the address it installs and therefore the address to classify. Function bounds are the `.eh_frame` records above taken whole-file rather than per section: 259,101 FDEs fold to 259,095 bodies with no two overlapping, and 196,137 of them lie in `il2cpp`, which is the exception census's count exactly. The probe prints the total against the figure documented here and asserts that the buckets account for every relocation.

| Bucket | Count | Share | Where the targets are |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| function entry | 247,358 | 53.0% | `il2cpp` 195,449, `.text` 51,909 |
| function interior | 0 | 0.0% | none |
| read-only data | 38,850 | 8.3% | `.rodata` |
| writable data | 180,093 | 38.6% | `.data` 142,261, `.data.rel.ro` 37,832 |
| bss | 18 | 0.0% | `.bss` |
| other loaded memory | 1 | 0.0% | `.plt` |
| outside every section | 0 | 0.0% | none |

Read the other way, by the section holding the slot the relocation writes: `.data.rel.ro` 243,666, `.data` 167,996, `.got` 54,631, `.init_array` 21 (the same hooks the load-strategy leg counts), `.ctors` 6. No slot falls outside a named section. The 466,320 rows name 409,941 distinct addresses, so 56,379 of them are pointers more than one place already holds.

Two findings carry the weight. No relative relocation points inside a function body: every one of the 247,358 code targets is an exact `.eh_frame` body start, so nothing static takes the address of a label partway through a body and no lifted function needs an extra entry point. That is a claim about the binary, not the classifier, so `tests/test_reloc_probe.py` also shows containment live on a shipped body — the same address map calls a body's first byte an entry and a byte 32 in, and its last byte, an interior. The exceptions are the single `other loaded memory` row, a `.got` slot at `0x50d7278` holding `0x4e26440`, the first byte of `.plt`: a pointer to a linker stub, which has no body in the frame table to name. And the entry bucket is checked against the bytes rather than only against the frame table: the 247,358 entries open on 47 distinct first bytes with none of them zero padding, led by `41` (REX.B, 59,677), `55` (`push rbp`, 53,546), `53` (`push rbx`, 37,388) and `48` (REX.W, 35,298) — the il2cpp prologue shapes, and every byte the census found is one an instruction can start with.

So rewriting those pointers is two jobs, not one. The 53% that name an entry are mechanical: each becomes a reference to a lifted symbol at a known address. The other 47% name memory the code lift never touches — 180,093 writable slots, 38,850 read-only ones and 18 into zero-filled `.bss` — so a loader or a rebuilt image has to reproduce those sections and relocate the pointers inside them too, and the one `.plt` pointer needs a callable stub rather than a function.

Four properties of raw pcode matter to anything else that reads it. A `LOAD` carries its space id in the first operand and the pointer in the second, so treating `in[0]` as the address reports a space, not a location. A rip-relative `lea` deposits its result in the `cptr` space, which holds a computed value rather than a read: `COPY reg <- ram,slot,8` is a fetch from memory and `COPY reg <- cptr,addr,8` is that address itself, so code looking for a load has to name `ram` and not any memory-like space. A wide temporary is read back piece by piece: Ghidra copies all 16 bytes of a `unique` temp and then reads `unique,X+4`, `unique,X+8` and `unique,X+12` as four 4-byte varnodes of it, so an offset in the unique space is an address and not an identity, and a translator that gives every distinct offset its own slot reads back uninitialized bytes. And a branch-arm count is not evidence of a switch — one thunk has seven distinct in-body branch targets, being a chain of null-pointer guards with its resolver path duplicated — so the bounds check that separates a switch from a thunk has to come from the x86 text: a compare against an immediate followed by `ja`, `jae`, `jb` or `jbe`. None of the 33 tail-call sites has one, and the switch has it ahead of its table load.

Correctness is checked by `tests/test_pcode_lift_differential.py`, which compiles nineteen freestanding C functions to an x86-64 ELF (loops, signed and unsigned division, popcount, overflow checks, a jump-table `switch`, double arithmetic with constants in `.rodata`, packed float and double division with the divisor in a register and in memory, packed float minimum and maximum, and truncating float-to-int conversions from both float and double to both a 32- and a 64-bit destination), lifts them through the same Ghidra and emitter path, rebuilds them for arm64 and compares them with the same C compiled natively for arm64 on 2,000 pseudo-random inputs each. Vector results are compared lane by lane, with lanes drawn to include zero, signed zero, infinity and both kinds of NaN, and two NaNs count as matching because a quietened payload is not the contract. Packed min/max is the exception among the vector functions: those return one of their operands unchanged rather than computing a value, so their lanes are compared bit for bit, NaN payload and zero sign included, which is what makes the second-source rule observable at all. The two are written as an element loop under `#pragma clang loop vectorize(enable) interleave(enable)`, because at `-O1` without it clang scalarizes the loop to `minss` and `maxss` and the test would be checking instructions the shipped binary does not use; the emitter records a hole for either of those, so a fixture that drifted to the scalar form would fail rather than quietly test something else. The four conversions are the exception to that oracle: the native build cannot be the reference there, because arm64 saturates where x86 returns the integer indefinite value, so they are compared with a C function in the same fixture that spells the x86 rule out with range comparisons, and their inputs are drawn to include NaN, both infinities and values just inside and just outside each destination's range. They match, and breaking the emitter makes the test fail. This validates the emitter. It says nothing about any il2cpp body, because there is no oracle for real game code.

radare2 6.2.6 was scored on 30 bodies and does not get there. Its `af` can follow tail jumps well past the function end (one body came back as 12.5 MB), its extent matched the frame record in 8 of 30, none of its 30 `pdc` outputs compile as C (register-level pseudo-C with raw `push` and bare address calls), it has no IR output, and `pdg` and `pdd` need plugins that are not installed.

Cost and what is still unmeasured, so the result is not stretched past "compiles and matches on synthetic C":

- **Size.** The best measured pipeline, `default<O2>` before `llc -O2`, produces **6.08 object bytes per x86 byte**, about **275 MB** extrapolated from 45.3 MB of game code. This is a sample projection, not a full lift. It reduces the verify-only ratio of 9.12 by 33.3%, with all 3,000 sampled bodies still verifying and compiling. The emitter's register-file byte-array representation is unchanged; this comparison measures post-emission passes, not a redesigned register model.
- **Exceptions.** 8,373 of the 196,137 bodies (4.3%) carry an LSDA, 29.6% of large ones, and two thirds of those need five or more landing pads; the census is above. `.gcc_except_table` is 1.1 MB and the unwind CIE names a personality routine, so managed exceptions run on C++ unwinding. The emitter lifts none of it, which leaves those bodies liftable but unable to unwind.
- **Calling interface and data.** Lifted bodies call each other with a state pointer, not the arm64 calling convention, and the il2cpp runtime and Unity call into the game code natively. Pointers stored in data (vtables, method tables) hold x86 addresses; the 466,320 relative relocations identify them, and the census above says what rewriting them costs: 53% name an exact function entry and none lands mid-body, so those are symbol references, while 47% name writable, read-only and zero-filled data the lift does not touch, which has to be rebuilt and relocated as well. Rewriting any of them has not been done or tested.
- **Float semantics.** The SSE float operations still without semantics are `minss`, `maxss`, `minpd` and `maxpd`. Out-of-range float-to-int conversion is settled, so it is no longer one of them: `cvttss2si` and `cvttsd2si` return the integer indefinite value, `0x80000000` or `0x8000000000000000`, for NaN and for any magnitude the destination cannot hold, where a bare `fptosi` is poison and the `fcvtsz` it becomes on arm64 saturates at `0x7fffffffffffffff` instead. TRUNC therefore lowers to a guard rather than to the cast: `fcmp uno` against itself, `fcmp oge` against `+2^(bits-1)` and `fcmp olt` against `-2^(bits-1)`, OR'd into one flag that selects a safe operand ahead of the single `fptosi` and selects the indefinite value after it. Every bound is a power of two, so each is exact in both float and double and no threshold can round. Measured on x86: `-2^31` is itself a valid result while `2^31` is not, and below `-2^31` truncation and the indefinite value are the same bits, so the window between them is unobservable. What is left unmeasured is NaN payload propagation in the operations that fold several cases into one instruction; the differential test pins NaN and signed-zero lanes for packed division, packed min/max, sign-mask extraction and the four unclamped conversions, and nothing else.
- **Thread-local storage and the rest of the platform shim** are untouched here.

The result for the routes: Route A and B′ are not blocked by liftability. They are blocked by the same things B is, plus the exception, calling-interface and size questions above, and nothing in this sample runs. Route C is not alone as the arm64-native option, but what it takes to make the lifted code actually run in the player is unmeasured.

### Accounting for the whole executable image

`task re:lift-image` (`scripts/lift_image.py`, writing `.tools/lift-image/coverage.json`) says what every executable byte of the pinned Linux `GameAssembly.so` is, before any full lift is attempted. Per section it counts the `.eh_frame` bodies and sorts every byte between them: padding when the gap decodes to nop instructions only (`90`, `cc`, and the `66`/`2e`-prefixed `0f 1f` forms), zero fill, frame-less stub bytes (`.plt` only), or unaccounted code. The sampled probe cannot see that last class because it draws only from frame records. Measured: `.text` holds 62,964 bodies over 3,922,764 B and 457,987 B of padding, `il2cpp` holds 196,137 bodies over 45,290,294 B and 1,648,506 B of padding with no unaccounted byte, `.plt` is 28,112 B of lazy-binding stubs (each `jmp [slot]; push idx; jmp PLT0`) that bind to imports rather than lift, and no frame record lies outside an executable section. Five stretches of code carry no frame record and are unaccounted: `.init` (36 B), `.fini` (14 B), 208 B at the start of `.text` (four C runtime start-up functions: the two `*_tm_clones` helpers, `__do_global_dtors_aux` and `frame_dummy`, with no marker between them) and 34 B at the end of `.text` (a walker over a function-pointer array). The command exits 1 while any code is unaccounted, so a runnable-build verdict cannot be reached until those five are lifted or excluded with a stated reason; the 208 B range has to be split into its four functions first, since lifting it as one body would fall through between them.

`task re:lift-image-build` scores the pinned image in 65 shards, including the seven frame-less functions split out of the five unaccounted ranges. It lists zero-length frame records separately because they cover no executable bytes, and it explicitly excludes `.plt` because those stubs bind to imports. The default output directory is `.tools/lift-image-full`, separate from the sample baselines. Rerun the same command to resume; shard reuse checks the image digest, dump-script fingerprint, Ghidra version, ordered address and size list, pipeline for scored rows, and the row inventory. Use a separate output directory after changing the lifter or LLVM version; the score cache does not key on those. Incomplete dumps retain complete rows and retry only missing ranges; a range that still fails on its own gets a blocking row. `task re:lift-image-build -- --retry-missing-dump` retries cached missing rows without rescoring the successful ones.

The body-by-body results are in `bodies.jsonl`; `summary.json` records stage counts, holes, the `branchind` proof census, the `tables` tally (bodies, and tables within them, lowered from the image's own table bytes rather than Ghidra's case list), rejected bodies and their addresses. The command exits nonzero if any body did not decode, lift without holes, verify, or compile for arm64. Object size is the sum of the per-body object outputs, including objects compiled with holes; it is not a linked library size. This run does not link the bodies, bind imports, or initialize the runtime, so compilation alone is not proof the game runs.

The full run accounted for 259,102 executable bodies: 259,095 nonempty frame records and seven frame-less functions. Six zero-length frame records were excluded. All 259,102 bodies decoded, verified, and compiled for arm64, and the default pipeline produced 581,169,280 bytes of per-body objects from 49,213,349 bytes of x86-64 bodies. 256,140 lift without holes; at that build the other 2,962 blocked the runnable verdict. The hole labels were `BRANCHIND` (2,527), `CALLOTHER:LOCK` and `CALLOTHER:UNLOCK` (427 each), and the SSE/`cpuid`/x87 tail listed below; a body can contribute several labels. The 22 `JUMPTABLE_TARGET` bodies the first full run reported are gone: those bodies now lower over their own table bytes, and `summary.json` counts them under `tables` (22 bodies, 24 tables) rather than under holes. Rescoring the whole image into a fresh directory seeded with the baseline's pcode shards took 4,949 s against 8,736.9 s for the same 65 shards with Ghidra running, so the dump is about 43% of the wall time of a from-scratch run.

Rescoring the same 259,102 bodies again, with the lock regions lowered, into a second fresh directory seeded with the same shards blocks 2,538 instead of 2,962: 424 bodies become hole-free and none becomes blocked, so the change costs nothing anywhere else in the image. `CALLOTHER` falls from 2,961 hole rows to 165, and only 12 bodies still carry one, all in the non-atomic tail (`pshufhw` 12 sites, the 19 `cpuid` leaf names 8 each, `packssdw` 6, `movmskpd` 4, and the rest in the list below). `BRANCHIND` is exactly unchanged at 2,527 bodies, which makes it the class that decides the verdict. `summary.json` reports `atomics` as 427 bodies and 1,398 sites, and the per-body objects grow from 581,169,280 to 581,263,896 bytes. 427 bodies lower their regions but 424 become clean, because three of them also carry another blocker. Wall time 4,549.7 s, with `runnable` still false.

Rescoring the same 259,102 bodies a third time, after the sibling-call gate, the four packed SSE ops, the `CALLOTHER` model tail and the x87 lifter, leaves `runnable` false with 148 blocking bodies instead of 2,538. 2,390 bodies become hole-free, and the per-body diff against the previous build is the check that the change is pure gain: no body that was clean gained a hole, and no body's hole set grew. All 259,102 still decode, verify and compile for arm64, `failures` is empty, and the per-body objects grow from 581,263,896 to 581,923,704 bytes in 4,017.9 s. The `holes` tally is down to two labels, `BRANCHIND` (184 sites in 148 bodies) and one body carrying `CALLOTHER:invalidInstructionException`; every `CALLOTHER` and x87 label that blocked an earlier run is absent from every row, including all five width-10 labels only `0x2121eef` ever carried. The jumps the gate now clears show up in the tail-call tallies: `tail_calls` 20,518 to 22,896 sites, `proven_tail_calls` 17,440 to 19,818, `indirect_calls` 124,911 to 127,289.

What is left is one class and one body. The 148 split 136 in `il2cpp` and 12 in `.text`, and their objects are 799,392 of the 581,923,704 bytes the build emits. 127 of them refuse one site, 12 refuse two, 4 three, 4 four and 1 five, for 184 refused sites. The census in `summary.json` records 187 entries across those bodies, 125 as `rsp not at entry value`, 42 as `no path in the dump reaches the site`, 20 as `rsp untrackable on a path into the site`; the 3 beyond the holes are load-traced tail calls, recorded as unproven and lowered anyway, one each in `0x3d44500`, `0x3faa930` and `0x4adaa00`. Reading that reason column as a verdict needs one caveat: the recorder writes the frame verdict's reason and falls back to `no path in the dump reaches the site` when the verdict is proven, so a site refused for the shape of its target is listed there as a frame failure. Asked of the gate's two tests directly, all 184 holes separate: 125 have rsp not at its entry value on some path into the site, 29 have no path in the dump that reaches the site, 18 have rsp untrackable on a path into it, and 12 pass both proofs and fail only the handover shape; 136 bodies fail on the frame proof, 12 on the handover shape, none on both. The one remaining `CALLOTHER` hole is `0x208b610` at `0x208bb6d`, and it is the trap rule declining rather than a missing lowering: the shape test passes there, the only reader of the model's result being the `BRANCHIND` in the next op, but two branch targets in that body lie at or past the trap's fall-through address (`0x208bb6f` and `0x208bb92`), so the code after the undefined opcode is live from elsewhere and ending the block would delete a reachable block.

The 12 handover refusals are three shapes, and naming them is the useful part, because each says what a further rule would have to know. They sit 10 in `.text` and 2 in `il2cpp`, which is most of `.text`'s 12 blocked bodies: the other two are `0x207bc90`, a frame-proof refusal, and the trap body `0x208b610` above. Five jump through a fixed word directly: the `BRANCHIND` operand is `ram,0x53…` itself, Ghidra having folded the `jmp *[rip+disp]` load into the operand, so there is no `LOAD` for the load-trace rule and no `COPY` from a slot for the slot form of it — four are one-instruction 6-byte bodies (`0x209d5c0`, `0x209e0a0`, `0x209e0c0`, `0x209e0d0`) and the fifth is the site at `0x20f6e90` in the 120-byte `0x20f6e30`. Four are 2-byte `jmp *%rsi` thunks (`0x21405c6`, `0x2141a96`, `0x215108c`, `0x2151a16`): the target is an architectural register the body never writes at all, so the caller's argument is the pointer, which is refused by the rule's one-`COPY` requirement rather than by anything the body does with it. Three tail-jump through `rax` after the body wrote it on two different paths — the lazy-resolution guard, read the cached function pointer out of a global or zero it (`0x2027ad0`, `0x4a61140`, `0x4a79900`) — so the single-write rule refuses them, correctly by its own terms. None of the three is a missing lowering; each is a rule that has not been written, and each would need its own evidence about what the pointer can hold.

`runnable` is the conjunction of the byte accounting and that stage census: coverage complete, every body decoded, hole-free, verified and compiled for arm64. Coverage is complete in this run, so the 148 refused indirect jumps are the only thing standing between the image and the verdict, and the caveat above still qualifies what the verdict would mean: nothing here links the bodies, binds an import or initializes the runtime.

For lifter iteration, rescore only the addresses blocked by a complete baseline. This reads the baseline pcode without launching Ghidra or changing the baseline. Use the baseline's `--shard-size` if it differs from the default. Each completed shard is checkpointed; rerunning skips scores whose pcode, scorer source, LLVM versions, pipeline, and tool timeout still match. The scorer source in that signature is the lifter, the probe, the pcode translator and the table decoder together, so a lifter change discards every cached blocker score and the run scores all of them again; it also refuses to reuse an output directory scored by a different signature. Run it after a full build finishes rather than beside one, because two Ghidra dumps on the same image contend for the same cores.

```sh
task re:lift-image-build LIFT_OUT=.tools/lift-image-blockers -- --blockers-from .tools/lift-image-full
```

Read `blockers-summary.json` and `blockers.jsonl` in the iteration directory. A zero exit means only that the baseline blockers now pass. To check for regressions and get a full-image verdict, use a fresh output directory seeded with the baseline's validated pcode shards only, and run `task re:lift-image-build` without `--blockers-from`.

When a blocker needs fresh jump-table evidence, pass the body start addresses explicitly. This probe disassembles only the requested bodies, leaves the baseline pcode and prior tables untouched, and writes separate per-body checkpoints. It defaults to 5 seconds per function, 90 seconds per Ghidra batch, and eight bodies per batch. A timeout is recorded at the active address; later bodies are still attempted. Rerun the same command to resume, or use a separate output directory with a longer deadline for the timed-out addresses only.

```sh
uv run scripts/lift_jump_evidence.py --out .tools/lift-jump-evidence 0x4bd70f0 0x2862830
```

`evidence.jsonl` contains the recovered targets, status, and elapsed time where available. A completed decompilation with no tables is not evidence that an indirect jump is safe to lower. C text is disabled by default; use `--emit-c` in a separate directory to compare table recovery with C generation enabled. This command does not merge evidence into the lifter or replace the full-image dump path.

Use `--bounded` to give the Ghidra function exactly the byte range in the frame record instead of letting Ghidra infer the body. Compare `function_bytes` and `outside_function_bytes` between the two modes. The assigned function body is not a guarantee that the decompiler follows only those bytes. In either mode, a table with a switch site or a target outside the frame is recorded with `out_of_body` and marked `unresolved_bounds` when decompilation completes. The full target list stays in `jt` for inspection; it is not safe to use as-is in the lifter.

On five shipped bodies, the table-only run and C-enabled run both took 19.7 seconds and agreed on the target lists. Both recovered the two known tables at their original addresses and timed out on `0x2862830` and `0x28629a0` at the 5-second function deadline, then continued to later bodies. Rerunning from checkpoints took 3.1 seconds without starting Ghidra. The measured gain comes from bounding and isolating slow functions, not from disabling C output.

Assigning explicit frame bounds to the same five Ghidra functions did not improve runtime: 19.7 seconds bounded versus 19.5 seconds inferred. All five inferred function bodies were already inside their frames; both slow bodies still timed out, and both recovered tables were identical in the two modes. For the body at `0x46442f0`, both modes reported a recovered table whose site and targets were outside the frame. An out-of-frame diagnostic can therefore appear even when Ghidra's assigned function body is in bounds.

On eight known blockers, the bounded pass produced no target lists that improved on the baseline: three completed without tables, two timed out, and three reproduced cross-frame targets already in the baseline. Those three external targets (`0x4920a40`, `0x4dd7120`, and `0x4e22580`) each match the start of another nonempty frame record. They are candidates for cross-body execution tests, not evidence that a switch edge can be lowered as a tail call. The byte audit below later showed none of the three is an entry of its own table: the body reaches each by an independent direct branch, so the `JUMPTABLE_TARGET` hole on those bodies was an over-collected case list rather than a missing table.

A recovered case list is checked against the image's own table bytes rather than trusted. Pass `--baseline <build dir>` with addresses to audit the tables in a completed build's pcode; each table in `audit.jsonl` gains `raw` (the decoded entries), `raw_matches` and, when they differ, `extra_cases`, `missing_cases`, and the extras split into `extra_direct_edges` (targets the body also reaches by a direct `BRANCH` or `CBRANCH` in its own pcode), `extra_outside_body` (targets past the end of the frame, which cannot be edges of it) and `extra_unexplained` (targets on the body's own code that no branch reaches). Without addresses it sweeps every table-bearing row of the build, and `audit-summary.json` tallies rows, tables, matched/unverified/unaudited tables and the three extra classes; tables are counted rather than rows because a body can carry several tables. Only files named `pcode-<digits>.jsonl` are read: a shard's backup copy left beside it would otherwise audit the same bodies twice. The decoder reads the `lea #table,%reg; movslq (%reg,%idx,4),%t; add %reg,%t; jmp *%t` sequence at the site with `llvm-objdump`, and takes the entry count from a `cmp $N` plus taken `ja` whose compared operand is the switch index: the index register itself, or the memory that a move between the guard and the indexed load brings into it. A guard on some other value bounds nothing, so it yields no count. Moves within the index register (`movl %eax, %eax`, `movzbl %al, %eax`) keep the bounded value; a load from anything but the compared operand, a store to compared memory, or a call between the guard and the load loses it. Entries are base-relative 4-byte offsets, or absolute 8-byte addresses at scale 8. The disassembly window starts at an instruction boundary the dump reported: decoding from an arbitrary offset changes the instructions and reports a pattern that is not there.

The lifter consults the same bytes through `TableEvidence` in `scripts/lift_tables.py`, passed to `lift_body` as `evidence=` and consulted only when a recovered case list holds a case outside the body. The list is replaced by the decoded entries when every entry is an instruction the dump reported and every case Ghidra added beyond them either is a direct-branch target of the body or lies outside the body: a table with a guard tied to its index names all the switch's targets, and an address outside the body is not one of the body's edges. A case on the body's own code that no branch reaches is the sign of a wrong entry count, so it keeps the `JUMPTABLE_TARGET` hole. `tables_audited` counts the tables a body lowered this way, and a scored body records it.

Measured over the 22 `JUMPTABLE_TARGET` blockers, all 134 tables in their rows decode: 67 match Ghidra's recovered list exactly, and in the rest the entries Ghidra added beyond them are 1,206 addresses outside the lifted frame, ten direct-branch targets of the body, and five addresses on the body's own code that no branch reaches, all in `0x2159b14`, whose list is also missing five decoded entries. None of them blocks: the whole set of 22 bodies lifts over the table bytes. Each differing list is the decoded entries plus addresses from elsewhere: in `0x47a1a30`, `0x4922a20`, `0x4922b60`, `0x4dd8a20`, `0x4dd8b60` and `0x4e22690` it is one address the body also reaches by a direct branch; in `0x41488d0`, `0x417f8a0`, `0x4188f10` and `0x4d8c980` a mix of one direct edge and out-of-frame addresses; in `0x415bfc0`, `0x415c400` and `0x415d000` only out-of-frame ones. In the eight `std::money_put` and `std::money_get` bodies (`0x2126a9e`, `0x2126e92`, `0x2130724`, `0x2130ac4`, `0x2148c80`, `0x2149070`, `0x2159b14`, `0x2159ee4`) and in `0x243cf40`, Ghidra's function spans several frame records: the row carries tables whose sites lie past the end of the frame being lifted, the in-frame site's list holds 67 to 77 addresses that belong to those other frames, and every one of them is outside the lifted body. The five-entry `std::money_base::_S_default_pattern` switches are guarded by `cmpb $0x4, 0x5c(%rsp,%r13); ja` with the index loaded by the matching `movzbl`, so the count comes from a guard on the pattern byte rather than on a register.

The execution fixture in `tests/test_lift_control_flow_exec.py` compares a shipped jump-table predicate and an indirect tail jump to a different shipped body against the same pinned ELF running on Linux x86-64. It sweeps all 82 in-range switch inputs and two out-of-range ones. The arm64 dispatcher aborts for an unresolved address or a malformed target inside a body instead of calling either address.

After the jump tables, the `CALLOTHER` holes were the remaining class with real semantics to write, and they were almost all lock-prefixed atomics: 1,398 sites in 427 bodies. Ghidra brackets one locked instruction with a `CALLOTHER` whose `user` is `LOCK` (input `const,11`) and one whose `user` is `UNLOCK` (input `const,12`); the wrapped instruction is `xadd` (712 sites), `xchg` (402), `cmpxchg` (187), `add` (39), `or` (29), `cmpxchg16b` (18), `inc` (7), `dec` (3) and `sub` (1), and a body can carry several. What sits between the markers is the operation as ordinary pcode: a `LOAD`, the arithmetic, a `STORE`, and the flag computations, which re-`LOAD` the same address once per flag. Ignoring the markers and lowering what is between them would emit a non-atomic read-modify-write, so the hole was the right verdict until the region was lowered as one atomic. The 19 distinct region shapes that census found are all lowered now: each of the 1,398 sites emits one atomic, and the emitted kinds reconcile with the instruction census (758 `atomicrmw add` for the 712 `xadd` plus 39 `add` plus 7 `inc`, 402 `atomicrmw xchg`, 205 `cmpxchg` for the 187 `cmpxchg` plus 18 `cmpxchg16b`, 4 `atomicrmw sub` for the 3 `dec` plus 1 `sub`, 29 `atomicrmw or`). Widths are 1,210 i32, 113 i64, 57 i8 and 18 i128. `Lifted.atomics` counts the sites a body lowered, `summary.json` carries the `atomics` tally beside `tables`, and a body that cannot be lowered keeps both `CALLOTHER:LOCK` and `CALLOTHER:UNLOCK` rather than emitting a sequence that only looks like the instruction. The narrowest region for each mnemonic, and the narrowest at each access width, is copied out of the dump into `tests/fixtures/lift/lock_regions.json`; `tests/test_pcode_lift_atomics.py` re-reads the dump to prove each entry is still what was dumped before checking the atomic it lowers to. The other `CALLOTHER` holes are a small tail, each with its own question: `pshufhw` (12 sites in 1 body), `packssdw` (6 in 1), `movmskpd` (4 in 3), `cpuid` plus 19 leaf-specific variants (8 sites each, the same 3 bodies, a CPU feature query whose answer the lifted code branches on), `rdrand` and `rdrandIsValid`, `mwaitx` and `monitorx`, `maxpd`, and `invalidInstructionException` (2 sites, where Ghidra itself declined to decode).

A region is lowered only when every op in it is one the rewriter models and its ops describe one memory location read once and written once. The accepted set is the one the dump uses: across the 1,398 regions Ghidra emits `COPY`, `LOAD`, `STORE`, `CBRANCH`, `BRANCH`, `SUBPIECE`, `POPCOUNT`, `INT_ZEXT`, `INT_LEFT`, the `INT_BINARY` arithmetic and `INT_COMPARE` comparisons, and the three flag forms `INT_CARRY`, `INT_SCARRY`, `INT_SBORROW`, so an op outside it (a call, an indirect branch, a segmented access, an unmodeled arithmetic) keeps the hole instead of being treated as inert. The address is followed through the region's own `INT_ADD` and `INT_MULT` chain to either a `ram` constant (a static address, resolved through the section table like every other memory operand) or a `unique` value built only by pure ops, and the region's accesses are the `LOAD`, the `STORE`, and any `COPY` with a `ram` operand; a second location on either side keeps the hole. The kind comes from the shape: a region containing a `CBRANCH` is a `cmpxchg`, because only the compare forms model the conditional store with a branch, and otherwise the stored value's definition chain either reaches an `INT_BINARY` (a read-modify-write) or leaves the region through an external varnode (an exchange). A `cmpxchg` whose `expected` operand is itself derived from the old value is refused, because the compare would then be against something the atomic is about to overwrite.

The rewrite changes only the location accesses and keeps everything else Ghidra emitted, in place, including the region-exit register file and the flags. Reads of the location before the `STORE` become the atomic's returned old value, reads after it become the stored value, and the `STORE` itself is dropped; the atomic occupies the slot of the region's first access. Op count is preserved, with displaced ops turned into `NOP`, because Ghidra's `CBRANCH` and `BRANCH` carry their targets as `const` op-index offsets into the same op list: removing an op would silently retarget every branch in the region. Pure ops that feed the atomic's operands are moved up to the atomic's slot only when that preserves their order against everything they jump over, which for a compare region means definitions are sought only in the prefix before the `CBRANCH` and in the arm that holds the `STORE`. Searching the whole region instead lets the arm that never reaches the store supply a definition, which reads the untaken path's writes as the value's origin, produces `COPY` cycles across the two arms, and feeds the atomic values that path computed. One exception is needed and made explicit: the varnode the atomic's result restores is written at the region's own slot, after the moved ops, so a moved op may reuse that name and an op left behind may still read the pre-region value from it.

The flags stay whatever Ghidra's own ops compute, because the region already writes them and the compare/branch still chooses the path. The measured shapes are worth recording because they are not uniform: `lock add` recomputes `CF` with `INT_CARRY` and `OF` with `INT_SCARRY`, `lock sub 1` writes `OF` with `INT_SBORROW` and leaves `CF` alone, `lock or` zeroes `CF` and `OF` with `COPY const,0,1` before the value is read, and `SF`, `ZF` and `PF` always come from the stored result (`INT_SLESS`, `INT_EQUAL`, and a `POPCOUNT` chain that sets `PF` on *even* parity). There is no plain `lock sub [mem], reg` in the image, only the immediate form.

Every emitted atomic is `syncscope("sys") seq_cst` with a `fence syncscope("sys") seq_cst` on each side, because `lock` is a full barrier on x86-64, and carries `align 1`. The attribute is a correctness requirement, not an optimization: the architecture requires the exclusives and `cas`/`casp` to hit a naturally aligned address, and the lifter has no way to know the alignment of a lock word the guest chose. On this host the tolerance is visible but not trustworthy: an `atomicrmw` compiled with its alignment attribute raised to natural inlines `ldaxr`/`stlxr`, and running that over an 8-byte word at an address 1 mod 8 neither faulted nor lost the update, which is Apple Silicon accepting what the architecture does not sanction. So `align 1` stays, and the fixture tests pin it as an exact suffix of every lowered region's atomic line rather than a substring match, which is the difference between catching a raised attribute and passing on `align 16`.

Who supplies the sequence when the attribute is present is per target, measured by compiling the lifted module of each of the 427 bodies with `llc -O2`:

| Target | Bodies referencing an atomic runtime symbol | Symbols |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `arm64-apple-macosx` | 387 of 427 | `___atomic_compare_exchange` (371), `___atomic_exchange` (129), 113 bodies both |
| `aarch64-unknown-linux-gnu` | 387 of 427 | `__atomic_compare_exchange`, `__atomic_exchange` |
| `aarch64-unknown-linux-gnu -mattr=+outline-atomics` | 418 of 427 | the two above plus `__aarch64_swp1_acq_rel` (36 sites) |

The two macOS names are exported by libSystem: the SDK's `libSystem.tbd` lists the unsuffixed generic forms next to size-suffixed ones that stop at 8 bytes, with no `_16`, which is why the 16-byte `cmpxchg` also has to be a call. So the emitted form links on arm64 macOS against the platform runtime with nothing added, and the executed tests link against it instead of supplying their own primitive; the check that survives there is that the lowering references nothing outside what libSystem provides. The Linux names are libatomic's entry points, so a lifted image for `aarch64-unknown-linux-gnu` takes libatomic as a link-time dependency, and a loader shim on that target has to resolve `__atomic_*` and `__aarch64_*` for real or refuse them rather than stub them. Removing the attribute from the atomic lines drops the references to zero on both triples, with the exclusives inlined, which is what makes the alignment proof the follow-up worth doing: a static location whose offset is a multiple of its own size under a page-aligned base can carry its natural alignment and pay no call on either platform.

Two measurements about the emitted set are recorded so they are not re-investigated. Nine of the 427 bodies produce an object with neither an atomic instruction nor an atomic symbol at `-O2` or at `-O0`: `0x2065e70`, `0x2089250`, `0x2098ad0`, `0x209b1f0`, `0x209dc90`, `0x209dde0`, `0x209f9f0`, `0x20d6480` and `0x20da2e0`. The lifter before any atomic work produces the same objects for the same nine, so their locked code is unreachable rather than unlowered. Checking this needs the disassembly, not the symbol table: at `-O2` an inlined atomic is an instruction (`ldaxr`/`stlxr`, `casa`, `swpa`, `ldadda`, `dmb`) and leaves no undefined symbol behind. And the rewrite introduces no unreachable code. The same bodies compiled before and after the atomic lowering give the same block count and the same set of blocks unreached from the entry, so the near-total unreachability of these bodies (417 blocks at `0x2057cd0`, of which 415 are never reached) belongs to the pre-existing dispatcher and branch lowering and is not something the atomics caused.

The remaining `CALLOTHER` holes were a tail of Ghidra opcode models, each with its own question about what the model is claiming. They are lowered one at a time, and each keeps its hole when the pcode is not the shape that model emits.

The four packed SSE ops in that tail became lane arithmetic on the 16-byte value. `maxpd` joins the family beside `divps`, `divpd`, `minps` and `maxps`, where a lane keeps the first operand only while it strictly compares against the second (`ogt` for `max`, `olt` for `min`), so a NaN in either lane selects the second operand. `movmskpd` gathers each lane's sign bit into a dword, `pshufhw` permutes the four high words by the immediate selector and copies the low half through, and `packssdw` saturates the destination's four dwords into the low words and the source's into the high ones. None lowers unless a shape guard accepts it: every varnode at the width the instruction has, the destination repeated as the first input, and for `pshufhw` a byte constant selector. A `CALLOTHER` named for one of these instructions but carrying another width, a non-register destination or a computed selector is not that instruction and keeps its hole. `maxpd` and `packssdw` are checked against the real instruction, the lifted body and `_mm_max_pd` and `_mm_packs_epi32` compiled for x86-64 run over the same operand pairs under Rosetta; `pshufhw` sweeps all 256 selectors against a word-index reference and `movmskpd` every sign-bit pattern against an explicit bit reference.

An undefined opcode — the `CALLOTHER` whose `user` is `invalidInstructionException`, where Ghidra itself declined to decode the bytes — becomes `call void @llvm.trap()` and the end of the block. Ghidra's model fabricates a `BRANCHIND` through the helper's result, and ending the block retires that jump instead of branching wherever the undefined value happens to be. The trap is only safe where nothing after it survives: a branch target or an exception landing pad past it in the same body, or a reader of the result other than the `BRANCHIND` in the very next op, keeps the hole, because then the fabricated control flow is load-bearing somewhere else. Executed on the host the lifted body dies with `SIGTRAP`.

`MONITORX` and `MWAITX` are dropped, and the drop is conditional on the dump agreeing they are inert: each carries only its opcode constant and no destination, and no op in those bodies reads a result. They are address-monitor hints, so emitting nothing is what a host without them does and a spin loop still spins; they emit not even an instruction. A `MONITORX` that returns something is a different claim and stays a hole.

`CPUID` and `RDRAND` bind to host helpers, because both answers have to come from the machine the lifted code runs on rather than from anything the lifter could write. `CPUID`, including its 19 leaf-specific variants, becomes one `call void @lift_cpuid(i64 leaf, ptr buf)` into a block the lifter reserves clear of the body's own temporaries; the four dwords are read back at `+0`, `+4`, `+8` and `+0xc` into `EAX`, `EBX`, `EDX` and `ECX`. That order is the dump's, not a guess: Ghidra's x86-64 register table puts `rbx` at `0x18` and `rcx` at `0x8`, so the block is `[EAX, EBX, RDX, RCX]` rather than the register order a reader would expect. `RDRAND` and its `rdrandIsValid` companion share one `call zeroext i1 @lift_rdrand(ptr)`, keyed on the instruction address, so the value written to `EAX` and the flag `EFLAGS` receives cannot disagree, and only a 4-byte destination lowers because that is the width `RDRAND` writes.

The decision recorded for `CPUID` is who supplies what: the lifter fixes the ordering and the embedder supplies the values. `scripts/lift_abi.py` emits both helpers as `weak` definitions so an embedder that has measured the target replaces them, and the default `lift_cpuid` answers through `lift_cpuid_leaf_at(leaf, out)`, whose built-in body says it has no measurement for any leaf; a declined leaf zeroes all four registers. That fail-closed default is the only safe built-in, because a zeroed feature word sends guest code down the conservative path while an invented one lets it execute a host instruction that is not there. `lift_rdrand` returns `_Bool` to match the `zeroext i1` the lifter declares, and its default is a deterministic splitmix64 stream that always claims success, which keeps a body reading randomness reproducible under test. `tests/test_lift_helpers_exec.py` runs both sides rather than inspecting the IR: a measured leaf reaching the four registers in the order the model reads them, a leaf with no table entry reading as zeros even when the helper scribbled into the block, the value and the flag a known `lift_rdrand` produced, and the trap case dying on the host.

x87 is in the image, but only in the statically linked code: `.text` holds 29 x87 instructions across six bodies and `il2cpp` holds none. Five of the six lift with no hole because nothing in them computes — `0x2100580` writes and reads the x87 control word as a 2-byte store and load, and `0x2139548`, `0x213969c`, `0x213b708` and `0x213b85c` each pair one `fldt` with one `fstpt`, which Ghidra models as `LOAD` and `STORE` of a 10-byte varnode with the register-stack rotation as a chain of 10-byte `COPY`es. A bit-faithful move of an 80-bit value needs no type that arm64 lacks. The sixth, `0x2121eef`, holds the other 19 (`fstp` ×5, `fstpt` ×4, `flds` ×2, `fxch` ×2, `fldt` ×2 and one each of `fld`, `fldz`, `fucomi`, `fucompi`) and does arithmetic and ordered comparisons on 80-bit values, so it blocked on `INT2FLOAT:10`, `FLOAT2FLOAT:float width 10`, `FLOAT_NAN:10`, `FLOAT_EQUAL:10` and `FLOAT_LESS:10`: arm64 has no 80-bit float type, so that body needed a decision about what the value is allowed to become, not an emitter case.

The decision is that an 80-bit value stays an integer. It is carried as `i80` through loads, stores and copies, and every op that decodes or builds the encoding works in `i128`, so no target-dependent long-double type reaches the IR. That is a measurement, not a preference: with the scorer's `llc` for `arm64-apple-macosx`, `fpext float` to `x86_fp80` is refused with "do not know how to soften fp_extend", and `fptrunc` from `x86_fp80`, `fcmp olt x86_fp80`, `fadd x86_fp80` and `sitofp i32` to `x86_fp80` each abort with "LLVM ERROR: unsupported library call operation", while all five assemble for `x86_64-apple-macosx`. A value that is only stored and reloaded does compile on both, which is why the five move-only x87 bodies above needed nothing at all; `i128` compiles everywhere, so the arithmetic is done in it. A width-10 operand is refused everywhere except the five shapes the image contains: `FLOAT2FLOAT` 4→10 and `INT2FLOAT` 4→10 build the sign, exponent and explicit integer bit directly, and `FLOAT_NAN`, `FLOAT_EQUAL` and `FLOAT_LESS` are comparisons over those fields. Every other width-10 shape stays a hole, including all narrowing and all width-10 arithmetic, because the image contains no instance whose rounding mode could be observed: a lowering there would be invented rather than measured.

The predicates and the two widenings are checked against the FPU, not against a reading of the model. `tests/test_pcode_lift_x87.py` uses the ops of `0x2121f26`, `0x2121f2c` and `0x2121f34` transcribed op for op from the cached dump, and one shared table of inputs drives both programs: an x86-64 binary under Rosetta widens each value with `flds`/`fildl` and `fstpt` and answers the comparisons with `x != x`, `x == y` and `x < y` on 80-bit values, while the lifted arm64 module is asked the same questions through a driver that pokes identical bytes into the offsets the dump names (`register,1100,10` and `register,1110,10` for the two stack slots, `register,200`–`203,1` for the four flags). 240 rows, equal on both sides. The comparison inputs are built as exponent class × integer bit × empty-or-not fraction with both signs, so they include the encodings x87 refuses to order — fake NaN, SNaN, the two pseudo-denormals, the exponent-without-integer-bit pair — and a focused pass compares only pairs where at least one side is one of those. Two more cases are worth naming: the dump's own `INT2FLOAT` takes `const,0,4` and has to store the all-zero encoding, and the same test asserts no 80-bit float type name appears in the IR at all, which is the check that the integer model did not quietly become an `fp80` one.

What the FPU answered, recorded so it is not re-derived: a normal single widens to exponent `e − 127 + 16383` with its 23-bit fraction left-aligned into the 63-bit field (`m << 40`) under the integer bit; a single subnormal renormalizes, `top = msb(m)`, exponent `16383 + top − 149`, mantissa `m << (63 − top)`; infinity is the integer bit alone with the exponent all ones; a NaN is the integer bit, the quiet bit at 62, and the payload shifted by 40. `FILD` of an int is exact at this width: the magnitude's `msb` sits at bit 63 and the exponent is `16383 + msb`, with zero as the all-zero encoding. A value is unordered when its exponent is nonzero without its integer bit, or when the exponent is all ones and either the fraction below bit 63 is nonzero or the integer bit is clear — so a quiet-bit setting on an otherwise valid value is not a NaN, and the fraction test masks below bit 63. `FLOAT_EQUAL` is ordered equality of magnitudes with −0 and +0 equal, which is why the sign is compared only when the magnitude is nonzero. `FLOAT_LESS` is ordered sign-magnitude comparison with the same −0/+0 tie, so equal magnitudes are never less and `−0 < +0` is false in both directions. Ghidra's x86 language definition calls the built-in `nan()`, `equal()` and `lessthan()` for these three, so there is no sleigh source to read and the hardware is the only authority. In the full rescore `0x2121eef` lifts with no hole, its module verifies and its object compiles for arm64, and it is the only body in the image those five labels ever appeared in.

Reachability is not a way out of the `BRANCHIND` count. Walking each blocked body's own edges from its first instruction — a `BRANCH` or `CBRANCH` target, the fall-through after anything that is not `BRANCH`, `RETURN`, `BRANCHIND` or `BAD` (a `CALL` falls through), and every recovered table entry — 2,513 of the 2,527 bodies have every `BRANCHIND` site reachable, and the other 14 have some reachable and some not. No blocked body has all of its sites outside the flow, so none of those holes is the artifact of a disassembled-but-dead tail. Two operands worth of care went into that walk: branch targets arrive both as `const,0x…` and as `ram,0x…,8`, a fixed-address jump written in the `ram` space like the tail-call slots, and reading only the first of the two marks live code unreachable.

The blocked sites are tail calls through a pointer the caller supplied, not dispatches through a table: at the site, the target register holds a value the body never computes. `0x1e3e800` at site `0x1e3e851` is the shape. Its target is `register,88`, defined there by `COPY register,88,8 <- register,38,8`, and `register,38` has no write before that point (its only write in the body is at `0x1e3e80c`), so the value arrived in a register from the caller. A census that asks only whether *some* writer of the target register is a `LOAD` misreports this class: 2,218 of the 2,574 sites pass that test and fail program order. Three routes to target provenance were measured, and none reaches the class. A reaching-definitions pass over the body's own control flow, with a definite-assignment side so a path that skips a definition cannot be papered over by a may-reaching one, accepts 9 sites of 2,574; 2,378 refuse because some path reaches the site without defining the target, 146 are copies out of a fixed slot whose frame is not proven, 39 have a target that may be undefined, and 2 sit behind a `CALLOTHER`. Whole-program evidence is thinner: 89 of the 2,527 blocked bodies (3.5%) have an inbound `CALL` anywhere in the image and 103 (4.1%) an inbound `CALL` or a `BRANCH` or `CBRANCH` out of another body, against 18.7% and 23.4% for hole-free bodies as a control; 41 have exactly one inbound edge and only 3 of those have every site proven balanced. Devirtualization from call sites is not a route for this class, and there is no in-body provenance to find.

What is provable at those sites is the frame. `site_proof` reports 2,395 of the 2,574 blocked sites proven with rsp equal to its entry value on every path the dump reads, 108 unknown-balanced, 51 with rsp not at its entry value, and 20 untrackable on a path into the site; 2,391 bodies have every blocked site proven. Only 4 of those 2,391 contain a `RETURN` at all, and 2,377 are single-block, call-free, return-free bodies that end in `jmp reg`, which is why an unqualified "the frame balances, so it is a tail call" rule would also license the computed switches the same file pins as holes.

So a `BRANCHIND` with no recovered table lowers as an indirect call and a return when the frame is proven at the site *and* the target varnode is handed over by exactly one op: a `COPY` from an architectural register, ahead of the site, with no other write to that varnode anywhere in the body before or after it. That handover is the positive evidence, and it names the invoke-trampoline shape the class ships, `mov rbx, rdi … mov rdi, rdx; jmp rbx`. Refusal is by shape: a target never written before the site, written more than once, written again after it, defined by a non-`COPY` op (including single-operand ops such as `INT_SEXT` and `CALLOTHER`), copied from a constant, copied from a static `ram` or `cptr` slot, or copied from a `unique` temporary. The temporary is refused because a copy from a temporary is how the body delivers a value it computed, and the operand space is what tells the two apart; measured over the blocked sites, all 2,378 that pass take their single `COPY` from a `register`, so the narrower rule costs nothing. No dominance check is needed: with one write, the value at the site is that copy or the caller's own, and neither is computed, so the emitted call and return transfer control where the guest `jmp *reg` would. Balance alone still licenses nothing, because a frameless switch also has rsp at its entry value.

Measured with the rule in place over the whole image, 2,378 of the 2,574 sites and 2,378 of the 2,527 blocked bodies become `BRANCHIND`-free, each with a proven tail call, all 2,378 assembling with `llc` for arm64, and none of the previously pinned computed-target holes changed verdict. The full rescore puts the residue at 148 bodies and 184 sites, and classifies every one of them against the gate's own two tests: 136 bodies fail on the frame proof (125 sites with rsp not at its entry value on some path into them, 29 with no path in the dump reaching the site, 18 with rsp untrackable on a path into it) and 12 fail on the handover shape with the frame proven; no body fails both.

`tests/test_lift_sibling_call_exec.py` executes one of the cleared bodies instead of only compiling it. `0x1ef48c0` is 8 bytes, `mov rax,rdi; mov rdi,rdx; jmp rax`, with no memory access at all, so the fixture needs nothing but the register file. The lifted body is called through the `lift_abi` shim with a function pointer in the first argument, a decoy in the second and a value in the third; the callee is an imported binding that prints what it finds in `rdi` and returns a function of it. Three things have to be right for the expected output: the two moves applied in order, so the pointer `rdi` originally held is the target; the handover delivered, so the callee sees the third argument; and the callee's return reaching the thunk's own caller with rsp left at entry plus 8, which is the shim's abort condition for an unbalanced frame.

### Executing a lifted shipped method

`task re:native-exec` is the first place a lifted shipped body runs rather than only compiles. The target is `InventoryUtility.GetWeaponMaxLevel` in `Assembly-CSharp.dll`. Its identity comes from the shipped Linux runtime, not from the Windows RVAs in `dump.cs`: `scripts/native_bind_probe/host.c` starts the Il2Cpp runtime headless through its exported C API, looks the method up by name, and now records `method_pointer`, `method_image_base` and `method_rva` for the body it resolved. On the shipped Linux `GameAssembly.so` (`a86fd434…5ba1`) that is RVA `0x225b7d0`, the same value as its ELF virtual address because the image base is zero, and `il2cpp_runtime_invoke` returns `40`.

The driver (`scripts/native_exec_probe.py`) then lifts that body from the same ELF with Ghidra pcode and `scripts/pcode_lift.py`, checks it with the LLVM verifier, compiles it with `llc` for `arm64-apple-macosx`, links it with `scripts/native_exec_probe/harness.c` and runs the result. The body at `0x225b7d0` is an 11-byte wrapper that calls `0x225b7e0` and adds `0x28`, so two bodies (11 B and 237 B) are lifted; the four callees they can reach (`0x20563d0`, `0x20565b0`, `0x2056710` and `0x2280400`; the first two sit behind the init guard and the class init flag, and `0x2280400` is called only once the instance is non-null) are stubs that fail the run if they are reached.

The callee reads runtime state, so the harness models exactly three words and the Linux control observes the same three through `--state-chain RVA:off,off,...`: the init-guard byte at `0x5357b2c` is set, the class's init flag at `K+0xe4` is nonzero, and the static `Instance` at `statics+8` is null. With a null instance the callee returns 0 and the wrapper returns `0 + 0x28 = 40`. The report compares the modeled and observed words, and a mismatch fails the run. Clearing the guard in the harness makes the lifted code reach `sub_20563d0`, which confirms the lifted control flow executes rather than the result being a constant.

Checks in `report.json`:

| Check | Fails when |
| --- | --- |
| `result_matches` | the arm64 result differs from the value the shipped Linux runtime returned |
| `body_identity` | the lifted body's RVA, or the control's image and method name, differ from the target |
| `binary_identity` | the `GameAssembly.so` SHA-256 is not a 64-digit digest |
| `native_arm64` | the harness is not arm64 or `sysctl.proc_translated` is not `0` (a Rosetta run reports `1`) |
| `no_unmodelled_calls` | a stubbed callee was reached |
| `state_matches` | the harness's modeled state differs from the state observed on Linux |

`--candidate-override N` (`NATIVE_EXEC_EXTRA='--candidate-override 7'`) replaces the arm64 result before comparison and must exit non-zero; the report marks it `result_overridden`. The report keeps three evidence groups apart: `shipped_game` (the Linux control against the shipped binary, with its digest), `lifted_arm64_harness` (this machine's run, including `lipo -archs` of the executable) and `probe_player`, which is `null` because no Unity player is involved.

Reproduce with the shipped Linux build already synced to `.tools/megabonk-linux-native/Megabonk/` (`task re:shaders-pull` copies it) and a recorded control: `task re:native-exec`. Record a fresh control from the Linux player box with `task re:native-exec NATIVE_EXEC_EXTRA='--capture-linux-host <ssh host> --linux-game-dir <path to the Megabonk directory>'`; it copies `host.c`, compiles it there and runs it headless, with no Steam session or mod loader. The raw control output, the lifted IR, the object, the stubs and the harness output stay under the gitignored `.tools/native-exec/`. Needs the brew `llvm`, Ghidra and Xcode's `clang` and `lipo`.

Limits. The 40 is the value the shipped code produces when the static singleton the callee reads has not been created yet, which is the state of a runtime started with `il2cpp_init` and nothing else; it says nothing about the non-null path, which goes through the `0x2280400` dictionary lookup and real game data. The lifted body reads the modeled globals directly, with the `_got` and `_bss` sections as zeroed arrays rather than the relocated image, and no real Il2Cpp runtime, Unity player or metadata is present on arm64.

The stubs here still fail if reached. Typed native/lifted adapters are verified separately below; this modeled singleton path does not exercise those imports.

### Native and lifted calling contract

`scripts/lift_abi.py` generates C adapters from explicit `Binding(address, name, Signature(args, result))` lists. Lifted bodies retain `void sub_<ELF-address>(unsigned char *reg)`. Native exports marshal into a private 8 KiB register file and 64 KiB guest stack per invocation; imports decode that state, call an explicitly declared native function, store its result and consume the guest return slot. Clang handles the Apple arm64 calling convention, including its different stack argument rules. No arm64 register placement is guessed.

Supported signature kinds:

| Kinds | Source Linux x86-64 SysV placement |
| --- | --- |
| `i8/u8`, `i16/u16`, `i32/u32`, `i64/u64`, `ptr` | independent integer bank: RDI, RSI, RDX, RCX, R8, R9; then stack |
| `f32`, `f64` | independent SSE bank: XMM0–7; then stack |
| `v4f32`, `v2f64`, `v4u32` | one XMM register per 128-bit vector; then 16-byte-aligned stack slot |
| `void` | result only |

Scalar/pointer results use RAX; floating/vector results use XMM0. Stack arguments start at guest RSP+8, after the return address; slots are at least eight bytes. Guest RSP is 8 modulo 16 on entry and must advance by eight on return. The adapter checks this after every exported call. Raw pcode already implements CALL's push and RET's pop: adapters must not push a second return address. Import adapters preserve guest registers except the result and RSP. Lifted bodies remain responsible for SysV callee-saved registers. Private per-entry state permits a native import to call a generated lifted export synchronously, including reentry, without a global register file or TLS state swap.

Enable `pcode_lift.lift_body(..., call_dispatch=True)` for these modules. Both `CALLIND` and recognized loaded indirect tail calls use `lift_dispatch`, generated from the same binding lists. Registered guest addresses select either a lifted body or a typed native import. An unregistered target aborts before dereferencing or calling it. Direct imports link through their `sub_<address>` adapter symbols. A plain `ptr` preserves data-pointer bits; it does **not** translate arbitrary function pointers. Native callbacks must explicitly use generated native export symbols, while lifted indirect calls use registered guest addresses. Do not mix the legacy unchecked indirect-call mode with this contract.

The initial headless integration surface in `scripts/native_bind_probe/host.c` uses scalar/pointer API types: class/method lookup, `il2cpp_runtime_invoke`'s pointer arguments/result, string/object accessors, and command-line/internal-call registration. IL2CPP static methods also carry their final `MethodInfo*` argument, which must be explicit in the signature. This is not a complete inventory of Unity icalls. By-value structs/unions, hidden aggregate-return pointers, variadic functions, `long double`, vectors wider than 128 bits and signatures exceeding 256 arguments are rejected by `AbiError` before C generation. Passing an aggregate **by pointer** remains an ordinary `ptr`; its pointed-to layout is the caller's responsibility. There is no verified by-value aggregate or variadic bridge yet: an integration needing either must add classification and execution tests, or a separately typed nonvariadic wrapper, rather than register a guessed scalar signature.

Run `uv run pytest -n 0 tests/test_lift_abi.py tests/test_native_call_probe.py` on arm64 macOS with Homebrew LLVM and Ghidra. Synthetic x86-64 fixtures cover both directions for every supported kind, integer/SSE overflow and vector stack alignment, direct nesting, indirect calls/tail calls, native callbacks into lifted code, guest callee-saved registers, return-address preservation and restored RSP.
